#2870. Spurs drop NBA Cup game to Trail Blazers despite Wembanyama’s big game

It felt like a playoff game in the Moda Center on the night before Thanksgiving. Maybe it was the drive to win an NBA Cup game, or maybe it was the Portland Trail Blazers wanting revenge on the San Antonio Spurs from last year's playoffs, and a thumping earlier this season. Either way, the Blazers were motivated, and the crowd was raucous. The Spurs kept pace for most of the game, but faltered late in a 116-111 loss.

It looked like Victor Wembanyama would will the Spurs to victory without De'Aaron Fox and Dylan Harper, who missed the game with injuries. Wembanyama had it going in the post, scoring 44 points on 17-for-30 shooting. Immediately, he took Blazers center Donovan Clingan into the low post, scoring over him or forcing him to foul, eventually leading to Clingan fouling out.

However, Wembanyama's effort wasn't enough to overcome Portland getting stellar performances from two of their stars. Damian Lillard looked like prime Dame, hitting three after three over the Spurs defenders. Lillard had 29 points and 6 assists while shooting 5-of-12 from three. He was outshined by his teammate, Deni Avdija, who hit the big bank three at the end of the game to seal the win for Portland. He had 33 points, 12 rebounds, and 7 assists on an efficient 11-of-21 shooting from the field.

San Antonio came into the fourth quarter seemingly taking control. A Devin Vassell fadeaway three-pointer to beat the third-quarter buzzer put the team up three points. After struggling to shoot the ball in the first half, the team got it going in the third quarter, shooting 6-of-10 from three-point range. With Portland out of challenges and its big man in foul trouble, the Spurs had several advantages to steal a win on the road.

Immediately, the Blazers went on a 12-2 run to take a 7-point lead early in the fourth quarter. They played with a lot of force on both ends. That was particularly clear when Sidy Cissoko drove right into Wembanyama and dunked over the top of him. The former Spur had 16 points, knocking down four of his ten three-pointers.

All game long, but especially down the stretch, the Blazers were able to get into the Spurs' ball-handlers' space and force mistakes. San Antonio turned the ball over 14 times, giving up 14 points off turnovers. That defensive pressure especially hurt the Spurs in the final two minutes of the game, when San Antonio turned the ball over two possessions in a row with the chance to tie the game.

Stephon Castle took on the majority of the ball-handling responsibilities and had an up-and-down game. Castle finished with 20 points, 9 assists, 6 rebounds, and 3 turnovers while shooting 6-of-18 from the field. He was stopped in the backcourt by Toumani Camara for an 8-second violation late in the game that gave the Blazers a shot of momentum heading into the end of the game.

Overall, it was hard for the Spurs to find an offensive rhythm outside of forcing the ball to Wembanyama in the high or low post. San Antonio shot just 43% from the field and 28% from three.

With the loss, the Spurs fall to 2-1 in the Western Conference Group C. They are in a four-way tie for first with Portland, the Golden State Warriors, and the Los Angeles Lakers. The Spurs play the Lakers, and the Blazers play the Warriors on Friday. San Antonio needs to win that game, and Portland to lose, to win the group outright. San Antonio may have a shot at the wild card spot if the Blazers and Spurs win.

#2868. Eagles pull off miracle Thanksgiving rally as Cowboys stunningly collapse after disastrous decision

ARLINGTON, Texas — Brian Schottenheimer said he second-guesses plenty of his decisions, just not the one that will be dissected more than any other in his two seasons as coach of the Dallas Cowboys.

Jake Elliott kicked a 59-yard field goal with 2 seconds remaining, and the Philadelphia Eagles stunned the Cowboys by rallying for a 39-38 Thanksgiving victory on Thursday.

The Eagles (4-7) got a chance for the win when Schottenheimer decided to go for it with a two-point lead on fourth-and-1 from the Philadelphia 39-yard line with 55 seconds remaining. Dak Prescott was sacked at the 48 of the Eagles, who needed just 11 yards to give Elliott a shot at the long field goal with 7 seconds to go.

A punt could have run off about 10 seconds and pinned the Eagles deep in their end of the field with no timeouts.

The shocking loss is sure to turn up the pressure on Schottenheimer after the Cowboys (5-7) again failed to win consecutive games and made any path to the playoffs difficult at best.

"Absolutely not in that situation," Schottenheimer said when asked if he second-guessed the decision. "There's things that come up and you look at the film and you want to know, 'What do you think about that?' But that situation, absolutely not."

Philadelphia quarterback Joe Milton runs in for a touchdown during the Eagles 39-38 comeback win over the Cowboys on Nov. 26, 2026, in Arlington, Texas. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

The Cowboys have one of the worst defenses in the league, and let Joe Milton, a quarterback who couldn't win the backup job behind Prescott in training camp this year, throw for 331 yards in just his third career start. All three have been with the Eagles since he was signed off the Dallas practice squad last month.

Prescott is taking his responsibility, saying he didn't communicate a change at the line of scrimmage and then hesitated on a throw to CeeDee Lamb before Ty Robinson closed in for the sack.

"That's one I'll think about for a long time," Prescott said. "Still had a chance at the throw, just so the DB was playing CeeDee pretty physical. And then I couldn't get off of that, felt the pressure, and took the sack. And I can't do that. Most important part of the game."

Dallas' only winning streak in two seasons with Schottenheimer was a three-game run last November capped by a Thanksgiving win over Patrick Mahomes and Kansas City.

The Cowboys led by 21 points in the third quarter — the biggest blown lead in Thanksgiving history — and were still up 14 in the fourth before the Eagles started their improbable comeback by blocking a punt and returning it for a touchdown.

Milton had touchdown passes of 50 and 45 yards to DeVonta Smith and a 14-yard scoring run as the Eagles won when they were already missing quarterback Jalen Hurts and lost star running back Saquon Barkley (stinger) and cornerback Cooper DeJean (concussion) in the first quarter.

The defending NFC East champions have won back-to-back games since a seven-game losing streak.

"A lot of emotions, but mainly just so happy for this team," said Milton, who was Prescott's backup last year before losing the job to Sam Howell this preseason. "This team never let up. These guys on the sideline, they never dropped their head. Everybody just kept saying, 'We're good, we're good. We're gonna keep going.'"

Jake Elliott celebrates after hitting the game-winning, 59-yard field goal in the Eagles' 39-38 come-from-behind victory over the Cowboys on Nov. 26, 2026, in Arlington, Texas. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Prescott threw for two touchdowns and ran for a score while Javonte Williams accounted for three TDs in a game the Cowboys didn't trail until Elliott's winning kick.

The Cowboys were up 14 early in the fourth when Robinson blocked Bryan Anger's punt, and Kenny Moore II picked up the bouncing ball at the Dallas 3 for the easy score.

Prescott answered by running through and vaulting over Moore on a 14-yard run that restored the two-touchdown lead.

Dallas then had another special teams breakdown on Britain Covey's 55-yard kickoff return. Smith, who finished with a career-high 200 yards receiving, had a 23-yard catch after having gone to the sideline earlier with an apparent back injury when he landed hard on an incompletion. Milton ran 15 yards for a score on the next play.

Defensive tackle Ty Robinson (95) blocks Bryan Anger's punt during the fourth quarter of the Eagles' comeback road win over the Cowboys. AP Photo/Julio Cortez

The Eagles went for a 2-point conversion and didn't get it, so they had to try again after Makai Lemon's 24-yard TD catch pulled them within 38-36 with 2:10 remaining. After a holding penalty, Milton's pass was broken up at the goal line.

Before Lemon's score, Brandon Aubrey missed a 40-yard field goal that would have put Dallas up 11.

Milton was 21 of 31 with three touchdowns in his third career start. The 50-yarder to Smith marked the fifth consecutive week Dallas has given up a touchdown at least that long.

Prescott's TD passes were to KaVontae Turpin and Williams, who also had touchdown runs of 1 and 12 yards. Lamb had seven catches for 134 yards.

Dallas set an NFL record with a fourth home loss this season when scoring at least 30 points. The Cowboys are the first in league history to average at least 30 points through 12 games (31.3) and have a losing record.

The Cowboys started November by matching a franchise record with a blown fourth-quarter lead of 14 points in a 33-30 overtime loss to Arizona. They ended the month with a replay against the Eagles, who tied the biggest fourth-quarter comeback on Thanksgiving. Chicago beat Detroit 23-17 in overtime in 1980.

"I guess I said a couple of weeks ago after the Arizona game how stunning that one was," Prescott said. "But this definitely takes the cake."

#2867. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for applicability of truth in lending and consumer leasing rules for consumer credit and lease transactions

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau today announced the dollar thresholds used to determine whether certain consumer credit and lease transactions in 2027 are subject to certain protections under Regulation Z (Truth in Lending) and Regulation M (Consumer Leasing).

By law, the agencies are required to adjust the thresholds annually based on the annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W. Transactions at or below the thresholds are subject to the protections of the regulations.

Specifically, based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the CPI-W as of June 1, 2026, Regulation Z and Regulation M generally will apply to consumer credit transactions and consumer leases of $76,300 or less in 2027. However, private education loans and loans secured by real property, such as mortgages, are subject to Regulation Z regardless of the amount of the loan.

Federal Register notice: Consumer Leasing (Regulation M) (PDF)

Federal Register notice: Truth in Lending (Regulation Z) (PDF)

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

#2866. S&P warns Nvidia credit rating could be cut over 'rising' management and governance risk

Nvidia's credit rating could be cut by one notch over the next 90 days if the company continues to have "deficiencies" in its management and governance framework, S&P Global Ratings said in the wake of the Jensen Huang's ouster and reinstatement as Nvidia CEO.

"News regarding the CEO and questions regarding potential export control violations have exposed vulnerabilities in Nvidia's management and governance framework, especially in regard to its board structure, composition, and effectiveness," S&P said in a Wednesday note.

Nvidia currently holds an AA rating, the third-highest level.

S&P has placed the chipmaker on "CreditWatch with negative implications," though any potential downgrade would be limited to one notch, to AA-, unless investigations or disclosures lead the agency to conclude that Nvidia's reputation or future prospects have also been harmed.

A downgrade can pressure bond prices, push yields higher and raise borrowing costs. Nvidia priced $25 billion of investment-grade senior notes in June, with maturities from 2028 through 2056, shortly after S&P upgraded the company from AA- to AA.

Nvidia has about $32.5 billion of bonds in the U.S. high-grade corporate bond index, according to Bloomberg.

S&P's Wednesday note follows Moody's Ratings changing its outlook on Nvidia to stable from positive on Nov. 18, citing governance concerns, and leaving that outlook unchanged after Huang's return on Monday.

In its note, S&P cited the "long tenure" of certain board members as a reason their independence may be "compromised" and said Nvidia's management and governance risk was "rising."

The ratings agency added that it remained unaware of any remedial action taken by the board to address what could be "meaningful" management and governance deficiencies.

Chairman Harvey Jones and early investor Tench Coxe have served on Nvidia's board since 1993, while Brooke Seawell joined in 1997. Mark Stevens served from 1993 to 2006 and rejoined the board in 2008.

S&P also flagged questions around geopolitical risk management and internal controls arising from Nvidia's exposure to China.

"While Nvidia management has delivered significant returns for its stakeholders over decades, weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning have brought questions around the "key man" risk to the forefront," S&P's Andrew Chang and David Tsui said.

Chang and Tsui noted that Huang's resignation and subsequent reinstatement did not resolve questions about the board's oversight of executive conduct.

The agency also intends to meet with Nvidia's board and management to discuss plans to address those deficiencies, particularly around "board independence, effective management oversight and a credible leadership succession plan."

Huang returned less than two weeks after Jones said Nvidia's directors did not intend to reinstate him. The board later voted unanimously to bring Huang back, sending Nvidia shares up as much as 36% during Monday's session.

Nvidia shares are down about 12.5% since Monday's close.

#2865. De’Anthony Melton stuffs the box score in Warriors’ pre-Thanksgiving win

On the first Thanksgiving, the overconfident Plymouth Rock settlers had to lean on the Wampanoag tribe to survive their first harvest in the New World. There's scant historical evidence about that first Thanksgiving, but some scholars believe that the Pilgrim colony found itself at a loss after Miles Standish suffered an abdominal oblique strain, one that wouldn't be evaluated until the Friday following Thanksgiving, a holiday that wouldn't exist for another quarter-millenium.

On the Wednesday before Thanksgiving 2026, the Golden State Warriors got 61 points from their bench players and eight three-pointers from De'Anthony Melton as they beat the Sacramento Kings, 129-110. Thanksgiving may not be a holiday in Brazil, but Gui Santos still feasted Wednesday night, putting up 22 points in 24 minutes.

Draymond Green had 11 assists, Yaxel Lendeborg had 15 points and eight rebounds and Brandin Podziemski notched a +16 in 32 minutes, but the Warriors won their game by dominating the bench minutes. Granted, bringing Leaky Black and Precious Achiuwa to an NBA game is like bringing a knife to a gun fight, especially if the knife turned the ball over 18 times, but the bench dominated, 61-19, to get the Warriors their second straight win.

That's intended in no way to minimize Melton, who is now shooting 44.9% from three-point range this season. He went 8-for-12 from distance Wednesday against the Kings and added seven assists, which just goes to show that any time you get the chance to have surgeries on both thumbs in one summer, you pull the damn trigger.

It was a game when the Warriors played well against type, taking a large 1st-quarter lead instead of falling hopelessly behind after eight minutes, a hallmark of their recent road trip. But they were back at the Chase Center, where the Warriors had won seven straight games after choking the home opener against the Memphis Grizzlies. And as the poet says, there's no place like home for the holidays.

Melton shot 4-for-5 from behind the arc in the opening period as the Warriors raced out to a 25-point lead after 12 minutes. They went 9-for-13 as a team and 14-for-22 overall, with Brandin Podziemski and Gui Santos each shooting 3-for-3 with two triples in the quarter.

Brandon Williams wasted no time in his first game in 16 days, scoring 14 points in 13 minutes, while putting up 14 shots and delivering three assists. That's officially a usage rate of "Thank God," and should help Williams when he lobbies the Chase Center's operations staff to change the current "road runner" sound effect when he scores to a Ferrari engine revving. He's now played 150 NBA games yet he already thinks he's too good for the road runner, a bird that has faced off against Wile E. Coyote and survived over a hundred times. Show him some repect!

In the second quarter, Melton made two more threes and Yaxel Lendeborg scored eight points to keep the halftime lead at 19 points. Melton also allowed Williams to use his laundry facilities this week, likely giving his teammate the confidence to dominate the ball the way he has. Williams does have his own apartment now, and more than perhaps any other Warrior needs them to advance to the prize money stage of the NBA Cup.

On the Sacramento side, Domantas Sabonis scored 27 points while taking only nine shots, also picking up 10 rebounds. Unfortunately for Sabonis, it's not clear if playing well or badly will get him what he truly wants: The chance to play for a team who isn't the 2-16 Kings.

Rookie Darius Acuff, Jr. had a high-volume, low-efficiency game, scoring 22 points on 9-for-24 shooting and logging nine assists. It's been a Make-A-Wish type season for Acuff, where the 20-year-old has been able to live his NBA dream of shooting whenever he wants, only it's also like he wished on a monkey's paw, because he's on a team that has won only two games all season.

But Gui Santos stood out with his first game of 20+ points all season, punishing smaller, slower, or just plain lazier Kings defenders to shoot 8-for-11 and score 22 points.

The Warriors are back up to 10-8, stuffing themselves on victories before heading to Portland Friday, where they'll face a team likely woozy from eating vegan turkey and giving out land acknowledgements all day Thursday. Could it be a three-game win streak for the Warriors? Maybe it's enough that the team is all together on a holiday. And still alive on the NBA Cup, which could even let Williams finally own his own washer.

#2862. Wealthy Europeans look beyond stocks amid AI selloff. Here’s where they’re investing

Wealthy Europeans are looking to alternative asset classes to build exposure to artificial intelligence themes amid turbulence in public markets.

Goldman Sachs Alternatives' inaugural survey of high-net-worth and ultra-high-net-worth investors found 47% of European respondents saw alternatives as the best way to gain exposure to AI and future tech growth, compared to just 24% of U.S. investors.

The survey found a significant gap between the U.S. and Europe in terms of investor knowledge of alternatives, which include assets like private equity, property and infrastructure. Goldman said 82% of European respondents are familiar with the alternative investments market — compared to 50% of U.S. investors.

Goldman polled 1,000 investors with at least 1 million in investable assets in their local currency across the U.K., France, Germany, Italy and Switzerland. The survey was carried out between June 29 and Aug. 18 this year.

The report on the findings, published Wednesday, did not specify the proportion of respondents who already have a stake in some kind of alternative investment — but wealthy Europeans appeared particularly bullish on the space.

Private equity made up the largest proportion of current alternatives holdings. Among wealthy Europeans with alternatives exposure, 62% said they invested in private equity. The second most commonly held asset class was private real estate, which was backed by 51% of European alternatives investors. Hedge funds were held by 37% of European alternatives owners, and private infrastructure was held by 30%.

One in four respondents who did not already own an alternative investment said they were likely to invest in the market in the coming 12 months. Among those already in the space, 26% said they were likely to increase their allocations over the next year, while just 5% said they planned to decrease their exposure.

Of the European investors who already owned alternatives, 54% said their assets had performed as expected, while 44% said their assets had exceeded their expectations to the upside. Only 2% said their alternative investments had performed worse than expected.

"Recent reforms gave individual investors unprecedented access to alternative asset classes like private equity, venture capital, and private credit," Kyle Kniffen, global head of alternatives for third-party wealth at Goldman Sachs Asset Management, said in Wednesday's report.

He added that wealthy investors "look ready to jump at the opportunity."

"Our survey shows satisfaction among current alts owners is high and adoption looks set to increase," Kniffen said.

Alternative investments have increasingly become a staple of high-net-worth individuals' investment portfolios.

Over the past year, JPMorgan Asset Management's private wealth alternative assets in the Asia-Pacific region have jumped 40%, while BNY Wealth says alternative investments now account for 60% of single-family office portfolios — recently surpassing traditional assets for the first time.

But investing in alternatives carries risks. Private equity, for example, can lock up capital for long periods, and although the number of exits ticked slightly higher last year, the value of those deals fell.

Concerns about private credit, meanwhile, have dominated headlines, with JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon warning on Oct. 14, 2025, that "cockroaches" were likely to be lurking in private markets.

— CNBC's Lee Ying Shan contributed to this article.

#2860. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

Pacome Dadiet, who scored seven points off the bench, goes up for a layup during the Knicks' 121-102 win over the Cavaliers on Nov. 25, 2026, at Madison Square Garden. Vincent Carchietta-Imagn Images

Lead assistant Chris Jent returned to the sideline Wednesday after missing nearly a month for personal reasons.

"I would be like, 'Yo, CJ, I need you to come with me. We need to work.' And he was kind enough to join me. So I give a lot of credit to where my shot is today from where it was to Chris Jent."

#2856. ‘Ready for any storm’: UAE minister optimistic on economy as IMF lifts forecast

The UAE is "very optimistic" about achieving 7%-8% growth next year, the country's economy and tourism minister Abdulla bin Touq Al Marri told CNBC after the latest data from the International Monetary Fund showed the country would avoid a recession in 2026.

The IMF last week revised the UAE's real GDP forecast to 0.2% growth in 2026 from a 3.9% contraction predicted a month ago. "The UAE is coming back, and that adjustment within one month shows that there is a huge potential aspect when it comes towards the end of the year," the minister told CNBC's Dan Murphy.

He said the tourism industry was already picking up with occupancy rates returning to "almost near normal" and international carriers including British Airways and Air France rerouting flights to the UAE. The minister expects economic growth to be higher than 0.2% this year.

The Iran war has disrupted economic activity in the UAE, with missile and drone attacks from Tehran and maritime disruptions weighing on energy trade, tourism, aviation and private-sector confidence.

Earlier this month, U.S. President Donald Trump said that renewed attacks on Iran were "highly unlikely" if negotiations produce a "very strong agreement." However, on Monday, Trump threatened that Tehran could face fresh U.S. strikes next year if the two countries failed to strike a deal, as per Fox News.

"We can we cannot promise the world for any storms that will land on our shore, but we can always promise that the UAE's economy is ready for any storm," Al Marri said.

He added that next year the UAE's economy will have a "different" model than the one before the conflict and investments will be focused on building factories and "things that we really need in times of crisis."

The World Bank in October said it expects the UAE's economy to recover next year, driven by rising oil production and exports and a rebound in tourism, trade, transportation, financial services, and construction.

It warned that the improvement in economic activity in the UAE largely depends on the duration and intensity of the Iran war, with a faster de-escalation likely to accelerate the recovery in services exports and private-sector activity.

#2855. Cowboys turn into turkeys during epic Thanksgiving Day meltdown | Opinion

ARLINGTON, TX – Give the people what they want, Dallas Cowboys. Or not.

Points. More points. Drama. And a holiday meltdown for the ages.

Hail to the depleted Philadelphia Eagles, capping a wild rally from a 21-point deficit with a 59-yard field goal from Jake Elliott with two seconds left that lifted them to a 39-38 victory – the largest Thanksgiving comeback in NFL history.

Joe Milton, the Eagles' fill-in quarterback signed off the Cowboys' practice squad in mid-October, got some serious revenge on the team that didn't keep him again as Dak Prescott's backup. Milton passed for a career-high 331 yards and three TDs. And boy did he live up to his "Bazooka Joe" moniker, using that cannon arm to connect with DeVonta Smith for a 50-yard touchdown in the first half and a 45-yard scoring strike in the second half.

Yet Milton's big day was only a part of the stain and shame left with the Cowboys.

They blew it again. Wasn't this the team that collapsed in crunch time in Week 8 against Arizona?

Indeed. Only this might have been even worse.

"Pretty stunning," said Cowboys quarterback Dak Prescott, a man who has way too much practice in trying to explain these embarrassing losses. "I guess I said it a couple weeks ago after the Arizona game, how stunning that was. This definitely takes the cake."

And the turkey, stuffing and sweet potato pies, too.

"Numbing is the word," Cowboys owner Jerry Jones offered.

He, too, has seen this season of promise for the Cowboys dissipate into disaster. Again. They are 5-7 and with road games against the Seahawks and Rams up next on the docket, this does not have the look of getting better. Yet, when Jones went on to hype the skill level of his coaching staff as he held court during his typical postgame briefing outside of the locker room, it was like the spin cycle repeating itself.

Which brings us to Brian Schottenheimer, in his second season as just the second coach in franchise history with a losing record (12-16-1), and a man losing, too, is trying to explain it.

"Every situation is different, each game is different," Schottenheimer said.

Maybe so, but there are common denominators. They lost to the Cardinals when Gardner Minshew came off the bench in the fourth quarter to throw his only three touchdowns of the season. They were upset by the Bucs, starting undrafted rookie quarterback Jalon Daniels on a night that started with the defense getting pushed around on a game-opening, 15-play touchdown drive. They lost in Week 3 in Rio with bone-headed blunders in crunch time, as the Ravens scored 10 points in the final 90 seconds.

Different situations, yet early or late in games, Dallas finds ways to lose.

Week 3: Indianapolis Colts kicker Spencer Shrader (3) celebrates with long snapper Luke Rhodes (46) after Shrader kicked a 58-yard field goal against the Houston Texans at Lucas Oil Stadium. The Colts debuted their "Anvil Strike" uniform - featuring an unfamiliar gray uniform and glossy, metallic blue helmet with the familiar horseshoe logo in white. The Colts won, 19-17, for their first win of the season.

Week 3: Buffalo Bills quarterback Josh Allen (17) hands off to running back James Cook III against the Los Angeles Chargers at New Highmark Stadium. For the game, the Bills debuted their "Nickel City" alternate uniforms - the most striking element being the blue "Charge Helmet," adorned simply with an oversized cutout of the team's familiar logo. The Bills won the game, 24-16.

Week 2: Los Angeles Rams quarterback Matthew Stafford (9) hands the ball to running back Kyren Williams (23) during a 28-6 win on "Monday Night Football" against the New York Giants at SoFi Stadium. The Rams debuted their new "Classic Sol" uniforms, an homage to the team's look during its 1951 championship season.

Week 2: Arizona Cardinals quarterback Jacoby Brissett (7) hands off to running back Jeremiyah Love against the Seattle Seahawks at State Farm Stadium. The Cardinals wore their desert-themed "Rivalries" uniforms in the 31-7 loss to the Seahawks. Arizona's "Rivalries" look was one of the best to debut in 2025.

Week 2: The Baltimore Ravens' Trey Hendrickson (91) reacts after a sack against the New Orleans Saints at M&T Bank Stadium. The Ravens wore their "White Noise" uniforms, just one of the team's new uniform combinations that will debut during the 2026 season. The Ravens lost the game, 24-17.

The Colts debuted their "Anvil Strike" uniform - featuring an unfamiliar gray uniform and glossy, metallic blue helmet with the familiar horseshoe logo in white. The Colts won, 19-17, for their first win of the season.

Week 3: Buffalo Bills quarterback Josh Allen (17) hands off to running back James Cook III against the Los Angeles Chargers at New Highmark Stadium. For the game, the Bills debuted their "Nickel City" alternate uniforms - the most striking element being the blue "Charge Helmet," adorned simply with an oversized cutout of the team's familiar logo. The Bills won the game, 24-16.

The Rams debuted their new "Classic Sol" uniforms, an homage to the team's look during its 1951 championship season.

The Cardinals wore their desert-themed "Rivalries" uniforms in the 31-7 loss to the Seahawks. Arizona's "Rivalries" look was one of the best to debut in 2025.

Week 2: New York Jets wide receiver Garrett Wilson (5) celebrates after scoring a touchdown against the Green Bay Packers at MetLife Stadium. The Jets debuted their "White Out" uniforms for their home opener, which ended in a 20-17 overtime loss.

This time, it started with a bang. The Cowboys had five possessions in the first half and scored four touchdowns. This flowed with recent trend. A few days earlier, when the Tennessee Titans came to town, Dallas had eight drives and seven TDs. And the week before that, in losing a shootout against the mighty Niners, they scored touchdowns on four of the last five possessions. In Cowboys math, that's 15 TDs over 18 drives during the three-game homestand (until country music star Miranda Lambert's halftime concert), which was nothing less than a certified roll.

Then poof. For all the feel-good flow of a 28-10 halftime lead enabled by Javonte Williams' 3 TDs, Dallas gagged against an injury-depleted team that used Aaron Rodgers earlier in the week to snap a seven-game losing streak.

MORE: Catch rule, Aaron Rodgers, Bears stars among winners and losers from NFL's Thanksgiving trifecta

The Eagles, fighting on for weeks after losing Jalen Hurts to blood clots, had one setback after another on Thursday. Star cornerback Cooper DeJean suffered a concussion on the game's first snap. Saquon Barkley had just five carries before getting sidelined by a stinger. Tight end Dallas Goedert was knocked out with a shoulder injury.

And Philadelphia (4-7) was still game enough to lay it on Dallas. Besides a huge, career-high 200-yard game from Smith, who torched Dallas on 11 catches as Milton's go-to man, the Eagles got a blocked punt from Ty Robinson early in the fourth quarter that Kenny Moore scooped and returned 3 yards for a touchdown. And then a 55-yard kickoff return by Britain Covey set up a 15-yard TD run for Milton. Throw in a 40-yard missed field goal by usually reliable Cowboys kicker Brandon Aubrey and yeah, words like stunning and numbing surely apply.

Reality check: The Cowboys, with all that explosive offense, still have a defense capable of imploding, of giving up huge chunks of yards, of allowing quick scores, of allowing opponents to stay in the game.

Of, well, allowing a castoff come back to haunt you.

"You never know when your opportunity is going to come," Milton said. "You never know when, where, how. The only thing you can is prepare yourself."

And if you're a quarterback, pray that the opportunity comes against the Dallas defense.

Remember when the Cowboys stripped first-year defensive coordinator Christian Parker of his play-calling duties? Like two weeks ago. They gave the reins to recently hired consultant Mike Pettine. Well, look at where that got them.

Still, for all of that, the Cowboys, with that red-hot, star-studded offense that produced 416 yards, could have bagged a victory by gaining 1 more yard.

The Eagles would have never had a chance for Elliott's big kick if Dallas had converted on fourth-and-1 from the Philadelphia 39-yard line with 55 seconds remaining. One yard.

Prescott was sacked for a 9-yard loss as he looked for CeeDee Lamb. Then it was left for Dallas' defense to protect the slim lead. And you know where that has gotten them.

Schottenheimer said he went for it because he trusted his offense to get a yard. Of course, there was no reason to trust the defense. And as it turned out, he couldn't trust the offense, despite a 100-yard game from Williams, to pound it inside to move the chains. Prescott took a shotgun snap. Then was eaten alive.

"You can't put the game on one play," Prescott said.

Yet sometimes, all it takes is one more play. Prescott acknowledged that part of his frustration was that his unit scored just 10 points after halftime. No, the defense's lapses and the special teams blunders could not be pinned on the quarterback, who has been on a tear with an 11-2 TD-to-INT ratio during the homestand.

Someone asked Prescott, fairly and pointedly, whether he'd expect to win after putting up 38 points.

"I'm not going to be the guy that's going to put things on other people," he said. "And I shoulder this one because of that last play."

Rest assured, there's plenty of embarrassment and shame to go around for the Cowboys. And little hope that this will be the season for a different type of finish.

#2851. There’s something to like about the way the Suns lost in Houston

The Phoenix Suns went to Space City to battle the team whose logo resembles one of those silly little patches that go on astronaut suits, ultimately losing by 12. What that final score doesn't tell you is that with 4:45 remaining, the Suns were down three points. What killed them down the stretch is what killed them throughout the game, and it shouldn't come as a surprise to any of us. Rebounding was the culprit. Or, more specifically, their inability to rebound.

Phoenix was outrebounded 51-38 overall and 20-13 on the offensive glass, but it was the 27 second-chance points they surrendered that ultimately sunk them. When Alperen Sengun pulls down 11 rebounds, and seven of them are of the offensive variety, you know you've done something wrong.

But my takeaway from this game? The impressively physical brand of basketball the Suns played. They stood up to a Rockets team that clearly had revenge on its mind as they ran up and down that bright red clowny court. After all, Phoenix had already beaten Houston earlier this season. When the Rockets came out with a chip on their shoulder, the Suns rose to the challenge and battled them throughout.

The final result comes down to two things. Houston possesses a more talented roster, and they're bigger, badder, and better on the boards. The attitude with which the Suns played, however, was something I thoroughly enjoyed watching. That's who this team was a season ago, and perhaps they needed a team like Houston to push them around a little, to jostle them enough to draw a line in the sand and say, "We're done with this."

The hope is that they carry that attitude forward. Because if they can bring that level of intensity and fight night in and night out, nobody is going to want to play that version of the Phoenix Suns. We know that because we saw it last year.

The rebounding issues aren't going away, and there will be nights when Phoenix simply doesn't possess the personnel to overcome them. But physicality and effort are things this team can control regardless of the opponent. What we witnessed in Houston was a willingness to compete on somebody else's terms without being intimidated by them. The Suns lost the basketball game, but somewhere on that obnoxiously red court, they rediscovered a little bit of their swagger.

Jalen Green did Jalen Green things against the Pelicans, logging his third Baller of the season with his 25-point performance on Monday.

#2850. Norse Atlantic suspends all flights, warns it may file for bankruptcy after share price collapse

Norse Atlantic suspended all of its flights on Thursday and warned it may have to file for bankruptcy after its stock collapsed on Wednesday following its warning about its "critical" financial situation.

Oslo-listed shares of Norse closed 85% lower on Wednesday.

"The board of directors continues to consider options to secure short-term financing," Norse said in a Thursday statement. "If a short-term financing solution is not secured urgently, it is the board's intention to file for bankruptcy."

The airline added that "there can be no assurance that a viable solution will be found."

Checks by CNBC showed the airline was still selling tickets for flights on Friday and beyond as of 9 a.m. Oslo time on Thursday.

Warning signs first popped up when PK786 from London to Islamabad and PK710 from Manchester to Lahore were cancelled because of "an unexpected operational suspension," the Pakistani flag carrier said on X.

The airline had been set to operate two Pakistan International Airlines flights Wednesday under a wet-lease agreement, in which one airline provides the aircraft and crew to another. The agreement had only been signed in early October.

PIA said it would operate the routes using its own Boeing 777 aircraft from the next day.

Norse's own flight Z0 783 from London Gatwick to Orlando was also shown as canceled Wednesday on live air traffic website Flightradar24. London Gatwick's website listed the flight's status for Thursday as "enquire airline."

In a bid to stave off financial troubles, Norse cut 75 jobs in May as part of an effort to reduce costs by $50 million, after Eivind Roald replaced founder Bjorn Tore Larsen as CEO in November last year.

The Norwegian carrier said Wednesday that its situation depended on securing financing for short-term liquidity needs and that it was in discussions about a potential strategic takeover.

"Norse is also engaged in discussions with selected parties on short term financing, however, there can be no assurances that such discussions will result in a viable solution," the airline said.

The Norwegian carrier has come under strain due to soaring jet fuel costs since the onset of the Iran war.

India's largest airline, low-cost carrier IndiGo, entered an agreement with Norse Atlantic in early 2025 to lease six 787-9 aircraft as it launched flights to Europe and Britain ahead of the arrival of its own widebody planes.

The agreement concluded at the end of October this year, with IndiGo citing airspace constraints, elevated fuel costs and currency pressures.

At its peak in 2024, Norse Atlantic had 4,822 scheduled flights. That number dipped to 4,695 in 2025 and 2,565 in 2026, according to aviation analytics company Cirium. The airline had 122 flights scheduled for November this year.

Norse Atlantic CEO Roald told CNBC in May that he was not "overly concerned" about access to fuel and said the airline had no indication at the time that the crisis would have an immediate impact. The airline said in October that it was continuing to operate at reduced capacity because of "prolonged high fuel prices."

Correction: This story was updated to reflect that Norse shares closed 85% lower on Wednesday.

#2845. ‘Your booth is waiting’: Denny’s welcomes Jensen Huang back as Nvidia CEO

Jensen Huang is back at the helm of Nvidia — and Denny's has a booth waiting for him.

The American diner chain welcomed the billionaire's return as Nvidia CEO by revealing that it had reinstated a commemorative plaque marking the birthplace of the artificial intelligence chip giant after learning of its removal on Nov. 13.

"Some things you just don't take off the menu. Jensen Huang is one of them," Denny's said in a post on X.

The plaque marks the booth at a Denny's restaurant in Silicon Valley, California, where Huang and his co-founders discussed the idea for Nvidia, which was founded in 1993.

The restaurant chain said it learned on Nov. 13, a day after Huang's resignation, that the plaque had been removed.

"We put the plaque right back up where it belongs," Denny's said, without specifying who had removed it or why.

Huang's ties to the diner chain, however, stretch back well before Nvidia's founding. He worked at Denny's as a teenager, washing dishes and serving customers before going on to build one of the world's most valuable companies.

The restaurant chain also disclosed that it had contacted Nvidia's communications team on Nov. 10 to express its pride in their shared history.

Huang's return came less than two weeks after his sudden resignation rattled investors and raised questions about corporate governance and succession planning at the chipmaker.

While his return has put those issues in the spotlight, Denny's offered a simpler message to its former employee.

"Jensen, your booth is waiting and the coffee's on. We're always open, 24/7," the restaurant chain said.

#2844. Nick Sirianni is pulled away from fans after comeback win

Good or bad, right or wrong, Eagles coach Nick Sirianni has had from time to time the demeanor of a fan who won a contest to be the head coach for a day.

On Thursday in Dallas, he displayed those qualities after a stunning come-from-way-behind win over the Cowboys.

While celebrating the 39-38 win (they trailed 31-10, late in the third quarter), Sirianni approached fans in field-level suites. He eventually had to be pulled away by Dom DiSandro.

It's unclear whether he was interacting with Cowboys fans or Eagles fans. He appeared to be making an aggressive movement toward someone before DiSandro intervened.

The Eagles contend that Sirianni was celebrating with Eagles fans, and that Sirianni was pulled away so that he could shake the hand of Cowboys coach Brian Schottenheimer after the game.

There was never any contact (at least none that the cameras captured). Still, it's a bad look — and it's definitely on brand.

While Sirianni has yet to cross the line, it seems like he will, one of these days.

Regardless, the Eagles have now won two in a row after losing seven in a row. They're 4-7 and on the fringes of contention in the NFC East.

#2842. Grading the Mavericks: Dallas’ accountability helped prevent a serious fall

The Mavericks went 4-2 over the last two weeks and sit at 11-7, in eighth place in the West. They lost back-to-back road games to Cleveland (127-117) and Boston (131-94), but rebounded with four straight wins against Golden State (116-94), the Clippers (110-100), Utah (116-114), and Denver (120-101). Cooper Flagg led the team in scoring with 23.7 points per game.

The Mavericks did not start the last two weeks well. Losing back-to-back road games in embarrassing fashion, then giving a suboptimal performance against a really hurt Golden State team was pretty dire, despite the large margin of victory. The three games since have been pretty convincing; the Mavericks are a good team somewhere in the realm of "better than bad, not elite, but can win on any night".

We have seen the emergence, or more accurately, the rebirth of Caleb Martin in the last six games. He is scoring over 10 points per game, shooting 56.5 percent from three, and grabbing 2.3 steals per game over that stretch. Martin is the true definition of a glue guy and has held the team together in their four-game win streak.

The win against Denver was incredible. It was an utterly dominant defensive performance. It is the type of win you have to build off of and refuse to let the train slow down. It was also Dallas' third NBA Cup victory, meaning if they beat Phoenix on Friday, they will win their group.

Dallas' two losses that applied to this round's grades were part of a three-game skid that lacked effort in every sense of the word. Losing to a Cleveland team that sat all of their starters after travel issues was bad, but going into Boston the next day and losing the game in roughly six minutes (after Dallas went down 24-4) was a true wake-up call. Cooper Flagg said after the loss: "I don't think we competed at a high enough level, you know, throughout the three games".

Every viewer noticed that, but having a guy in the fire acknowledge their shortcomings showed they could get through the adversity. And they did, to the tune of four straight wins. Even after the third victory in Utah, where they squandered a 17-point lead in three-and-a-half minutes, Kyrie Irving held the team, including the coaches, after practice to run in preparation for their game in Denver's altitude. It paid off with a 19-point road victory.

That is the sign of a good team. Being able to have honest conversations about your play and notice when things need to change is a key component of success. The Mavericks clearly have that. There will be more adversity and plenty more losses to come this season. If the Mavericks can lean on this framework, they will be solid, no matter what happens.

The Mavericks already have a weird roster, but the player that sticks out the most is Naji Marshall. He is not a good enough defender to justify the offensive dominance, and he turns the ball over far too much to be handling the ball at the rate he does. Part of his 20 percent usage rate is the fact that the roster is lacking ball handlers, but some of it, and perhaps the most frustrating part, is that he simply chooses to play hero ball far too often.

It's not clear when you look at his 10.9 points in 24.1 minutes per game. And sometimes he does play well enough to matter. But it's the choice to keep a dribble handoff, or the pass that he forces in transition, or the insistence on taking floaters and shrinking the floor that really hurts the team. The extension Dallas signed him to already feels cumbersome, and I expect the best version of this team to not have him on the floor. Whether that is because he is benched or traded away, I am not sure.

Maybe we have been spoiled by the likes of Luka Doncic and Cooper Flagg, or it's the guys picked behind him playing well, but Johnson Jr., the ninth overall pick in this past draft, has felt overwhelmingly underwhelming. His athleticism does not jump off the screen a lot of the time, his offensive awareness is sketchy, and he fouls a bit too much to stay in the game consistently. That being said, he has shown flashes of being a really good glue piece to a great defensive team.

One of these came against Dallas' statement win in Denver, where he scored nine points and had a team-high 10 rebounds. He played excellent defense, had a monster dunk, moved his feet incredibly well, and impacted winning on all fronts. It is this type of game that makes you wonder why he can't do this all the time. Some of it may be physical, with the shin splints still affecting him. A lot of it is probably mental. But either way, although the consistency isn't there yet, you can see the vision finally.

#2837. NFL news, live updates: Aaron Rodgers clears waivers after release following seething rant against Steelers coach Mike McCarthy; Eagles, Joe Milton III upset Cowboys on Thanksgiving

Week 12 of the NFL season kicked off with a rare Wednesday matchup between the Green Bay Packers and Los Angeles Rams, prepping us for a full Thanksgiving feast of football the following day, headlined by an MVP showcase between the Buffalo Bills and Kansas City Chiefs on Thursday night.

Ahead of that matchup, the Steelers made headlines by reportedly benching veteran quarterback and former MVP Aaron Rodgers in favor of backup Mason Rudolph for Week 12 after recent struggles on offense.

This storyline then reached a fever pitch on Wednesday when Rodgers appeared on "The Pat McAfee Show" and went scorched-earth on a rant about head coach Mike McCarthy. Rodgers accused McCarthy of repeatedly falling asleep in meetings and ending a team breakdown with "1-2-3 Let's go Packers."

Shortly after his appearance on the show, the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette's Gerry Dulac reported that Rodgers was being released. The quarterback had said on the show that he was "not going back to Pittsburgh." He was officially waived on Wednesday afternoon's transaction wire.

We're watching plenty of injuries ahead of the action, including Las Vegas Raiders All-Pro tight end Brock Bowers, who missed Week 11 with an ankle injury, though he was reportedly pushing to play in that matchup. The Kansas City Chiefs have already ruled out running back Kenneth Walker III for his third consecutive game with a groin injury, giving rookie Emmett Johnson the nod for a Thanksgiving contest with the Buffalo Bills.

Follow along with Yahoo Sports for the latest NFL news, injury updates and more coming out of Week 11.

Veteran quarterback Aaron Rodgers passed through waivers Thursday and was not claimed. He is now officially a free agent after going unclaimed on waivers and is free to sign with any team.

Rodgers was released by the Pittsburgh Steelers on Wednesday and was subject to waivers. It is unclear which teams may be interested given how he conducted himself this week.

News broke after 11 p.m. ET Tuesday night that Rodgers would not only be benched for the team's Black Friday game against the Denver Broncos, but would also be inactive.

Rodgers responded by joining "The Pat McAfee Show" on Wednesday afternoon and going scorched-earth on head coach Mike McCarthy for about 35 minutes. The quarterback went off on McCarthy, accusing him of sleeping in meetings, not watching film, and saying, "1-2-3 Packers!" when breaking down a halftime huddle with players.

While the writing was on the wall that Rodgers could be headed for a release, his interview with McAfee certainly sealed it. During his appearance, the quarterback even said he was not going to return to Pittsburgh.

The soon-to-be 43-year-old is now a free agent. Rodgers could land with a team that has quarterback injury problems or is right on the playoff bubble, but his conduct on Wednesday reportedly means that his options might be even more limited.

Rodgers may have played the final game of his illustrious career, but his tenure with the Steelers will almost assuredly be remembered for his rant on Wednesday.

49ers quarterback Brock Purdy did not practice on Thursday. It was his second straight day without practicing as he deals with an ankle injury. If he can't play on Sunday, it would be backup quarterback Mac Jones taking over against the Seahawks.

Veteran wide receiver Mike Evans was upgraded to a limited practice participant after not practicing on Wednesday.

The rookie quarterback continues to deal with a back injury and missed his second consecutive practice of the week.

If Beck misses Sunday's game, Josh Johnson is projected to be under center against the Commanders. Arizona did bring back Kedon Slovis this week after he was waived by the Ravens, but there hasn't been any indication that head coach Mike LaFleur is leaning toward starting Slovis after claiming him this week.

Chargers quarterback Justin Herbert remains in the league's concussion protocol and missed practice on Thursday.

If he doesn't practice on Friday, he will likely miss L.A.'s game on Sunday against the Patriots in which backup quarterback DJ Uiagalelei will likely be under center.

The Jaguars' offense had some concerning entries on the injury report on Thursday.

Wide receiver Parker Washington was limited due to a shoulder issue after not being on the injury report on Wednesday.

Starting running back Bhayshul Tuten missed his second consecutive day of practice due to an ankle and foot injury.

Jacksonville will have one more day of practice on Friday before statuses for Sunday's game against Tennessee are due.

The Steelers have had a tumultuous week, but got at least a spot of good news on Thanksgiving as veteran wide receiver Michael Pittman Jr. was activated off of IR.

Pittman is expected to play for Pittsburgh against the Broncos on Friday afternoon.

The veteran wideout was acquired from the Colts ahead of the regular season and promptly agreed to a 3-year, $59 million extension.

Pittman has played in only three games this season and has been on IR since October 10 due to a left foot injury. He heads into this week's game with 89 yards on seven receptions and is still looking for his first touchdown as a Steeler.

It's a real shame the sports world learned about Mike McCarthy's ice cream sandwich assistant after Halloween. Given the absurdity of that revelation, the Pittsburgh Steelers coach's ice cream pal would have been a popular costume among sports fans looking to have fun with the moment.

Thankfully, McCarthy's ice cream sandwich assistant is also perfectly in theme for the next big holiday. Thanksgiving is here in the United States, and, as you might have guessed, McCarthy's ice cream assistant is getting a little extra attention as fans and sports stars get ready to sit down for a day of gluttonous behavior.

Houston Rockets star Kevin Durant was among the biggest names to reference McCarthy's ice cream sandwich assistant on Thanksgiving. Durant called the move a "great idea," and said he would be hiring an ice cream assistant of his own on the holiday.

If you somehow missed the whole McCarthy saga, well, here's a refresher. After learning he was being benched by the Steelers, quarterback Aaron Rodgers went on Pat McAfee's show and completely blasted his own head coach.

Rodgers said, frankly, a lot of wild things. He claimed McCarthy fell asleep in team meetings regularly and broke down the Steelers with a chant of "1-2-3 Packers" during their last game. Some of those claims have been confirmed by local media or McCarthy himself.

But the ice cream sandwich assistant has even shadier origins. As all the Rodgers madness was popping off, former Steelers DT Breiden Fehoko claimed McCarthy had an ice cream sandwich assistant, someone responsible for bringing the coach an ice cream sandwich on demand. Fehoko claimed he received that information from a Steelers player.

That report has not been confirmed by local reporters or McCarthy himself, though that's not going to stop sports fans from having a little fun.

1. New York Jets (Trade with Tennessee Titans) — Drew Mestemaker, QB, Oklahoma State

In this deal, the Jets keep the No. 5 overall pick and package the Nos. 4 and 15 picks and a 2028 second-rounder to move up and tab Mestemaker as their QB of the future. Mestemaker has solid size (6-foot-3, 215 pounds) with a good but not overwhelming arm. He plays in a friendly offense that he maximizes with his aggressiveness. He's a loose athlete with a knack for changing arm slots and the arc on his throws. He's had some inconsistent play over the last month or so as his aggression leads to too many dicey decisions. Yet he's the exact type of gunslinger who would be fun to pair with the Jets' assortment of talented young skill players.

2. Los Angeles Chargers — Jeremiah Smith, WR, Ohio State

Three-time Defensive Player of the Year Aaron Donald said what the Green Bay Packers and Los Angeles Rams might all be thinking: "I wish he would stay out one more week."

Donald was referring to Packers edge rusher Micah Parsons. A member of the Packers might also have applied the sentiment to Rams edge rusher Myles Garrett.

Instead, the NFL's first Thanksgiving Eve game will feature a matchup of two teams who spent big to upgrade their pass rush recently — before injuries sunk their hopes within months.

The Jaguars were without running back Bhayshul Tuten at Wednesday's practice as he deals with both a foot and ankle injury.

With the chaotic week, Jacksonville benefits from a normal schedule, not having to play the Titans until Sunday.

Tuten has played in all 10 games for Jacksonville this season. He has 642 yards and five touchdowns on 158 carries, and has added an additional 226 yards and three more scores on 21 receptions.

San Francisco 49ers quarterback Brock Purdy is dealing with an ankle injury, head coach Kyle Shanahan told reporters on Wednesday.

Shanahan specified that Purdy does not have a high-ankle injury, but the quarterback may not be able to play Sunday against the Seattle Seahawks.

If Purdy can't start the NFC West divisional game, backup quarterback Mac Jones is expected to take over.

San Francisco is 9-1 and leads both the division and the NFC. The 49ers had their bye week in Week 8 and have won three consecutive games.

Purdy is having one of the best seasons of his career. In just 10 games, he has already thrown more touchdowns (23) than in each of his last two seasons (20). Purdy has thrown for 2,624 yards and five interceptions, and has added 222 additional yards and two more scores on the ground.

While the Rams continue taking injury hits on offense, they are getting a reinforcement on defense.

Defensive end Kobie Turner has been activated ahead of Wednesday night's game against the Packers.

Turner had been on IR because of a groin injury and last played on October 18. He heads into tonight's game with just seven sacks on the year.

The Steelers have reportedly released veteran quarterback Aaron Rodgers, per Pittsburgh Post-Gazette's Gerry Dulac.

Rodgers' release was reportedly expedited after the quarterback went scorched-earth on former head coach Mike McCarthy on Wednesday's episode of "The Pat McAfee Show."

The NFL went out of its way to schedule five compelling matchups for Thanksgiving Eve, Thanksgiving and Black Friday.

The schedule comes out in May, and things can change dramatically over six months. Yet four of the five matchups have held up very well. That's not a bad percentage, even if the Eagles and Cowboys have let us down. Everyone will watch those two marquee franchises anyway.

There's a lot on the line in terms of the playoff race during this week. We rank the most important games, in terms of what's at stake.

Veteran wide receiver Brandon Aiyuk has signed with the Washington Commanders practice squad as a free agent, ESPN's Adam Schefter reported on Wednesday.

Aiyuk was released by the 49ers on Monday and passed through waivers on Tuesday, going unclaimed. Now, he reunites with his former teammate at Arizona State, quarterback Jayden Daniels.

The Commanders, fresh off a stunning upset over the Cincinnati Bengals in Week 11, have been in need of help at receiver following a season-ending injury to star wideout Terry McLaurin. The team sits at 4-5-1, just two games back from the New York Giants, who hold the lead in the NFC East.

This marks a fresh start for the 28-year-old, who signed a four-year, $120 million contract extension with the 49ers ahead of the 2024 season but played just seven games on the new deal. He tore his ACL and MCL on Oct. 20, 2024, ending his season.

The Chiefs are looking at another game without star running back Kenneth Walker III. Despite returning to practice Tuesday, Walker will reportedly not play against the Buffalo Bills on Thanksgiving, according to multiple reports.

It will mark the third straight game Walker will miss due to the issue.

Prior to the injury, Walker was well on his way toward posting his finest season in the NFL. Through eight games, the running back already rushed for 872 yards and seven touchdowns. He also hauled in 26 catches for 172 yards and four receiving scores.

There were four MVPs, 10 Pro Bowls, a Super Bowl championship, dozens of highlights that defied belief over 22 seasons, and it all seems to end with a quick statement from the team after most of Pittsburgh had already gone to sleep.

It's not like Rodgers has played great this season, which he said will be his last in the NFL. Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy was asked this week if Rodgers was still the team's starter, so it wasn't completely out of the blue that he got benched.

It was still abrupt, and with some unusual timing. There was no time to celebrate the end of a legendary career or even realize that the snaps he took Sunday might be his last.

It's just done, barring some other turn of events over the final few weeks of the season.

The Steelers made the decision late Tuesday night to bench quarterback Aaron Rodgers. On Wednesday morning, the team began dealing with the fallout from that decision.

Rodgers was reportedly not in the building early Wednesday when the Steelers held their morning team meeting at 8 a.m. ET, per ESPN's Brooke Pryor.

Additional reporting suggested Rodgers was excused from going to team facilities the day after it was announced he was benched.

At that meeting, Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy officially announced the quarterback change to the rest of the team. That decision was reportedly "not a popular" one within the locker room, per The Athletic.

While Rodgers struggled this season, he was still well-liked by his teammates, per Pryor.

The Pittsburgh Steelers announced Tuesday night that Mason Rudolph will start at quarterback on Friday against the Denver Broncos.

The Eagles listed star running back Saquon Barkley as a full practice participant on Tuesday.

The injury report did note that it was just a walkthrough, but yesterday, when Philly did not practice, the team listed Barkley as a limited practice participant due to a neck injury as well as an illness.

#2833. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2831. Switzerland’s biggest bank set for showdown with lawmakers as capital fight intensifies amid crisis planning

UBS's crisis planning has won qualified support from market regulators, which called its plan "executable" in principle. But Switzerland's biggest bank still faces a major fight over sweeping plans that would force it to hold more capital against its foreign subsidiaries.

FINMA, the Swiss markets regulator, said Wednesday that UBS's resolvability — or its ability to fail safely, without wreaking havoc on the economy — has improved since it integrated rival Credit Suisse, with progress made in implementing the emergency plan across group-wide resolution planning.

The preferred resolution strategy — involving plans to allow for recapitalization and restructuring through the use of loss-absorbing bail-in bonds — is "executable," FINMA said. The regulator formally approved UBS's recovery plan for the first time since its ​Credit Suisse integration.

How UBS would withstand a future crisis remains in sharp focus among Swiss authorities ever since the lender's emergency takeover of stricken rival Credit Suisse in 2023.

UBS is now the country's only global systemically important bank, and the Swiss government wants it to hold stronger capital cushions, specifically in relation to its foreign subsidiaries, which would help it withstand another banking collapse.

UBS has pushed back against the overhaul, saying the measures are disproportionate and would damage its international competitiveness, forcing it to hold an extra $16 billion in capital.

A parliamentary showdown now looms, with the National Council, Switzerland's lower house, scheduled to debate the proposal Dec. 16.

On Tuesday, the lower house's economics committee backed plans to force the bank to support its foreign subsidiaries with 90% high-quality core Common Equity Tier 1, or CET1, capital. The Council of States, the country's upper house, approved the 90% plan in September.

The Swiss government had earlier proposed a 100% threshold. UBS wants it to be no more than 50%.

UBS CEO Sergio Ermotti told CNBC in an interview Oct. 28 that 90% would not allow the company to be a "competitive player" long-term. But he would not be drawn on speculation that the measures could push the bank to relocate out of Switzerland.

In a statement, the bank said the economics committee's recommendation would herald further excessive tightening of Swiss capital requirements, "which are already among the most stringent globally."

FINMA said in its Nov. 25 update that the goal is to avoid government solvency support and emergency law.

"The aim is to enable the authorities, in the event of a crisis, to choose the option that offers the best prospects of success in safeguarding financial stability and ensuring the continuation of systemically important functions," the regulator said.

#2818. What is ‘texture-maxxing’? The food trend putting crunch on the menu

Texture-maxxing is the latest food trend taking social media by storm as consumers, driven by aesthetics and taste, look for a sensory experience.

British supermarket Waitrose's latest food and drink report highlighted "texture-maxxing" as one of the trends of the year. It describes the phenomenon as a multi-sensory indulgence that is "fuelled by texture-maxxing crunch and intense flavour hits," pushing shoppers to fill their trolleys with crunchy peanut butter, dumplings, and trifles.

"This year, the sound of food went mainstream," Waitrose's Senior Development Chef Will Torrent said in the Wednesday report. "Driven by social media's obsession with audible, multi-sensory cooking, we're creating high-contrast meals that combine shatter-crisp crunch with pillowy chew."

"The emphasis in social media content has flipped from narrative voiceovers to ASMR-inspired audio, where the evocative sounds of food are just as important as the visuals," he added.

Food trends on social media have ranged from the obvious to the absurd. Fibermaxxing and proteinmaxxing have taken off in the past few years. Meanwhile, food and beverages like Dubai chocolate, protein coffee, and ube were some of the big social media hits.

Nestlé's USA 2026 trends found that 75% of Gen Z and 80% of millennials sought out texture in snack cravings with the hashtag "crunchtok," amassing 1.5 billion views.

Waitrose noted that shoppers looking to maximize on texture bought its viral No.1 Alphonso Mango Dessert, which involves "cracking through the crisp shell" to access a "smooth mango mousse and passion fruit curd on a crunchy white chocolate base."

Waitrose's TikTok and Instagram videos of eating the dessert were watched by more than two million people, with a whopping 6,173 hours and 36 minutes of play time on TikTok. Its videos featuring crunchy toast also went viral.

The U.K. grocer's sales of jarred chili crunches, which include crispy rayu oils such as itsu Crispy Chilli Oil, saw a 13-fold jump. Meanwhile, crunchy peanut butter outsold smooth.

Dumplings also had a moment, with sales of chicken gyoza dumplings up 116% this year and searches on waitrose.com for gochujang and peanut butter udon noodles jumped 60%.

"We've seen an explosion in demand as shoppers treat dumplings not just as a quick weeknight bite, but as a total sensory experience," Waitrose's Executive Chef Stephen Parkins-Knight said.

"Driven by the rise of texture-maxxing, people want that ultimate contrast: delicate skins, juicy fillings and crispy skirts, topped with very chilli crunch. With innovation ranging from fusion fillings to rich dipping broths, dumplings have taken a delicious centre stage in 2026," he added.

Texture-maxxing also extended to desserts, as shoppers bought twice as many chocolate trifles compared to 2025, and sales of strawberry trifles increased 160%.

Tiramisu was also popular, with searches for waitrose.com's tiramisu recipe page increasing 155% year-on-year.

#2817. Robots could be doing household chores in just 1-2 years, Chinese EV firm says

BEIJING — The household robot could soon become a reality, Brian Gu, co-president of Xpeng, a Chinese electric car company which has entered the humanoids space, told CNBC.

"Maybe after successful deployment into commercial settings, maybe another one to two years, we can start thinking about deploying into home settings," Gu said. "Technology is moving at a much faster clip."

Xpeng revealed its latest general-purpose humanoid robot Iron on Wednesday. The company plans to start training the robot as a store assistant in its retail car locations in the first quarter next year, after starting mass production in January. Sales are set to begin in the second quarter, with deliveries expected later in the year.

Founded in 2014 as an electric car company, Xpeng beat Tesla to rolling out driver-assist in mainland China. The Chinese automaker has launched its own artificial intelligence chips. It's also expanded into personal flying vehicles and robotics. The robotics unit this summer raised $900 million at a $6.3 billion valuation in its first funding round, led by IDG Capital.

Investors, however, appear to be a tad skeptical about the developments at Xpeng, with the downturn in the Chinese auto market also weighing on the company. Xpeng's shares, listed in both New York and Hong Kong, have dropped more than 55% this year amid broad sector declines.

China's humanoid industry has faced more regulatory scrutiny on financing in recent months after Unitree's high-profile IPO and stock slump, as well as a rush of capital into the sector. That's all raised questions about commercialization and the efficacy of the core tech.

Gu said Xpeng's robotics stands out for its rare combination of the hardware, software and real-life use cases. He's expecting to add more investors after Iron's planned commercial launch next year.

"You can always make the head talk, you can always make the leg run, you can always make the hands to do certain tasks," Gu said. "But doing a task while you're walking and answering questions at the same time, that's the most challenging part. And then doing everything well, not falling down, not getting distracted. The model has to work in sync."

Most humanoid robot startups, especially in China, focus on a portion of the tech, such as hands or the artificial intelligence-powered brain. Some in the industry also see themselves as original equipment manufacturers, with another company tying the parts together for the finished robot.

Xpeng's co-founder and CEO He Xiaopeng, a software engineer, always thought that software could solve "everything,'" Gu said. "And after he got into the EV business he realized how hard it is to make hardware ... really do the things that you want to do safely and consistently."

On Wednesday, Xpeng announced its "Turing Physical AI Architecture" for using AI and real-time decision-making tech to control cars and humanoids.

Gu told CNBC that Xpeng's robotics development only needs the driver-assist chips, which the company already has, indicating limited impact from U.S. restrictions on advanced Nvidia chip sales to China.

"Right now we don't see any change to our supply chain, and also ability to have these chips."

In 2023, Xpeng's then-head of autonomous driving, Xinzhou Wu, left to lead Nvidia's automotive chip business.

As Xpeng ramps up its humanoid robotics business, Gu said the company's existing work in autonomous driving technology translates easily to a new product line — with far higher profit margins. "It's much easier to be profitable for the robotics business than the autos business."

Robotics will likely become a meaningful part of Xpeng's business in two to three years, Gu said, with profitability coming as soon as next year. That value will "dramatically" increase with robots entering households, he said.

But Gu noted that compared to commercial environments, homes require robots to act safely around elderly people, children and pets, in addition to reacting to emergency situations. That increases requirements for battery power and performance consistency, he said.
