#2815. Rick Pitino lost to Louisville in reunion, but found it a 'rewarding experience'

LAS VEGAS — If you expected an emotional roller coaster for Rick Pitino facing his former team, you'd be surprised it didn't exist.

"I didn't have any of it, to be perfectly honest with you," he said when asked about his emotions.

What he did get, though was a roller coaster of a game that ended in a scene he knew all about – but hadn't seen from the other side.

Louisville got the best of its former coach, blitzing the Johnnies from the start and fending off a late rally and controversial sequence with a buzzer-beating shot for a 93-90 win to punch its ticket to one of the Players Era 16 championship games.

Things were cordial ahead of tip. Pitino shared pleasantries with the Louisville fans, who cheered when he took the court and when his name was called in the pregame intros. When he came onto the court, one Cardinals fan told him "Good to see you Rick."

He embraced Cardinals coach Pat Kelsey, who said two days before the game "a big part of Louisville is Rick Pitino."

Hours before, Kelsey was able to have an actual conversation with Pitino for the first time, describing it as "pretty cool."

Once that ball tipped, the kindness ended. Louisville punched Pitino's team right in the mouth, and it looked like it would be ugly.

The Cardinals scored the first seven points of the game, momentum carrying over from the opening round win against Texas Tech, which was the first AP top five win in the Kelsey era.

St. John's had to deal with Tounde Yessafou getting in foul trouble just three minutes into the game. Without its top rebounder, it opened it up for Louisville to grasp control of the boards, and Seth Trimble notched a career-high 33 points as it looked like a Cardinal runaway with five minutes left to go.

Somehow, St. John's came back. It sprang to life in the final five, with the defense forcing mistakes to generate a 18-3 run that made it a two-point game with 19 seconds left.

Then controversy ensued. Louisville had the ball and no timeouts, and when guard Jackson Shelstad got caught in a trap, the referee blew the whistle and said a Cardinals timeout was called. Shelstad was confused and Kelsey was irate as it appeared no timeout was called from Shelstad or anyone on Louisville's side.

The frustration was all moot, as Adrian Wooley drilled a 3-pointer before the buzzer to cap off the win "that just lit up the city of Louisville," Kelsey said.

Despite the loss, Pitino was proud of his team for fighting back and nearly coming away with an improbable win.

And while he didn't have any emotions today, he admitted it might have been different if it was at the KFC Yum! Center in Louisville, which he is expected to return to on Jan. 2 when the program will honor longtime sports information director Kenny Klein, who died in June at age 66. Klein also joined Pitino at St. John's as a special advisor for the 2024-25 season.

Pitino also said it's "a terrific Louisville basketball team."

"Love the fans of Louisville. Proud of what Pat Kelsey's doing with the team, and I congratulate them. It was a great, great win for them, and we move on, try to get better," Pitino said.

Even as Kelsey soaked in the triumph of a wild victory, he was still trying to grasp the fact he "just coached against possibly the greatest college coach in the history of the game."

That's why he had to check himself in the midst of it all. When Woolery hit the game-winner Kelsey jumped onto the court, did a long fist pump and even kicked the scorer's table.

Then he realized he had to shake Pitino's hand.

"I'm like, 'Oh crap!' I got to get buttoned up here," Kelsey said. "Went down and shook his hand. It was cool.

"It was an honor to coach against him," he added.

So in the end, Louisville won the reunion, but both teams left somewhat satisfied. Pitino said there was plenty he liked from his team that impressed him. He believes these are the type of games that make you better for March, and it'll pay off in the end.

And it's all thanks to the place he will forever call home.

"What I saw tonight was much more rewarding than 40-point victories early in the year. This to me was a very rewarding experience, and it showed the fight that you have to have to be an NCAA (Tournament) team," he said. "So we'll rally from here."

#2814. Trump, NATO chief caution Russia as attacks on Ukraine’s energy infrastructure intensify

U.S. President Donald Trump and NATO chief Mark Rutte issued a joint statement on Thursday urging Russia not to take any actions, intentionally or otherwise, that would further expand the Ukraine war.

The statement comes as Ukraine's allies sound the alarm over Russia's growing threat to European security and just one week after Trump's historic trilateral meeting with Russian President Vladimir Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping in Asia.

Trump and Rutte described their meeting at the Mar-a-Lago club in Palm Beach, Florida on Wednesday as "very impactful," with Russia's nearly five-year-long war in Ukraine and the Iran war among the key topics of conversation.

"We also discussed the importance of Russia avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war, and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the war as rapidly as possible," Trump and Rutte said in their statement.

Moreover, the two discussed the importance of further increasing defense spending as well as investment in the defense industrial base in both the U.S. and NATO countries, according to the statement.

Trump also said in a post on Truth Social Thursday that he "briefed NATO on the tremendous progress" being made in growing defense production, noting that the focus will be on building Patriot air defense systems, THAAD systems and Tomahawk cruise missiles, as well as "many new Weapons, both Offensive and Defensive, that have not yet been viewed by the Public, or other Countries."

"There has never been a time when the United States has produced more Military Equipment or Munitions than now. Our 'cupboard' will soon be full and, when it is, our Allies and Partners throughout the World will be benefitting greatly," he said in the post.

Trump's meeting with Xi and Putin, in which he described the Russian president as his "friend," sparked alarm among European lawmakers about Washington's commitment to transatlantic security.

NATO's Rutte, a seasoned diplomat known as a consensus builder during his nearly 14 years as Dutch prime minister, has repeatedly sought to de-escalate tensions in the alliance and frequently used flattery to win over the U.S. president.

While some have questioned whether this approach has delivered any tangible benefits, Trump has previously described Rutte as a "great leader" and NATO's "biggest asset."

Russia has stepped up its attacks on Ukraine's energy infrastructure in recent weeks, with further attacks on the central Ukrainian city of Kryvyi Rih overnight disrupting power supplies and bringing municipal electric public transport to a standstill, according to Oleksandr Vilkul, head of Kryvyi ​Rih's defense council.

Ukrainian officials on Wednesday said Russian attacks on the country's power sector had damaged energy infrastructure and caused power outages in five regions as cold winter weather sets in.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz on Wednesday accused the Kremlin of "escalating" threats toward European security, citing actions in the Baltic region, Poland and on the Black Sea, and adding that "Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than Ukraine."

A spokesperson for Russia's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment on Thursday morning. The Kremlin has repeatedly denied seeking military confrontation with NATO countries.

#2811. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2810. 'He was off balance' - R Ashwin criticises Rishabh Pant for dropping Daryl Mitchell in IND v NZ 2nd Test

'He was off balance' - R Ashwin criticises Rishabh Pant for dropping Daryl Mitchell in IND v NZ 2nd Test originally appeared on Cricket News. Add Cricket News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Ex-India spinner R Ashwin has criticised Rishabh Pant for dropping Daryl Mitchell off Jasprit Bumrah.

Ashwin says Pant hadn't settled into a balanced position before Bumrah let go.

Mitchell was on 9 and went on to make 31 before Sundar dismissed him.

R Ashwin has criticised Rishabh Pant's wicketkeeping after the India gloveman spilled a chance to get rid of Daryl Mitchell on Day 1 of the second Test in Christchurch.

The chance arrived in the 56th over, when Jasprit Bumrah began with a fierce bouncer that rose right over Mitchell's shoulder. The batter got a glove on his hook shot, and Pant leapt high to his left, but he could only get one hand to the ball, which struck his glove between the thumb and finger.

The timing made the drop more costly for India. The visitors had squeezed New Zealand for 24 runs in 12 overs, and a wicket at that point could have tightened the grip even further. Mitchell stayed on 9 instead.

The missed catch also denied Bumrah his first wicket of the match. He finished the day with figures of 19-4-37-0 and remains without a wicket in this game.

MORE: 'Is he crying?': Rahane & Kartik slam India team selection as Siraj is dropped

Ashwin posted a frame from the delivery to make his point, and he began by trying to keep the debate away from personalities. He wanted people to look at the issue itself, which in his view was about fundamentals.

He wrote: "Before ppl come in making it about the person, it's about the issue and lack of basics."

In his reading, Pant was still moving and upright when Bumrah released the ball, instead of having settled into a low stance. That left him unable to launch properly when the edge came.

"Look at this frame where the keeper has not stepped in to a balanced position before the bowler has let go of the ball, hence he was off balance when he had to take off for the catch. These are things that are non negotiable at this level."

Mitchell wasn't able to build a big score, as he went on to make 31 off 74 balls, with four fours and no sixes, but he did put together a partnership with Tom Blundell.

Finally, the breakthrough came in the 65th over, when Washington Sundar got one to turn and bounce. Mitchell had gone back, expecting to work the ball around the corner, but the extra bit of movement off the surface did him in.

MORE: IND vs NZ 2nd Test session timings: Start times, lunch and tea breaks

The Mitchell chance was not the only blemish on Pant's day behind the stumps.

He had another awkward moment in the 70th over, when debutant Gurnoor Brar banged in a bouncer on off stump to Glenn Phillips. The batter swung hard at it, and Pant leapt in the air and appeared to get a glove on the ball, but he couldn't hold on, and it raced away to the boundary.

The score was officially recorded as four byes, although the replays suggested that Phillips may have got an edge or a glove on it, which would have made it a chance for the keeper.

However, Pant took two clean catches off Akash Deep, to remove New Zealand captain Tom Latham for 59 and Rachin Ravindra for a duck.

He came into the Test with 172 catches and 18 stumpings in 53 matches. He was also fresh from an innings of 128 off 153 balls in the drawn first Test in Wellington, so his form with the bat has been strong.

Ashwin's critique is spot on: a keeper who hasn't settled when the ball is released will always struggle against a bouncer that climbs, and Bumrah is the kind of bowler who tests that more than anyone.

India also have to live with the cost. Bumrah went wicketless again, and Mitchell, given a life, added 22 more runs and a useful partnership. In a game that could swing on small margins, those moments count.

The best outcome is a quick fix. Pant is an instinctive player who can improve his set-up with the right work, and Ashwin's post may even help him. The team should treat it as a useful reminder that the basics never stop mattering.

#2809. Former Steelers coach Mike Tomlin calls Aaron Rodgers drama a 'dumpster fire'

It may be Thanksgiving. It may be a day removed from Aaron Rodgers' stunning divorce from the Pittsburgh Steelers, who will host the Denver Broncos for the league's now-annual Black Friday game on Nov. 26.

But the four-time NFL MVP and, suddenly, NFL free agent remains at the forefront of the discourse around the league and even the country in the aftermath of his very pointed exposure of Steelers coach Mike McCarthy's alleged issues and why they drove Rodgers to the point of issuing ultimatums and later detailing just about everything that had been occurring in the Pittsburgh locker room in a remarkable break from NFL protocol − and Rodgers' long-held belief that matters that swirl about a football team's inner sanctum should remain private.

But after a little more than 24 hours of pundits, reporters, social media, Rodgers' former teammates and a handful of current ones weighing in on the situation, a man with a perspective as unique and, for the most part, as informed as any shared his thoughts regarding the shocking meltdown that's occurred in the Steel City.

Mike Tomlin, the Steelers' coach for 19 seasons from 2007 through 2025, and perhaps the primary reason Rodgers came to Pittsburgh a year ago, discussed what has happened to the Steelers at halftime of the Kansas City Chiefs-Buffalo Bills game in his current as an analyst for NBC.

Speaking remotely with NBC's NFL play-by-play man, Mike Tirico, Tomlin said he felt a "general sadness" watching the public disintegration of Rodgers' and McCarthy's relationship and the quarterback's abrupt exit from the team.

"Certainly I had a range of emotions watching those course of events yesterday," Tomlin said of Rodgers' interview on "The Pat McAfee Show."

"I think the prevailing one is just general sadness – sad that we might be looking at the end of a legendary career, the quarterback, Aaron Rodgers. And just on a macro level, whether it's organizationally or for the game of football at large, to see laundry aired out that way in a public forum – those are things that kinda run counterculture to professional football."

Tomlin said Rodgers' remarks about McCarthy "certainly did" shock him, "particularly because I know both men involved."

He also characterized Rodgers, despite the scorched earth route he took Wednesday, as a "beloved" teammate.

"I'm not overexaggerating that," Tomlin continued. "He worked at it, and people appreciated his openness to get to know and understand the Steeler culture, to be engaged with teammates."

He also touted Rodgers' patience and outreach to fellow Steelers, especially younger ones.

NATE DAVIS: For Aaron Rodgers, his 'truth' is always worth burning things down

As for McCarthy, Tirico asked Tomlin if his successor can effectively lead the Steelers in the wake of Rodgers' comments.

"It certainly created some challenges, and 'challenges' is probably an understatement if all those things are true," Tomlin said.

"But (McCarthy's) ability to overcome that or inability to overcome that, in my opinion, is based on relationships and the depth of those relationships and how those guys feel about him or how those guys buy into what he's selling.

"It makes you wonder the level of that challenge, but it will be relationship-based − his ability to overcome (this)."

Asked what it was like to see the scrutiny trained on his longtime employer, Tomlin said: "I'm upset about it. It's a dumpster fire. And I don't say that in a judgmental way − I've presided over a few dumpster fires in my 19 years (as coach). But it certainly is upsetting, and I'm sure Steeler Nation is upset."

While questioning McCarthy's dedication and efficacy at his job while speaking to McAfee, Rodgers took the opportunity to praise Tomlin anew. Both he and his former coach had long had mutual admiration before teaming up for the 2025 season. But Tomlin stepped down at the end of the campaign, which ended with a resounding defeat in the wild-card round to the Houston Texans in Pittsburgh. Per reports, Rodgers, who played quite poorly against the Texans, was in tears when Tomlin announced the decision to leave his post following the loss.

"Leader of men, he knew everybody's name, knew where they came from," Rodgers said of Tomlin on Wednesday, a description that also served to distinguish him from McCarthy.

#2807. Best trade destinations for Clippers veterans who are getting showcased

Best trade destinations for Clippers veterans who are getting showcased appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Los Angeles Clippers arguably have the bleakest outlook among all 30 NBA teams in light of the Kawhi Leonard cap circumvention saga that has left them empty-handed in terms of draft assets.

The Clippers already owed a few of their future firsts due to previous trades for Paul George and James Harden, both of whom are no longer with the team. But the NBA took away their first-round picks from 2029 to 2033 — including the one they got from the Indiana Pacers in the Ivica Zubac trade.

This Clippers team traded Leonard away in the offseason, and yet they cannot rebuild due to this horrible draft-pick situation they find themselves in. Now, they find themselves stuck with veteran pieces who fit better on contending teams, but those players won't nearly be impactful enough to warrant significant returns on the trade market.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

LA might just go ahead and remain as competitive as possible since they have no lottery balls to play for until 2034. But their veterans are likely to seek greener pastures anyway, and it might be best to get as much of a return for them in trades. And with the likes of Derrick Jones Jr., Rui Hachimura, Kris Dunn, Brook Lopez, and Max Strus being on the final year of their contracts, LA is further incentivized to deal them away.

ClutchPoints Clippers insider Tomer Azarly even mentioned that other teams have either "inquired" of their availability or are at least curious about what it would take to trade for them.

With that said, here are some of the best possible trade destinations for the aforementioned veterans as the Clippers ponder whether or not to hold a fire sale prior to the trade deadline in February.

Image Credit: Mark J. Rebilas-Imagn Images

On paper, Jones appears to be a perfect fit for the Heat, a team in need of more size and athleticism on the wing to put alongside Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo. With Andrew Wiggins being a shell of his former self to begin the 2026-27 season, Miami has had to resort to drastic measures to cover for the small forward position.

They've started Pelle Larsson at the three as of late and even relied on Myron Gardner to start when Larsson was out with an injury. And while Larsson can create for himself off the bounce and moves well without the basketball, Miami could use even more size and defensive stability on the wing — something Jones provides in spades.

Jones is even shooting over 40% from three this season on almost four attempts per contest, and it helps that he has experience playing under head coach Erik Spoelstra, spending three seasons under his tutelage.

The Lakers gambled this offseason by relying on low-cost options such as Matisse Thybulle and Ziaire Williams to fill their need for a 3-and-D wing to complement Luka Doncic and Austin Reaves. But in the playoffs, the Lakers may need even more defense on the wing to survive four playoff rounds.

Jones broke out and became a full-time starter in the NBA when he played alongside Doncic, so there is already existing synergy that should instill confidence in the Lakers that a trade for him would work out. The problem is that the Clippers and Lakers don't tend to trade with each other, being in-city rivals and all, complicating a potential pursuit of Jones.

As for the Nuggets, that team simply needs more depth in the worst possible way, and they will always have the contract of Zeke Nnaji as well as some other filler to dangle in a potential trade. But the Clippers, more likely than not, should be able to find a better offer for Jones elsewhere.

The Pistons have embraced their identity as a hard-nosed team that relies on its defense to win plenty of games, especially in the regular season. But in the playoffs, opposing defenses are relentless. They will dare the worst offensive threats on the other team to shoot, and most of the time, the margins are so thin that this proves to be the difference over the course of a seven-game series.

With the departure of Tobias Harris in free agency, the Pistons could use another big wing who can shoot the basketball and even create his own shot from the perimeter on occasion. Hachimura is shooting over 40% from deep yet again this year and he would feast on the open looks Cade Cunningham would deliver on a silver platter.

Detroit also has the the trade pieces to make a potential deal work (building a package around Ron Holland works, salary-wise), so expect them to be a major suitor for Hachimura if he ends up being available for trade.

Another team that's always in need of more scoring punch is the Magic. They rank 25th in the NBA in offensive rating, and they need more spacing, as per usual. Hachimura can provide just that.

As for the Pacers, it's always a good idea to bolster their scoring options on the wing to prepare for Tyrese Haliburton's eventual return from injury. The Pacers could package Jarace Walker, Ben Sheppard, and a few draft assets to acquire the 28-year-old Japanese international.

The Celtics may not want to rock the boat even further after already shaking up the roster this past offseason by trading away Jaylen Brown for a package built around Paul George. It makes sense for them to make some moves on the margins, which could involve a trade for Kris Dunn.

Dunn is a steals machine who fits in perfectly into the Celtics' ethos of winning the possession battle. He is also a tenacious point of attack defender for Boston to throw at quicker guards to spare Derrick White and George from taking on those matchups.

Boston's identity is predicated on defense and threes, and Dunn provides half of what they need. If he makes enough threes to survive in a playoff series, he could be an impactful enough addition for the team in green.

Meanwhile, the Nuggets are relying on Tyus Jones to provide backup minutes at the one in 2026, which is less than ideal. Jones is shooting 31.6% from the field thus far this season and has looked washed. Denver needs a backup ballhandler, and Dunn gives them that — all while improving their defense as well.

It's a telltale sign that the Heat need more help on the wing that they're brought up here as a potential destination for two former Miami players in Jones and Strus. If anything, Jones' skillset would be more helpful to Miami considering that he's a proven point-of-attack defender who can help address Miami's defensive fragility on the perimeter.

But Strus thrived in Spoelstra's system in the past, and Tim Hardaway Jr. has already fallen out of favor in the Heat rotation — opening up a potential spot for another sharpshooter like Strus to come in.

Strus is not a defensive liability like Hardaway can be, and considering his previous experience in Miami, one would think that he'll be able to contribute right away. Miami can trade away Nikola Jovic's contract to bring in Strus, as Jovic, despite making $16.2 million this season, is racking up DNPs anyway.

He might be just 23 years of age, but Miami is here to win now, and it might serve them best to get back a proven contributor instead of banking on Jovic's potential that may never be fully realized.

The Mavericks currently don't have a movement shooter like Strus in their rotation. It seems like any contending team would benefit from having a guy with that kind of skillset in the rotation, and perhaps getting someone like Zaccharie Risacher back as a reclamation project would appeal to the Clippers.

The Rockets may not be looking for another backup center considering that they already have Steven Adams and Clint Capela to rely on behind Alperen Sengun. But Lopez provides a different dimension as a floor-spacing big man who could help grease the wheels for Houston's offense in the playoffs.

Backup center has been somewhat of a weak spot for the Lakers to begin the 2026-27 season. Kevon Looney is more of a locker-room veteran than a rotation player at this point of his career, and it might be best for LA to reinforce that position prior to the trade deadline.

But again, the Lakers will find it difficult to negotiate a reasonable trade with the Clippers due to their rivalry. Moreover, the Lakers don't have much in the way of trade assets, and they may want to acquire a younger big man who can contribute beyond this season, such as Day'Ron Sharpe of the Brooklyn Nets.

Nonetheless, Lopez would thrive with the open shots he's going to get in this high-octane Lakers offense. Even at age 38, Lopez is averaging over 14 points while shooting over 43% from deep through the first 17 games of this season — and that's despite playing for a Clippers team that ranks 26th in offensive rating this season.

NBA rumors: Insider teases Nuggets making another run at Clippers trade target

#2806. 'All about giving': Why the Lakers' Ziaire Williams enjoys giving back

As the gathering of kids began, their eyes grew wide and several of them said "oooh" when they saw the tall, slender young man with the stylish dreads approach the microphone and introduce himself as Lakers forward Ziaire Williams.

Williams had come to Inglewood High to provide meals and fellowship with about 150 kids from the Boys & Girls Clubs of Metro Los Angeles, an event he has done for his entire six-year NBA career.

Having grown up in Lancaster and attended Sierra Canyon, where he was a high school teammate of Bronny James, Williams saw himself in those kids. He, too, went to the Boys & Girls Club while a youth. He remembers the influence it made on his life and how his parents, Ziaire Williams Sr. and Marquita Fields-Williams, felt at ease because their son was safe.

Williams, who held the event last week, believes it is important to be active in the community.

"Being a part of a Thanksgiving event like this means a lot to me," he told the kids. "I appreciate all of you guys coming out to support the event. This is a time of giving and Thanksgiving. It's just all about giving back and being appreciative for your loved ones and all the small things in life.

"So, I hope you guys can meet some new friends, eat some good food, and just appreciate the small things in life. That's what it's all about."

Williams then helped to serve the food, a smile on his face, "joy" in his heart to see the kids enjoying the moment and each other, all of them relishing the opportunity to meet a Laker in person.

"I've actually been doing this every year since I've been in the NBA," Williams said. "Growing up, I was in the Boys & Girls Club. My parents were always at work. The Boys & Girls Club, it was almost like my second home growing up. It's just a great safe place after school just to hang out, do your homework, meet some new friends and occasionally Christmas, Thanksgiving, hopefully have a good meal, maybe a pizza party or something.

"So, since I've been in the NBA, I always try to just find different Boys & Girls Clubs I can partner with for just the holidays and seasons of giving. Now, just do my part to give back, give them something to smile about and, you can't go wrong with a good meal."

Initially, some of the kids looked nervous and shy when they walked by Williams.

But he greeted them all, shook hands, took pictures and listened to their stories.

This was their day and Williams was happy to be a meaningful part of it.

"Oh man, there's no words like it, man. It's hard to describe. It just makes my heart feel warm," Williams said. "I feel like God gave me this amazing ability to play basketball, but he also has allowed me to use my talents to do events like this and give back.

"And, man, it's amazing. Every time I just get to hang out with kids and just see a smile on their face, it really just brings me back to when I was their age, and it's amazing, man. There's no words that can describe it."

Lakers forward Ziaire Williams dunks over Trail Blazers guard Scoot Henderson during a game earlier this month at Crypto.com Arena. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

By most accounts, the 6-foot-9 Williams, 25, is having his best season as a pro.

He's averaging 9.1 points per game and shooting career bests from the field (53.8%) and three-point range (42.1%).

"Man, I would just say I just stuck with it. Just the same consistent work," Williams said of his success. "Sometimes it doesn't always pay off right away, but if you just keep sticking with it, keep being disciplined, your daily habits, it's bound to change quick. As the late [rapper] Nipsey Hussle always says, just never quit.

"Just the marathon always continues, and that's a principle and a moral I live by, just continuing to always try to be the best version of myself every day. And I'm just grateful to be in this position. I'm on a great team, a winning team, guys who care about the game and guys who want to compete at the highest level. And it's been an honor to play with the Lakers so far, and we still got a lot more work to do, but it's been a great start for sure."

Whether starting or coming off the bench, Williams has delivered by being the quintessential three-and D player the Lakers expected.

Before the Lakers signed Williams to a one-year, $3-million contract during free agency over the summer, they saw something else in him that made them yearn to have him as a member of their team.

"He's a beautiful human," Lakers coach JJ Redick said. "I didn't know him. We set up a workout for him in Vegas and he worked out. Rave reviews. I met with him the next day for about 45 minutes at our hotel and I was like, 'I got to get this guy on my team. Regardless of the player, I got to get this person on my team.' We sat there for 45 minutes and he looks you in the eye and it's 'yes sir, no sir. I got you, sir.' He's awesome. He's an incredible human."

Lakers coach JJ Redick congratulates forward Ziaire Williams along the sideline as he comes off the court for a timeout. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

Both of Williams' parents were in the military and he was taught to be respectful of others.

So, what Redick saw and felt was real, because Williams was raised that way.

"Yeah, it was definitely strict, but I'm thankful for it," Williams said. "Just the discipline and just prioritizing certain things. It was never hard for me to do because I just grew up with that discipline always from Day 1. So, it was definitely hard. I got yelled at. I got my butt whipped a couple times. It was all for the greater good. My parents wanted me to just be the best young man I could possibly be and put myself in a better position than they were in."

He remembers going to LeBron James' basketball camp in San Diego and winning the MVP award. That "was the coolest thing ever" to meet James. He remembers watching NBA games on TV and thinking how they "seemed like superheroes."

Now Williams is playing against James and those players he saw on TV.

But when he does his basketball camps and his Thanksgiving events as he did last week, he doesn't want the kids to view him as anything more than a humble person.

"I don't really want them to necessarily look at me as a superhero, but more so as just a person who's always willing to give back," Williams said. "I tie my shoes just the same way they do, you know? Every morning I just wake up and I thank God and I just try to be the best version of myself.

"That's what I just hope these kids get out of it is just whatever you put your mind to is possible. You just have to really believe in it and work at your craft every day. And I work on my craft every day and now it's got me here with the Lakers."

#2805. Jerry Jones still confident about Cowboys HC Brian Schottenheimer, doesn't expect coaching change after Thanksgiving loss to Eagles

Thanksgiving wound up being a very bad night for Dallas Cowboys head coach Brian Schottenheimer, but it apparently won't cost him his job.

Immediately after the Cowboys' collapse against the Philadelphia Eagles on Thursday, team owner Jerry Jones told reporters he doesn't expect to make a coaching change any time soon. Instead, Jones suggested continuity would give Dallas its best chance in the coming weeks.

Via Nick Harris of the Fort Worth Star-Telegram:

"I wouldn't anticipate that, no, at this juncture. We can give ourselves a better chance to win these coming games and be competitive, in my mind, without coaching changes."

The Cowboys face the Seattle Seahawks in Week 13, then have a bye before facing the Los Angeles Rams on Dec. 20.

Jones went on to say he was still "confident" about Schottenheimer, but made clear that confidence was based on nothing the team showed Thursday:

"Nothing we've seen out here tonight, but you've got to look at everything. That's certainly not the thought on my mind at this particular time. We've got several more games to go and we've got to have our mind on that."

Thanksgiving really was that bad for the Cowboys, who led 28-10 at halftime and held a lead as large as 21 points near the end of the third quarter.

Brian Schottenheimer had a bad Thanksgiving.

The team was then hit with a cascading series of disasters. Former Cowboys backup Joe Milton led the Eagles to touchdowns on three of their next four drives, with the Eagles also returning a blocked punt for a touchdown. The Cowboys still had the advantage in the final minute, but decided to go for it on fourth down in Eagles territory and watched Dak Prescott get sacked.

Eagles kicker Jake Elliott proceeded to make a 59-yard field goal in the final seconds. A Prescott interception ended the game.

Both the overall collapse and late-game decision-making left nearly every finger in Dallas pointing at Schottenheimer, who has been facing the hot seat for some time. He said he wasn't worried about such speculation after the game, via Jon Machota of The Athletic:

"I don't worry about that. I worry about the guys in that locker room. That doesn't affect me."

Schottenheimer's hire was controversial when it was first announced, as he was hardly a popular offensive coordinator under previous head coach Mike McCarthy (who is dealing with issues of his own right now). Jones' decision to promote Schottenheimer demanded trust in his own team's operations, and the results have been a 12-16-1 record.

In more than three decades of owning the Cowboys, Jones has fired only a single head coach during the season: Wade Phillips in 2010. Jones has said he regrets that decision and that could very well be informing his thinking here, though that will hardly protect Schottenheimer in a month and a half.

#2804. Meet the boy with Formula One dreams

An 11-year-old boy is starting to turn heads in the world of motorsport as he consistently beats competitors with more experience and money.

William Robinson, from Somerset, has driven in 47 karting races, and brought home 41 trophies.

But if his dream of winning the Formula One world title is to come true, he will have to buck the trends of the financially-driven sport.

Simply practicing at a track can cost hundreds, if not thousands of pounds - limiting William to one session per month. However, his mother Rebecca said she believed he will prove that talent gets to F1, not just how deep your pockets are.

William said he has wanted to be a racing driver from the age of three - his family describe it as an obsession.

"We say he was born with cars on the brain, he would go to bed holding toy cars," said Rebecca.

"When he was five, we asked if he'd like to try football, swimming, or to play the guitar. He replied 'I just want to race, mummy'."

The 11-year-old has already won two UK Championship titles [Rebecca Robinson]

The family got in touch with a local karting track, and six years on William has won two UK Championship titles, and come runner-up in the World Championship.

He said racing takes him into a different world.

"When I'm driving, I don't think my brain is on earth, it's somewhere else," he said.

"It's my happy place, I feel at one with myself, like nothing bothers me. I just have a desire to win."

William is hoping to do just that in America next month, when he competes in the World Finals at Willow Springs Raceway, California.

Rebecca says a karting season with travel, food and accommodation costs the family £12,000 [Rebecca Robinson]

William's success so far has come despite having less track time than a lot of his peers.

Ideally, regular practice would involve drivers having their own kart, being able to hire a track and pay for an engineer.

The family said that can be done "cheaply" for a few hundred pounds a time, using old kit and old tyres.

But with better equipment, the cost can run in to thousands for every session - the kind of money the Robinsons do not have.

They have praised the FAT Karting League, a competition set up by former F1 engineer Rob Smedley, which was designed to break down financial and social barriers, making motorsport more accessible.

William wants to emulate two other F1 World Champions from Somerset, Jenson Button and Lando Norris [BBC]

"William wouldn't be racing without the FAT Karting League, who have made motorsport more affordable and accessible," explained Rebecca.

"Without the league, it could be £100,000 per season, which is beyond us, and beyond a lot of people.

"Now it's around £6,000 per season, which is still a stretch, but William loves it and he's good, so he can't not race," she added.

William's dream route to Formula One - to follow in the slipstream of his idol Lewis Hamilton - would mean competing in Formula 4 in a few years' time, and that costs £500,000 per season.

"Lewis didn't come from a wealthy family, so maybe I can be like him. I want to win 10 world titles," said William.

Rebecca is hoping sponsors could back William, if he continues winning races.

"We're hopefully going to prove that talent gets to F1, not just how deep your pockets are," said Rebecca.

A spokesperson for motorsports governing body the FIA previously told the BBC: "Improving accessibility and affordability across the motorsport pyramid is a core priority for the FIA.

"But it is important to recognise that many of the costs associated with competing - including travel, logistics and team operations - are outside the direct control of the regulator.

"These costs have also been impacted by wider economic factors over time," they added.

#2803. SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts

SpaceX said Wednesday it will donate one share of the company's class A stock to every active Trump Account, and CEO Elon Musk would personally match the gift.

"SpaceX is building the infrastructure of the future and working to make life multiplanetary," the company said in a statement. "We believe ownership of the company creating these systems should extend to the next generation of Americans for their long-term benefit."

The White House announced in October that nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created, including more than 60 million through auto-enrollment. It's unclear how many are considered active.

Musk said in a social media post on X on Thursday that the share from SpaceX would be transferred next month, without providing further details. "The share directly from me will be transferred when my lockup expires in June next year," he added.

Eligible children must have their account claimed to enable contributions from family members, friends and employers, and to receive the government's one-time $1,000 seed contribution. Auto-enrolled children can receive philanthropic contributions without a parent or guardian claiming an account, according to TrumpAccounts.gov.

Representatives for SpaceX and the U.S. Department of the Treasury did not immediately respond to a request for further details about the planned gift.

SpaceX Chief Operating Officer Gwynne Shotwell had previously agreed to make a personal gift of over 2 million SpaceX shares to Trump Accounts, with eligible account holders expected to receive them in December.

In October, Elon Musk posted on X that he would give a share of SpaceX to every kid in America next year.

Trump Accounts, also known as 530A accounts, are open to any U.S. child under 18 with a Social Security number.

Parents, guardians, grandparents and others can collectively contribute up to $5,000 per child per year in after-tax dollars up until the year before the beneficiary turns 18. The annual contribution limit is indexed for inflation after 2027.

Since the July 4 launch, families and friends have collectively contributed more than $1 billion to Trump Accounts. Total deposits now sit at more than $9 billion, CNBC first reported.

Children born from 2025 through 2028 may receive a one-time $1,000 deposit from the Treasury. Other funds may also be available, depending on certain criteria.

The Treasury rolled out temporary regulations at the end of September that allow Trump Accounts to hold stock donations, a move officials have said is meant to encourage more philanthropic gifts.

Contributing stock can benefit donors, who are able to avoid capital gains taxes triggered by selling appreciated stock. But families can face restrictions related to selling that donated stock. The policy also means some children's wealth accumulation could be tied to the performance of individual companies.

Under U.S. Department of the Treasury guidelines, families cannot refuse stock gifted to Trump Accounts and generally cannot sell it until the earlier of five years or the end of the calendar year in which the beneficiary turns 17.

SpaceX shares have been under some pricing pressure as the company negotiates a possible merger with Elon Musk's automaker Tesla. The companies have established two special committees to handle merger negotiations, and hired advisers for a potential mega-deal.

CNBC's Sam Meredith, Kevin Breuninger, Kate Dore and Jess Dickler contributed to this story

#2802. Lakers, behind Luka Doncic, must crack Wemby code, exploit these areas to beat Spurs

The stars are aligning for a special regular-season game between the Lakers and Spurs on Friday night in San Antonio.

It's two of the NBA's best teams and the Western Conference's top teams matching up for the first time this season, featuring the top early-season MVP candidates in Lakers guard Luka Dončić and Spurs big man Victor Wembanyama.

Luka Doncic of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots a free throw. NBAE via Getty Images

And it's also, essentially, a win-or-go-home NBA Group Play game for West Group C.

For the Lakers, the Spurs will be the biggest test for this still-newish group, with the reigning Western Conference champions equipped to poke the holes that most of the Lakers' previous opponents haven't been able to.

But the Lakers are also equipped to test the Spurs in ways not every team is able to.

And it starts with Dončić — one of the league's best offensive players who can force the Spurs to have to adjust their defensive strategy of keeping Wembanyama around the basket as much as possible.

Even amid a shooting slump, the Slovenian superstar's pull-up shooting and playmaking force defenses to be more aggressive with their coverages when he's the ball handler in pick and rolls.

San Antonio Spurs center Victor Wembanyama walks up the court. AP Photo/Darren Abate

Or even when he's isolating his defender, with opponents often showing proactive help to crowd driving and passing lanes.

If Austin Reaves is available against the Spurs after missing the previous five games due to a bruised left heel, the Spurs won't be able to load up their defense on Dončić in the same way.

The Spurs' defensive scheme allows for their perimeter defenders to be more aggressive with the intent of funneling actions toward Wembanyama, who is the league's best rim protector in large part due to his 7-foot-4 frame.

But the Lakers' use of Dončić starting possessions off the ball before receiving a screen to get the ball can test the Spurs' preference.

Austin Reaves (15) of the Los Angeles Lakers clapping. NBAE via Getty Images

If Wembanyama is too far in a drop and Dončić's defender doesn't stay attached, Dončić has the room he needs to get his shot off.

Wembanyama has the size and agility to play closer to the level of the screen while being in position to recover to protect the rim.

It's a cat-and-mouse game Dončić is just as equipped as any player to win against the Spurs, who once again have one of the league's best defenses.

Another area the Lakers are equipped to exploit is the Spurs' stingy transition defense.

The Spurs are one of the best teams at preventing scoring on fast breaks, but the Lakers are one of the best. They'll have to remain consistent with their throwahead passes without turning the ball over.

And defensively, Walker Kessler empowers the Lakers to prioritize protecting the paint against Wembanyama and forcing the Spurs, who are shooting 33.7% on 3-pointers (No. 25), to beat them with outside shots.

Friday will be a big test for the Lakers.

But they're equipped to come out of the matchup better than expected.

#2800. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2799. Bucks vs. Nets Player Grades: Turner, Burries, and rest of team come down to earth in dreadful showing

In what might have been the worst Bucks game I've watched in some time—which is saying something—the Milwaukee Bucks lost to the 3-13 Brooklyn Nets 104-97. It really felt like the Bucks got the monkey off their backs against the Raptors and came into this game without a care in the world. They didn't play as a team, nor did they play with any passion. The worst part is that there aren't going to be many games this season where the Bucks have a genuine 50-50 chance of winning, and for Milwaukee to put up that kind of performance against a Brooklyn team that really tried its hardest to lose was really disappointing. The game also brings the Bucks to 0-3 in the NBA Cup. Read our full summary of the game here and catch a six-minute audio recap on the Bucks+ podcast, Bucks In Six Minutes, below.

37 minutes, 28 points, 3 rebounds, 5 assists, 3 steals, 5 turnovers, 10/20 FG, 3/9 3P, 5/5 FT, 0

I mean, Herro was just about the team's only source of creation, so I do want to credit him for that. Overall, though, this wasn't all that impressive of a game. I didn't think Herro was as willing to get his teammates involved as he had been, settling for a fair few bad shots and dominating the ball too much; granted, he would have had a few more assists if anyone could make a shot, but alas. And then the turnovers, man; most of them felt like killer live-ball giveaways.

18 minutes, 2 points, 3 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 0 turnovers, 0/7 FG, 0/4 3P, -7

Oof. Really did not like Rollins' game. He made some nice reads to open the third, but overall, most of his shots came after he dribbled the air out of the ball for 10-14 seconds, then settled for step-back threes or wild drives at the rim. It was almost as if Rollins was trying to force his way into a groove, rather than playing instinctively and letting opportunities come to him. Moreover, we can usually rely on Rollins' defence being solid, but even that part of his game felt off; there was one possession in the third where the Bucks had finally taken the lead, were in a groove, and Rollins was guarding someone on the right wing; he was in perfect position to contain the ball, but he lunged for the steal, didn't get it, and his man went right past him, leading to the open three. Absolute coach killer.

31 minutes, 13 points, 3 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 turnover, 5/11 FG, 3/7 3P, -12

Not too many complaints about Kuzma's game. He was just about Milwaukee's only credible defender on Randle, and overall, I thought he did a solid job making the Nets veteran work. Also made some solid reads to get into the paint and then spray out to shooters.

34 minutes, 21 points, 4 rebounds, 4 assists, 2 steals, 9/15 FG, 1/5 3P, 2/4 FT, +5

Jaquez was solid in the middle game and a key part of the Bucks' best runs. He finished well around the rim and got his teammates involved. Still, can't go 1/5 from three when it feels like most of them are wide open. Just can't happen.

25 minutes, 3 points, 6 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 3 blocks, 3 turnovers, 1/2 FG, 1/2 3P, -1

Obviously, people will look at the scoring numbers, but I would say Turner's lack of production was a byproduct of the Bucks' lack of ball movement. Against the Raptors, the ball was moving, and that's how he got his opportunities; against the Nets, everything was stagnant and slow, so Turner shot just twice. The big man still impacted the game in other ways, but he also had a few backbreaking live-ball turnovers. Overall, it wasn't a good game for Turner by any means, but I thought other factors also contributed to him not playing well.

22 minutes, 13 points, 14 rebounds, 2 assists, 2 blocks, 1 turnover, 6/10 FG, 1/2 3P, -6

On balance, Ware was probably one of Milwaukee's better players. He created extra opportunities, with five of his 14 rebounds coming on the offensive glass, and he would have scored more points if just a few of those tip-ins had gone in. Also, continues to rack up blocks and be a presence around the rim (on both ends). I will say that the Nets were happy to play much higher in their pick-and-roll coverage with Ware in the game because they didn't trust his decision-making with a numbers advantage; he has to improve in that facet of the game.

21 minutes, 3 points, 3 rebounds, 3 assists, 2 turnovers, 1/6 FG, 0/2 3P, 1/2 FT, -4

Burries ended his hot streak with a stinker. He couldn't find the same creases he had been finding (which is weird against one of the league's worst defensive teams), and he also didn't impact in other areas the way he usually does. Burries also made a really odd turnover in the fourth quarter: he caught the ball with no awareness that MPJ was right there and got it ripped away; part of that was on Ware for passing him the ball there, but Burries needs to have better awareness.

21 minutes, 8 points, 3 rebounds, 1 turnover, 4 PF, 3/6 FG, 2/5 3P, 0

I mean, Ament got most of his looks off catch-and-shoot opportunities, but he also got handed a few grenades as the shot clock was winding down. The rook was the third defender (behind Kuzma and Jaquez) on Randle, and despite a major weight disadvantage, he was decent in that matchup; Ament clearly read the scout, forcing him to his right hand and into help as much as possible—good stuff. He had a few possessions where he rotated late from the weak side in help, though.

12 minutes, 4 points, 4 rebounds, 3 assists, 2/7 FG, 0/5 3P, +2

Weird minutes for Jakucionis, who played three minutes in the first half and then was thrown into the fire at the end without really getting a feel for the game. He missed all his threes, but most were the right shots to take (the one bad one being his corner three, where he had Herro wide open for the extra pass up top). Also got baited into a bad foul late as the Bucks were making a charge.

11 minutes, 2 points, 2 assists, 1/2 FG, -6

I don't know. I guess LeVert was fine in his minutes. As I'll get to below, my issue was with how much he played.

This was the first game where I truly felt Jenkins played a key role in the team losing, mainly because of rotations. He opened the second quarter with a Rollins-Green-LeVert-Kuzma-Turner lineup that had a -78.6 net rating in 3:18, with Rollins—who, news flash, has been struggling—being the only creator (and boy did he try to create!). Then, to start the fourth quarter, Jenkins threw out a Jakucionis-Burries-LeVert-Ament-Ware lineup that had a -40.0 net rating in 2:38. One downside to starting all the veterans—a unit that, to their credit, played well together in this one—is that you can eventually get to lineups like these. Granted, I get that with all of this you're robbing Peter to pay Paul to some degree: if you take one guy from one lineup to help another, that original lineup will lose something. Even so, Jenkins can strike a much better balance than he did last night.

DNP-CD: Jericho Sims, Ousmane Dieng, Bogoljub Markovic, Gary Trent Jr.

Inactive: Pete Nance, Cormac Ryan, Kam Jones,

Even the Bucks can get high on their own supply and, dare I say it, disrespect their opponent.

Jenkins: "We abandoned what was working for us in the first half. We didn't have rhythm to our offence. I guess we got bored with the stuff that was working well for us."

Kuzma: "I think we were just thinking for ourselves… How can I get myself going instead of how can I get the team going? That's the story of our season right now."

I can't stress enough how much the Nets tried to give this game to the Bucks. They shot 41.6% from the field and 26.3% from three. Michael Porter Jr. went 4/14, for goodness' sake; you have to win when that happens. It felt like the team entered this game thinking they'd get a win; they were playing a team just as bad, and another win would come easy while they all played for their own numbers.

The Bucks had 14 turnovers for the game, and nine came in the last quarter.

Assistant coach Brad Jones noted at halftime that all 47 of the Bucks' first-half points came after touching the paint in that possession—every one.

I don't get the LeVert minutes sometimes. Like, if he stands out in his first stint, sure, throw him out there for another; but if his first stint doesn't stand out in any way, why put him back in? Surely you'd rather give those minutes to Dieng or even a guard. Sometimes the rotations can feel incredibly random.

The Bucks head to Detroit on Friday to face the Pistons, kicking off Milwaukee's back-to-back, with OKC in town the following night. Catch the game at 6:30 p.m. CST on MY24.

#2798. Tom Brady rips into Aaron Rodgers for breaking ‘the sanctity of an NFL locker room’

This time it was Tom Brady, who kept it professional but made clear that Rodgers' takedown of the Steelers after his benching needed to stay in house.

"The sanctity of an NFL locker room, it's a very sacred place," Brady said on the Fox broadcast of the Eagles' 39-38 win over the Cowboys on Thanksgiving in Dallas. "We shouldn't ever lose track of that as players. It's a privilege to be in that locker room. You're fighting with your teammates, you're fighting with your coaches, you're always acting as a team together. Fox Sports broadcast analyst Tom Brady looks on before the 49ers' win over the Cowboys on Nov. 15, 2026, in Arlington, Texas. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

"I wasn't a perfect player, [Bill Belichick] wasn't a perfect coach, but we did some things in the game that we're so proud of now," Brady said. "And even me being four years removed from the game, things that matter more than winning a Super Bowl or trophy is my relationship with my teammates, my coaches. That's going to transcend anything you do on the field.

"It's just unfortunate. I hate it, I hate to be even talking about it. It's a privilege to be in the NFL. You want to inspire the next generation of athletes, and you always want to do it the right way."

Aaron Rodgers wears a frustrated expression during the Steelers' loss to the Eagles on Nov. 22, 2026, in Philadelphia. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

#2797. Brittany Mahomes Calls Out Refs For Overturned Rashee Rice Play

The Kansas City Chiefs fell to the Buffalo Bills, 13-10, on Thanksgiving Night. It was the final game of a jam-packed NFL schedule, and Tyler Bass hit the game-winning field goal after a tipped punt by the Chiefs to give the Bills their 10th victory of the season.

There were plenty of moments that the Chiefs will reflect on and say they want back, especially in a one-score game.

However, there was a controversial play involving wide receiver Rashee Rice.

The Chiefs WR caught a pass over the middle that was ruled a catch at the time. However, the officials overturned the call.

That decision by the referees sparked plenty of backlash at the time, and it continued after the game.

Brittany Mahomes, the wife of Chiefs quarterback Patrick Mahomes, fired off a harsh message at the officials on her Instagram.

"HAHAHAHAHAHA WHAT A JOKE Guess we can't even play football without getting it called against us… not sure what an actual catch is after that I guess?"

That catch would've given the Chiefs a first down with less than two minutes remaining.

Instead, it resulted in a punt, and the Bills got the ball back in a momentum-shifting moment.

NFL VP of Instant Replay Mark Butterworth also gave his insight on the decision.

"As the receiver is going to the ground, he had the ball in his right hand and when he contacted the ground, his hand slid up the ball and we deemed that a loss of control," he said after the game.

Chiefs head coach Andy Reid spoke to reporters after the game and said it needs to be "clear and obvious" while adding that the play was "not clear and obvious."

Former NFL QB Robert Griffin III also said on X that Chiefs fans have a right to be upset after the call was overturned.

Here is a video of the play in question, and it was a big moment late in what was a tie game.

Former NFL star receivers Chad Johnson and Terrell Owens also shared their thoughts on X, and neither one thought the call should've been overturned.

The Chiefs did get the ball back after that, although they didn't do anything and ended up punting the ball away, giving the Bills good field position to win the game.

There were plenty of controversial calls in this game, but the Rashee Rice catch, or non-catch, will be one that might be circulating around the league office for the next few days.

And, the Chiefs certainly aren't happy about it, and neither are plenty of former NFL players and analysts.

#2796. Spain’s Pablo Hernández de Cos set to lead European Central Bank

Pablo Hernández de Cos is set to succeed Christine Lagarde as president of the European Central Bank next year.

He received backing from euro zone leaders at the European Council Summit on Thursday. The role still requires formal appointment after consultation with the European Parliament and the ECB's Governing Council, but neither has veto power and the decision follows intense political wrangling to reach a consensus on who should serve the eight-year term.

De Cos is a 55-year-old economist and career central banker who rose through the ranks of the Bank of Spain, starting as a researcher in the 1990s and working in various departments before becoming governor from 2018 to June 2024.

He chaired the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision from 2019 to 2024, and has been General Manager of the Basel-based Bank for International Settlements since July 2025.

Both de Cos and his main rival for the ECB top job, the Netherlands' Klaas Knot, were widely seen as candidates with deep knowledge and experience in monetary policy and financial stability — attributes that took on particular importance at a time of resurgent inflation, fragmentation and political uncertainty.

Lagarde had extensive experience in financial institutions but was criticized by some early in her tenure for a lack of central banking experience.

De Cos gained sufficient political backing in closed-door discussions and negotiations since Lagarde said she would step down on Feb. 7, 2027, over seven years after she became the first woman to helm the ECB. That was despite a final push by the Netherlands at the European Council meeting this week backing Knot.

Representatives from the euro area's biggest economies — Germany, France, Italy, Spain and the Netherlands — have been horse-trading as they look to secure representation on the central bank's six-member Executive Board.

Influential roles as chief economist, expected to go to Germany's Markus Brunnermeier, and head of the Markets Portfolio, for which France's Laurence Boone has reportedly been tapped, also change hands in 2027.

Analysts say de Cos's commentary and actions over more than two decades at Spain's central bank paint him as moderately dovish — inclined to favor lower interest rates to support economic growth — but also as a pragmatist who has been willing to adapt.

That has included support for strong rate hikes during the energy price shock that followed Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, but also giving staunch backing for rates below zero and the huge stimulus package launched by then-ECB President Mario Draghi to reboot the euro zone economy in 2019.

De Cos quipped in 2024 that he was an "owl" after being asked if he was a hawk or a dove, emphasizing his focus on data and the situation at hand.

"It is true that Hernández de Cos supported the ECB's uber-dovish monetary policy during the period when inflation was persistently below target. However, his monetary policy views may well be regime-dependent," Christian Kopf, head of fixed income and foreign exchange at Union Investment, said in emailed comments.

"We are now clearly in a higher-inflation regime, with core inflation in the euro area expected to remain above the ECB's target through 2029."

Kopf added that de Cos was the candidate with the "strongest technical understanding of monetary policy and central banking," which he said was important at a time when the world is "witnessing an assault on central bank independence, not only in the United States but also in countries such as Japan."

"By appointing a career technocrat such as Hernández de Cos as ECB President, the European Council would send a strong signal that Europe will not waver in its commitment to price stability and that the euro will remain a hard currency," Kopf said.

Spain's de Cos as president, Germany's Brunnermeier as chief economist and France's Boone as head of market operations represent a "carefully crafted package deal," Kopf also noted, because it balances representation from northern and southern European countries. The result was partially due to the knock-on effects of the German government pushing hard for the chief economist position, he said.

Along with a desire among politicians for influence, a north-south balance is seen as important as the northern countries are seen as leaning more hawkish — more inclined to opt for rate hikes and restrictive policy to tame inflation — while southern are perceived as more dovish.

John Orchard, chairman of the Digital Monetary Institute at think tank OMFIF, said that as a "central banker's central banker," de Cos had "managed to earn respect from typically hawkish northern observers."

Another supportive factor of de Cos's candidacy was the stellar growth rate of many of those southern economies in recent years.

Once the "sick men" of the euro area's 2010s sovereign debt crisis, Portugal, Italy, Spain and Greece have become some of the bloc's strongest growth drivers in recent years.

Factors such as strong tourism, relative political stability, increased foreign investment and strengthening public finances have boosted southern economies. Meanwhile, the euro zone's traditional powerhouse Germany has stuttered amid soaring industrial costs and a struggling autos market.

Orchard said that some believed that Lagarde's presidency had ushered in an "esprit de corps" at the ECB, with policymakers focused on the interests of the whole European economy.

That would, in theory, reduce the importance of nationality among leadership and of traditional notions of hawkish northern nations and dovish southern ones. However, Orchard also noted the recurrent theme of national representation in the recent debate and the perception of Knot as a hawkish choice.

Diverging performance is a core challenge for any leader of the ECB, which must set monetary policy for 21 economies all growing at different speeds and with their own domestic priorities and inflationary pressures.

But it has become particularly pressing this year as the bloc's second-biggest economy, France, is teetering on the edge of its own potential debt crisis.

Moves in French markets and whether the ECB needs to take more extreme measures beyond continuing to urge fiscal discipline will remain in focus for de Cos.

The ECB Executive Board's view of what measures could be taken to prevent market disorder and under what conditions was a key topic under discussion when choosing who should fill the roles, according to insiders.

Despite political efforts to pass a budget that will reduce its budget deficit to around 5% of GDP, investors have been driving up French borrowing costs on concerns over its mounting public debt-to-GDP ratio, high budget deficit and contracting economy.

Risks are being compounded by political uncertainty ahead of France's presidential election in April, which is stymying business activity and raising wider questions about EU unity as far-right and far-left candidates pull ahead.

Rising French yields and the widening spread in France's borrowing costs over Germany's have led to speculation over whether the ECB might need to deploy tools such as liquidity injections or its never-used Transmission Protection Instrument to protect other countries from contagion and support the euro.

This would be politically controversial unless France agreed to stringent fiscal discipline measures akin to those that southern nations signed up to during the Sovereign Debt Crisis — which could be complicated if far-right presidential frontrunner Marine Le Pen, who has a combative relationship with EU institutions, wins.

Ken Egan, head of European sovereigns at ratings agency KBRA, said fragmentation was always a challenge for the ECB, which does not want to see divergent financing conditions and high sovereign spreads across the bloc.

However, he said that in the case of France he believed we were "some way from having a serious conversation about ECB intervention."

"If anything, sovereign spreads have pulled back a bit and tightened, and some measures of market liquidity have also improved in recent weeks," he said by email.

"That doesn't mean the underlying risks have disappeared, but I think it's important to distinguish between periods of market volatility and a more fundamental breakdown in monetary policy transmission."

De Cos also takes up the mantle as Europe faces yet another surge in price pressures.

Euro area inflation rose to 4% in October, its highest level in more than three years. Energy was the biggest contributor, as the Iran war drives up oil and gas prices and tightens the supply of refined fuels.

That leaves the central bank facing a difficult policy balance, preventing higher energy costs from spreading into wages and wider prices while avoiding unnecessary damage to growth.

De Cos will inherit major supranational projects, including plans for a digital euro and Europe's long-delayed capital-markets integration. In a recent speech, Lagarde warned that each year without capital markets integration meant "more of our savings are invested abroad and more of our growing firms look elsewhere for funding."

France is not the only euro area country heading for a pivotal election next year, with votes in Finland, Greece, Italy, Estonia, Slovakia and Slovenia also expected, adding to the politically uncertain climate.

It is also not the only country grappling with higher government borrowing costs, public debt and a struggle to find fiscal headroom, a growing problem among many developed countries.

The ECB's Financial Stability Review meanwhile warned this month of mounting risks from European investors' growing exposure to the U.S. economy, and equity and credit markets' vulnerability to AI.

Outgoing head Lagarde, meanwhile, told the European Banking Congress in Frankfurt this month that her biggest concern was now cyber threats, an area she said policymakers had not paid enough attention.

"The one [worry] that keeps me awake, and many of you awake ​as well ... ​is ⁠AI applied together with cyber threat," she said.

Center Juice Scruggs was called for back-to-back false starts and then picked up a third later in the half. "We don't have to. We don't have to," Simmons said, via Dave Birkett of the Detroit Free Press "We line up and play football. They quarterback check. I said, 'Run.' I didn't say, 'Hut.' I mean, there was a couple times that they false start, we didn't say a word. So that's their problem, not ours. . . . If I say, 'Run,' I'm telling my defense, run. Think I care to tell them what they about to do? I'm talking to my guys. So all the crying that they're doing, that's a them problem, not me."

Mahogany was flagged for taunting Simmons during a 70-yard Jahmyr Gibbs touchdown run in the fourth quarter and kicker Jake Bates missed the ensuing extra point to leave the score 35-24. The Lions would not score again and the Bears headed back to Chicago with an improbable victory.
