#2793. ‘You’re a Rich Bad Teammate’ — Hall of Famer Takes Issue With Aaron Rodgers’ Decision To Expose Mike McCarthy’s Habits

The fallout from Aaron Rodgers' scorched-earth interview on "The Pat McAfee Show" continues to rock the NFL, with Pro Football Hall of Famer Michael Strahan the latest legend to weigh in on the mess.

Speaking on Fox's Thanksgiving pregame broadcast, Strahan sharply criticized Rodgers for publicly airing internal grievances and revealing behind-the-scenes details about Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy, arguing that the quarterback's actions ultimately hurt the entire locker room.

After being benched by the Pittsburgh Steelers, Rodgers appeared on "The Pat McAfee Show" and opened up about his experience with McCarthy before being subsequently released by the team.

The 42-year-old quarterback made several accusations against his former coach, claiming McCarthy would fall asleep and snore during quarterback meetings, miss walkthroughs, and fail to properly study game film. Rodgers also recalled an incident in which McCarthy reportedly shouted "1-2-3 Packers" while breaking down a halftime huddle.

His comments quickly made headlines, with several anonymous players and former teammates corroborating Rodgers' account. One source even confirmed the validity of the anecdotes to ESPN's Brooke Pryor, stating plainly, "It's all true."

However, Strahan believes that being right about what happened inside the building does not give Rodgers carte blanche to discuss it publicly.

"We've all had disagreements. But you just don't publicly air those things like this… Mike McCarthy, he's probably not innocent with a lot of these things. But I'm sure there's something on the other side. Keep it between yourself. Work it out. You hurt your team. It's about the team. We are playing a team sport," Strahan said.

PREDICT THE NFL SEASON: PFN's Free NFL Playoff Predictor

He went on to question Rodgers' mindset as a leader, suggesting the quarterback consistently prioritizes his own narrative over the franchise's collective good.

"Always let the coach win in public. In private, you handle your business with the coach. He lost that rule… You're a rich, bad teammate. And I'm not saying McCarthy is innocent. Trust me. But when you look at it, there always seems to be smoke around Aaron Rodgers, he added.

Strahan isn't the only Hall of Famer who took issue with Rodgers' decision to publicly discuss the Steelers' internal problems. Pittsburgh legends Terry Bradshaw and Rod Woodson also questioned the quarterback's conduct following his departure.

Joining Strahan on the FOX broadcast, Bradshaw defended his longtime friend McCarthy and hit out at Rodgers for lacking grace.

"The one thing you don't do, all of us athletes up here, none of us turn on our teammates. We don't turn on our coaching staff. We don't turn on our ownership. We show class on our way out. Aaron Rodgers didn't do that," Bradshaw said.

Rod Woodson expressed similar surprise that internal disagreements had become public during his appearance on "The Rich Eisen Show." However, the former Steelers DB also questioned McCarthy's leadership and whether Pittsburgh's decision to bench Rodgers was motivated by personal disagreements rather than football performance.

With his Steelers tenure over, the 42-year-old Rodgers is now an unrestricted free agent.

Having previously made it clear that 2026 could be his final season, Rodgers, who ranks 29th among QBs with a 67.1 PFN QB Impact Grade, now faces the possibility that his explosive media appearance has ended not just his time in Pittsburgh, but his NFL career.

#2792. Inside India newsletter: Roaring economy, foreign investors’ return and now record auto sales

Hello, this is Priyanka Salve, writing to you from Singapore.

Welcome to the latest edition of "Inside India" — your one-stop destination for stories and developments from the world's fastest-growing large economy.

Despite 2026 being a challenging year with high global energy prices and supply chain disruptions, the Indian economy has fared surprisingly well. The auto industry, a key pillar of manufacturing and employment, has breached several records this year and is poised to report its best-ever sales.

This week, I spoke to experts to decode what this stellar performance means for one of the world's most promising consumer markets.

Any thoughts on today's newsletter? Share them with the team.

India's economy expanded at 7.8% in the June quarter, beating expectations, even as growth in several major economies cooled. Foreign portfolio investors who had been exiting Indian markets in droves for most of the year have also started returning in historic numbers.

And now, the world's third-largest auto market is set to for a record this year as better road connectivity, tax cuts, and easier financing options have fueled bumper sales over the last several months. That comes at a time when major auto markets across the world, from China to the U.S. have been seeing a slump in sales.

"10 years ago, despite having the money, people did not buy cars, but now even if they don't have the money, they take financing and buy," Amar Sheth, vice president of the Federation of Automobile Dealers Associations, told CNBC, pointing to the shift in consumer mindset.

They are becoming "more optimistic" about the future and, rather than waiting, consumers are willing to take a loan and purchase something useful to them now, he adds.

The industry body that Sheth represents said on Monday that automakers in India were poised to sell more than 30 million vehicles in the financial year ending March 2027, dubbing the sector as a "reflection of the wider India growth story."

From April to October, total vehicle retail sales in India reached 18.7 million units, up 10% from a year earlier, with car, tractor and commercial vehicle sales growing more than 15%, as per FADA data.

The optimism of the industry body is also shared by major automakers; several of whom have announced capacity expansion plans this year to meet the growing consumer demand.

Japanese automakers Maruti Suzuki and Toyota plan to raise capacity by 1.2 million units and 200,000 units, while Korea's Hyundai Motors will add capacity for 150,000 units, as per data shared by automotive industry intelligence provider Mobility Global. Leading Indian companies Tata Motors, Mahindra & Mahindra and JSW have also announced capacity addition plans, the data showed.

India is the only big auto market in the world that is growing while most others such as France, Germany, and Korea are "either declining or are flat," Puneet Gupta, director India and ASEAN at Mobility Global, told CNBC, explaining the reason behind car companies investing in the country.

Gupta said that the Chinese automotive market hit an inflection point in 2007, when the GDP per capita crossed $2,500, and now India is "at a similar point." He added that government support for the auto industry would be crucial in replicating the boom seen in the Chinese auto industry.

The catalyst for India's auto sector boom traces back to last September, when the government announced a wide range of tax cuts to spur domestic consumption.

The auto industry contributes 7.1% to the country's gross domestic product, 49% to manufacturing GDP, 15% to goods and services tax collection, and supports 30 million jobs, as per government data.

While the tailwinds from the tax cuts have faded, experts now believe that replacement demand and the growing reach of financing options to smaller cities will unlock the next phase of growth.

Consumption has been strong this year as the government has partly shielded people from rising energy costs while corporates, with their strong balance sheets, have been slow to pass on price hikes, Gaura Sengupta, chief economist at India's IDFC First Bank, said.

So far this year, wage growth has been good, the labor market has held up equity markets haven't corrected sharply, keeping the wealth effect intact, she said, underscoring the reasons driving consumption along with easy access to credit.

Despite the economy facing external shocks, producers are also "feeling confident to add capacity" as they have seen domestic demand sustain, Sengupta added.

However, experts said that the central bank's recent tightening of monetary policy and price hikes by automakers could slightly take the edge off the auto sales boom in the last few months of the financial year.

While urban demand is unlikely to get affected, rural sales growth could moderate, they added.

"Overall retail should close FY27 in high single digits," Diwakar Murugan, senior automotive analyst at Omdia, told CNBC, adding that this year's record sales are not a one-time spike but a "structural reset."

China has changed tactics but is still thwarting India's rise, a U.S. panel saysChina is "actively working to suppress India's rise," even as the relationship between the two major Asian powers has thawed in the past year, according to a U.S. panel that sees an opening for greater outreach to India.

U.K.-India trade deal faces a stiff test over Scotch whisky import taxesWithin months of taking effect, the India-U.K. trade deal is facing an early test, as New Delhi resists extending preferential tariffs to bulk Scotch whisky imports.

Investors give a thumbs down to Tata Steel-APL Apollo $1.67 billion deal, plunging sharesTata Steel, one of the world's leading steel producers, on Monday said it will buy a 26.4% stake in India's largest structural steel pipe and tube maker, APL Apollo, for 155.1 billion rupees ($1.67 billion). However, investors reacted negatively to the deal.

Nov. 27: Mahanadi Coalfields IPO listing.

Nov. 30: GDP data for July-September 2026.

#2791. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2790. Even the price of toys is on the rise. How Americans are scaling back holiday plans

As Amanda Brecht planned how her family would spend Thanksgiving this year, she had to make some difficult decisions.

Because of high gas prices, the 30-year-old decided she wouldn't drive across her home state of Michigan to celebrate with extended family. Instead, Brecht and her husband will spend the holiday with just their three kids. And with food prices rising, they'll be skipping appetizers and serving turkey breast instead of a whole bird.

"Thanksgiving is definitely going to look different this year," Brecht said. "Money doesn't grow on trees. You can only do so much."

With inflation accelerating, Americans like Brecht will see higher prices almost everywhere they look this holiday season. At a time of year synonymous with food, gifts and big spending, many consumers are instead pinching pennies, paring back their plans and making tough choices about how they'll celebrate.

Some are raiding their savings accounts and running up debt on credit cards. They're reporting downbeat outlooks about the economy in surveys. And they've become more selective with spending as inflationary pressures mount.

"Families are facing some tough choices this year," said Alexander Smith, an associate professor who researches behavioral economics at the Worcester Polytechnic Institute. "They might have to cut back on holiday spending in whatever way that they can."

A classic Thanksgiving feast will be harder to pull off as grocery prices rise.

The price of a 10-person Thanksgiving dinner increased 6% to $58.63 in 2026, according to an annual survey from the American Farm Bureau Federation. Faith Parum, the organization's deputy chief economist, said the price increase was a result of higher costs for labor and transportation.

Grocers including Walmart and Aldi have gained traction on social media by offering Thanksgiving meal bundles as a value-focused workaround to higher prices. Walmart's offering promises to feed 10 for $35, and includes a turkey and ingredients to make mashed potatoes, green bean casserole, cranberries, marshmallow-topped sweet potatoes and a pumpkin pie.

Still, some consumers will feel disappointed by having less choice in their menus due to higher prices, according to Joe Bruseulas, chief economist at tax firm RSM.

"We're missing the point here if all we're doing is focusing on the fact that Walmart will sell you a homogeneous dinner meal" at a discount, Bruseulas said. "People feel horrible that they have to go to that because the price of everything has just risen so significantly over the past five years."

Inflation is affecting more than Thanksgiving staples like turkey or stuffing. Food prices on average have jumped around 24% over the last five years, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Some items have seen even sharper increases: Coffee and ground beef, for example, have each climbed by more than 40% in the same period despite pulling back from recent highs.

At the same time, millions of Americans lost SNAP benefits under policy changes included in President Donald Trump's "big beautiful bill." Food banks across the U.S. have seen foot traffic skyrocket this year as the reduction in federal nutrition aid coincides with rising prices in grocery aisles.

The Salvation Army said some of its food pantries in October provided double or triple the amount of groceries than they did in the same month a year ago. Demand is now so high that some food banks in Feeding America's national network are worried about running out of supplies.

"We want to make sure that our food banks are as full as they possibly can be going into the holiday season," said Linda Nageotte, Feeding America's president and operations chief. "Far too many of them have empty shelves."

High energy prices as a result of the war with Iran are also affecting Americans' holiday plans.

Slightly fewer Americans will travel at least 50 miles around the Thanksgiving holiday in 2026, marking the first year-over-year decline since the country was in lockdown from the pandemic in 2020, according to AAA. Stacey Barber, a vice president at the auto association, attributed this year's pullback in part to higher fuel costs.

AAA said that about nine out of every 10 Thanksgiving travelers will do so by car. While gas prices have come down from a May peak, they are still up about 18% year over year and could set a new record for the month of November, according to the organization's data.

Travelers are paying about 31% and 23% more for roundtrip domestic airfare around Thanksgiving and Christmas, respectively, than they did a year ago, according to booking platform Hopper. Airlines have blamed the ticket price hikes on rising jet fuel costs in the wake of the war.

"There will be a significant shell shock when they see the price of traveling," said Hayley Berg, lead economist at Hopper.

Berg said price increases would feel more noticeable on routes previously serviced by Spirit, the budget airline that shut down in May. Travelers are booking holiday flights earlier than they did in past years, a sign that prices are top of mind, she said.

Staying home for the holidays isn't a silver-bullet solution, either, as heating costs jump.

The average American household is expected to spend 7% more on home heating this winter than they did last year, according to the National Energy Assistance Directors Association. For the typical family, that equates to an increase of about $66 and a total bill for the season above $1,000.

To be sure, some families will feel it more than others. Heating oil, which is used mainly in homes in the Northeast, has jumped around 70% year over year. However, natural gas, which is one of the most common heating types in the U.S., costs about the same as last year.

Some U.S. states, like Massachusetts and New York, beefed up their home energy support programs to account for higher prices. But Mark Wolfe, NEADA's executive director, said federal aid offerings haven't been meaningfully expanded this year.

"Some of these increases are caused directly by the decision to go to war," Wolfe said. "Why isn't the federal government helping families pay these higher bills?"

The spirit of giving will take on a different meaning for consumers this year.

Retail sales during the all-important holiday season are expected to grow almost 5% this year and surpass $1 trillion for the first time, according to Bain & Company. But the consulting firm said most of that growth would come from inflation, rather than from consumers buying more products.

And Citi on Wednesday said that increased holiday spending would be mainly driven by higher earners this year. The bank said that's a result of the "K"-shaped economy, a term used to describe a scenario where better-off Americans splash out while others struggle.

Toy prices have climbed more than 9% over the past year and now sit at their highest levels since 2019, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics. Manufacturers have raised prices in response to higher costs for transportation and plastic, according to Chris Byrne, an independent toy industry analyst. The artificial intelligence boom has driven up prices for the chips that power gaming devices and other electronics.

Byrne said that's causing shoppers to focus more on the quality of gifts rather than quantity.

"The toy industry always reflects the culture at large," Byrne said. "It's almost true economically as well. As inflation is rising, people are being a little bit more cautious about what they're buying."

Just 16% of respondents to a Morning Consult survey said they planned to spend more in the 2026 holiday season than they did a year ago. Respondents who were older or had lower earnings were more likely to say their budgets were shrinking, the survey found.

Some are doing away with gifts altogether. A poll from consulting firm TSG and the Electronic Transactions Association found 5% of Americans did not plan to buy any presents in 2026, up three percentage points from last year.

One shopper, Wendy Cooper, set a limit of one gift for each of her three adult children this year. For her teenage daughter, the 56-year-old Atlanta resident has scoured Five Below, the trendy value retailer, for budget-friendly presents.

"It's super stressful," Cooper said. "You have to weigh: Do you guys want lights on and a place to live? Or do you want gifts?"

Across the board, the Bureau of Labor Statistics' consumer price index reaccelerated after the U.S. first struck Iran in late February. Annual inflation rates have begun rising faster than wages in the same period, leaving consumers with less purchasing power.

Even if Americans keep spending for the holidays, economists caution that high prices during this busy shopping period can further drag on their economic outlooks. In the CNBC Voter Poll early this month, about three-fourths of participants rated the economy as poor or not good. Consumer confidence gauges had dropped significantly this year, but have regained ground in the runup to the holiday shopping season.

From her home in Northern Michigan, Brecht is doing everything she can to make Christmas feel special without breaking the bank.

The graduate student has been delivering DoorDash orders in her spare time and listing clothes on resale platforms to save up money for her kids' holiday gifts. Brecht is watching closely for discounts and will look for potential presents at thrift stores in the coming weeks.

If necessary, Brecht said she would turn to credit cards for some holiday purchases. She isn't alone: TSG and ETA's survey found that about one-third of Americans said they expected to go into debt during the holiday season, a roughly 74% increase from 2025.

"I don't really know how we're going to do it," Brecht said. "I'm just praying for good Black Friday deals."

#2789. Jerry Jones backs Mike McCarthy after Aaron Rodgers' rant: 'One of the finest people around football that I've been around'

Cowboys owner Jerry Jones offered strong praise for Pittsburgh Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy after Dallas' late loss to the Philadelphia Eagles on Thanksgiving.

McCarthy, who coached the Cowboys from 2020 to 2024, has been in the headlines this week after his former quarterback, Aaron Rodgers, leveled a series of accusations against him Wednesday on "The Pat McAfee Show."

Rodgers said McCarthy slept during meetings, didn't watch film and even used the wrong team name while breaking a huddle. Since that interview aired, local beat reporters have cited anonymous sources in the building who confirmed some of the claims.

While the timing likely could have been better, Jones still took the time to address the claims, and he ended up giving McCarthy his full support with effusive praise.

"I offered him [McCarthy] to stay at least another year when he left. I thought he was outstanding as the head coach. Had a great command of, frankly, quarterback, offense. Can't say enough good things about him.

He's, frankly, one of the finest people around football that I've been around. I can't say enough about him.

I never had the type of thing that is being said about him. I never had that in consideration in any of my time with him, extending his contract, any of that it was never on the table. We never had an issue within the organization."

After the 2024 season, the two sides reportedly reached an impasse in contract talks and mutually agreed to part ways.

McCarthy ended his tenure in Dallas with a 49-35 record.

Jones is the latest to defend McCarthy this week. Ahead of each Thanksgiving Day game, hosts on pregame shows defended the head coach and criticized how Rodgers delivered his message.

Former Steelers quarterbacks Terry Bradshaw and Russell Wilson, as well as Hall of Famer Michael Strahan, were among those who spoke out against Rodgers' public delivery of criticism of McCarthy.

#2788. Maple Leafs Could Be Focusing on the Wrong Trade Priority

The Toronto Maple Leafs may be looking in the wrong place to find a much-needed upgrade. While the defense corps needs some sprucing up, there's another area that requires more attention.

What if some secondary scoring actually mattered more than the blue line? That's why looking into someone like Jake DeBrusk might actually make a ton of sense.

Most of the ongoing chatter has connected the Leafs to the Simon Edvinsson sweepstakes while also suggesting that management remains interested in finding a legitimate upgrade on defense.

At face value, improving the D makes the most sense.

But what if Toronto is starting to realize its problems go deeper than just the blue line?

The reality is that the Maple Leafs defense corps has managed to hold up relatively well. But it has been another area that has cost them games this season.

Secondary scoring remains one of the biggest pain points for the organization. The Kirill Marchenko injury and William Nylander's constant disappearing act have made that clear.

That's especially problematic for a team looking to get back into the Eastern Conference playoff picture.

That is why the Leafs may ultimately need a different type of trade target than Simon Edvinsson.

And someone like Jake DeBrusk could quietly solve a roster problem that has lingered for years.

This isn't to say that the Maple Leafs shouldn't look into Edvinsson. But if there's anything that fans have learned this season, is that scoring has been a bigger pain point for the team than the defense itself.

Jake DeBrusk isn't going to be a top-line, 50-goal scorer. But he is the sort of guy who could come in and deliver in a top-six role.

While Toronto has big names in the lineup like Auston Matthews and William Nylander, the Maple Leafs have not consistently found dependable secondary production from players outside its core group.

That's where DeBrusk becomes an interesting proposition.

The 30-year-old has topped 20 goals in four of the last five seasons. While he's topped 50 points only once in his career, he has been a consistent scorer.

That's what DeBrusk could bring to the Maple Leafs lineup.

At times, Toronto's wing depth has lacked consistency, particularly when injuries begin piling up or certain players hit cold stretches. Adding a player capable of contributing throughout the lineup would provide the coaching staff with significantly more flexibility.

From a roster construction standpoint, that could be every bit as important as improving the defense corps.

Sure, there may be issues with tagging room and clearing up enough cap space this season. But if the price is right and enough cap space can be cleared, the Maple Leafs could find a viable alternative in DeBrusk.

The other factor working in Toronto's favor is DeBrusk's potential availability.

DeBrusk has been one of the names floating around recently, particularly after it seemed that he doesn't plan to stick around for the Vancouver Canucks' rebuild.

As such, DeBrusk could become one of the veteran pieces that Vancouver may be looking to move sooner rather than later. And while the team would want to get something decent in return, the Leafs may have an opportunity to move some unwanted contracts like Miles Wood.

Ultimately, the Maple Leafs may be looking in the wrong spot. While the blue line could certainly use a bit of a makeover, the bottom line is that scoring has been a much bigger challenge for the team.

And it might just be that someone like Jake DeBrusk can deliver just enough depth to help the team overcome its lack of scoring when most needed.

#2787. Liesman: Why Greenspan could bet on productivity — and Warsh needs to hike

In a discussion on CNBC's Halftime Report Wednesday, the question came up: Why can't Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh hold the line on interest rates the way his icon Alan Greenspan did at the beginning of the dot-com era?

Greenspan famously leaned against inflation hawks on his committee in 1995 and 1996, holding the line on interest rates amid strong growth, because he believed in the productivity promise of the internet revolution.

It would be nice if Warsh could follow the same playbook amid the artificial intelligence build-out and keep rates steady until the productivity arrives. Unfortunately, a quick look at the numbers shows just how different the situation is for Warsh when it comes to inflation, the magnitude of the capital-investment boom and the monetary and fiscal stimulus boosting the economy.

Bottom line: the Fed looks like it has to hike — and maybe substantially — to be anywhere close to the position where the Fed was in Greenspan's time at the dawn of a major technological revolution.

It may be that the massive capex spending on AI will mean more productivity and less inflation down the road. But the front-end build-out is proving to be more inflationary, and more challenging, to a Fed already dealing with high inflation. In many ways, that's the opposite of what Greenspan had to handle.

#2784. Buyer beware? Nvidia's volatile swings expose risks of single-stock ETFs

Nvidia's latest bout of volatility is highlighting risks tied to single-stock exchange-traded funds.

Among the hardest hit: the GraniteShares 2x Short NVDA Daily ETF (NVD). The fund sank 63% on Monday after Jensen Huang got reinstated as the chip giant's CEO. Meanwhile, the firm's 2x Long NVDA ETF (NVDL) soared nearly 62% on the same day.

ETF Action founding partner Mike Akins joined "ETF Edge" as Nvidia shares soared 31% during Monday's session, wiping a large portion of the losses from the days following Huang's resignation. Shares cooled on Tuesday and Wednesday, however.

"Historically speaking, we do not see these types of large drawdowns followed by huge drives up in a healthy market," Akins said on Monday. "It usually tends to be when you're in a recession or in a bear market territory, which we're not technically in for the broader market, but we are in for this AI trade."

But it may not be time to go cold turkey on single-stock funds.

"[Leveraged ETFs are] definitely a retail trading vehicle…  I think it's OK to have a little fun trying to trade that," Akins added. "I'm certain there are people that are better at trading these than others. But on the whole, timing the market is very, very hard."

GraniteShares founder and CEO Will Rhind also joined "ETF Edge" on Monday as Nvidia single-stock short ETFs overshadowed his firm's new Anthropic launches: The 2x Short Anthropic Daily ETF (ANS) and the 2x Long Anthropic Daily ETF (AIL).

"We thought this was going to be the biggest news event of the day with Anthropic the first leveraged ETFs going live," he said. "I can tell you this is like a sideshow compared to what we're seeing with Nvidia."

Rhind suggests Nvidia's wild swings aren't dampening enthusiasm for single-stock ETFs.

"As long as there's enthusiasm in the underlying name… people will want to trade it and most likely want to trade it with leverage either on the long on the short side," added Rhind.

"Fast Money" tackled the risks tied to ETFs that are tied to single stocks, too.

"If this is somehow part of a portfolio, [then] that is a dangerous game to be playing," trader Guy Adami said on Monday.

Karen Finerman also addressed the strategy's downsides.

"Leverage is always the thing that kills. It's always leverage," said Finerman, co-founder and CEO of Metropolitan Capital Advisors, in the same interview. "You can survive anything but leverage."

As of Wednesday's close, the GraniteShares 2x Short NVDA Daily ETF plunged 55% so far this week while the GraniteShares 2x Long NVDA Daily ETF is up 31%.

#2783. French President Emmanuel Macron condemns 'appalling' Marseille fan violence after 11 Levski Sofia supporters are injured at Stade Velodrome

French President Emmanuel Macron has led a wave of political condemnation following violent clashes between Marseille and Levski Sofia supporters at the Stade Velodrome. The Europa League fixture was overshadowed by ugly scenes that left eleven visiting fans injured, sparking a diplomatic response from both French and Bulgarian authorities.

Macron has reacted with fury to the "appalling" scenes that took place during Marseille's 1-0 victory over Levski on Thursday. Clashes broke out within the historic Stade Velodrome, leading to significant injuries among the travelling Bulgarian contingent. Macron, who spoke with Bulgarian Prime Minister Roumen Radev during a European summit in Brussels, made it clear that the perpetrators of the attacks would face the full weight of the law.

According to the official statement from the Elysee Palace, as reported by RMC Sport, Macron condemned "with the largest firmness" the "acts of violence aiming at Bulgarian supporters." The French leader further stressed that such "acts of violence have no place in sport nor in the public space," promising his Bulgarian counterpart "that these acts would not remain unpunished."

The violence reportedly began about ten minutes before the scheduled start of the match. Hundreds of Marseille supporters from the Virage Sud section managed to storm the Jean-Bouin stand, prompting a massive crowd movement. Masked individuals, some armed with flagpoles, were seen pursuing Bulgarian fans. The chaos escalated further when a fire extinguisher was discharged into the crowd, and projectiles were exchanged between the two sets of supporters near the visitor section.

The local police prefecture in the Bouches-du-Rhone department confirmed that eleven Bulgarian nationals were injured in the fray. Five of those fans required hospitalisation for serious conditions including eye trauma, head injuries, and a dislocated shoulder, while six others were treated on-site for various wounds. While the violence was widespread, authorities noted that there have been no arrests at this stage of the investigation into the disorder.

Bulgarian Vice President Iliana Iotova has expressed her horror at the treatment of her compatriots in Marseille. Writing on social media, she described the images from the stadium as "appalling" and labeled the behavior of the home crowd as "inadmissible." Her comments reflect a growing demand for a thorough investigation into how security measures failed so significantly during the high-risk European fixture.

"The French authorities must shed full light on the circumstances of the attacks and bring their authors to justice, while UEFA must check the conditions in which the security of the Bulgarian fans was ensured in the stadium. We are ready to bring all the necessary assistance to our compatriots!" Iotova wrote. The Bulgarian Ministry of Foreign Affairs echoed these sentiments, strongly denouncing the "aggressions committed against Bulgarian nationals" and confirming that diplomatic staff are monitoring the situation on the ground.

Despite the significant pre-match unrest, the game kicked off as planned, with Marseille securing a narrow win on the pitch. However, the atmosphere remained volatile throughout, necessitating a heavy presence of riot police around the 500 visiting fans. Tension flared again during the half-time interval, though the local security forces managed to prevent any further large-scale confrontations before the final whistle signaled the end of a difficult evening.

Marseille released a statement later in the evening to address the breakdown in order. The club said: "The rapid intervention of the club's security teams allowed calm to be restored, without consequence on the progress of the meeting. The club is currently working to establish the precise circumstances of these incidents, in link with the competent authorities."

#2781. How Trump’s latest tariff moves hand India an edge over rival Asian exporters

The latest U.S. tariff rates announced Wednesday have signaled that Washington is willing to offer India an edge over its competitors if New Delhi agrees to finalize a long-pending trade deal.

The Office of the United States Trade Representative has proposed a tariff of 17% on Indian exports if New Delhi "were to reach an Agreement on Reciprocal Trade" with Washington. It added that without a trade deal, India could face tariffs of up to 25% under Section 301 investigations encompassing excess production capacity that harms U.S. interests.

Meanwhile, the USTR has proposed tariffs of 18% on Bangladesh, Cambodia, Thailand, and 20% on China and Vietnam, subject to their respective trade deals, handing India a competitive advantage over rival exporters.

The U.S. expects the announcement of a lower tariff rate will encourage New Delhi to sign off on a trade deal before the G20, Atman Trivedi, partner at Washington-based DGA Albright Stonebridge Group, told CNBC.

India has got a "better bargain with Washington than its main competitors," and it may be enough for the Indian government "to declare victory and secure domestic backing for the deal," Trivedi said.

In February, U.S. President Donald Trump had announced a deal with India, claiming that New Delhi had accepted to "Buy American" at a much higher level after the U.S. agreed to slash tariffs to 18% from 50% earlier.

The India-U.S. trade deal has been pending with both sides indicating there was little room to grant concessions, while the Supreme Court annulling Trump tariffs allowed New Delhi to demand preferential tariffs that give it an edge over competitors.

"Today's announcement increases the urgency of reaching an agreement," Ronak D. Desai, visiting fellow at Hoover Institution, Stanford University, told CNBC. He added that while the prospect of a 17% tariff may strengthen the economic case for the deal, the threat of a 25% tariff could stir the sensitivity of the Indian government.

"The Modi government will be particularly sensitive to any perception that India is conceding under American Pressure," Desai said.

India's commerce ministry on Wednesday said it "does not have structural excess capacity in any sector," denying the findings of the USTR investigation, but added that it remains committed to "working closely with the United States towards the early conclusion of the India-U.S. Bilateral Trade Agreement."

Despite India facing steep tariffs, which were raised to 50%, New Delhi's trade surplus with the U.S. rose to $57.3 billion in 2025 from $45.2 billion in 2024.

Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi and Trump are likely to meet in December for the G20 leaders' meeting in Florida, and several experts believe the two leaders could seal the trade deal on the sidelines of the summit.

#2778. Planes, trains and automobiles: The best ways to beat travel fatigue—so you can come home feeling rested and fulfilled

Five-hour flights. Eleven-hour road trips. Fifteen-hour train rides. All potentially exhausting options for the annual tradition of convening around the dinner table with loved ones on Thanksgiving.

An estimated 81.5 million Americans are projected to travel, or have already traveled, at least 50 miles from their homes between Nov. 24 and Nov. 30, according to the American Automobile Association. And their mode of transportation is shifting: Fewer people are flying or driving this year compared to 2025, and more are traveling by buses, trains and cruises, AAA data shows.

High gas prices and soaring plane ticket costs may factor into the shift. As of Wednesday evening, fuel prices are roughly 17% higher than they were on the same date in 2025, according to AAA data. Meanwhile, "months of elevated jet fuel costs due to the conflict in Iran have contributed to Thanksgiving and Christmas airfares reaching 10-year highs," according to fare-tracking platform Hopper.

The alternate modes of transit may open travelers up to new experiences — and new forms of travel fatigue to keep you from returning home feeling rested and fulfilled. You're probably already familiar with the exhaustion you might get from a day of plane travel, for example. In airplanes, "there can be lower humidity, maybe lower oxygen levels at cabin pressure. Sometimes people report feeling more dehydrated or drained. You're adjusting to that change in altitude," says Alaina Tiani, a Cleveland Clinic behavioral sleep medicine psychologist.

But even waiting in places like train stations or cruise ports can feel draining due to sheer sensory overload, Tiani says. And then, depending on your mode of travel, you may still need to deal with cramped seating and limited ability to get up and walk around or drink some water and eat a snack.

Once you arrive at your destination, managing responsibilities like remembering where you put your hotel key and deciding what to eat and how to structure your day can also take a mental toll, says travel writer Shannon O'Donnell, who's written about managing travel fatigue. "All of that actually requires a lot more thinking and planning for your brain," she says, adding: "People just don't understand how those add up and why a relaxing vacation suddenly has them feeling wrecked."

The consequences can sneak up on you, O'Donnell notes. Travel fatigue can "present as maybe a lack of interest in something that you might usually find fascinating," she says. Instead of looking out the window on a plane ride or playing car games with your kids on a road trip, you might disengage and appear apathetic to the people around you.

You might stand a better chance of a fulfilling, restful holiday if you prepare for your travel fatigue. Here, Tiani and O'Donnell share their advice for how to do it:

#2777. So much for a Spurs fan to be thankful for in 2026 and beyond

My first Thanksgiving post for Pounding the Rock was on November 27, 2014, about a month after I joined the team. (The Pounding the Rock team, not the Spurs.) I didn't write about being thankful for the Redemption Finals, even thought we were all VERY thankful for, and the joy that win brought all basketball fans. "Especially in light of everything going on in the sports world, I loved to hear Rudy T's comments about the team we all root for. He used the words "good people". Among all the things to be thankful for this Thanksgiving, the list includes the ability to cheer on a Spurs team that is a group of good people. Not everyone can say that, but we can. Happy Thanksgiving, Spurs Nation."

I feel the same way about the present Spurs team. Watching the players interact with each other, the coaches and the staff, and in their interviews with the media, the Spurs continue to be "good people". The way the guys on the bench support the players in the game is truly wonderful. I remain thankful to root for a team with good people.

Things changed a lot for the Spurs between 2014 and 2019. In my short Thanksgiving post on November 28, 2019, I tried to find some things to be thankful for in a season that ultimately ended with the COVID lockdown, the Spurs at 33-39 and missing out on the Bubble Playoffs:

"At this point in the season, it may be difficult for Spurs fans to find something to be thankful for. But remember we still have: (1) the memory of 22 straight years of winning; (2) likable and exciting players during those 22 years, including the Big Three (3), who won more regular season games and more playoff games than any threesome ever; (4) the best NBA coach ever; (5) an excellent front office, and (6) being part of a passionate and knowledgeable fan base. And, of course, the Coyote. So Happy Thanksgiving, Spurs fans."

Four years later, a portion of my 2023 Thanksgiving post remains as true now as then:

"The ex-Spurs. I am very thankful that the Spurs of our (relative) youth are still around and invested in the team. It is always great to see David Robinson in the stands at a Spurs home game, except for the fan who has to sit directly behind his 7'1" self. The Big Three also seem to be around a lot. Manu Ginobili in particular, I am confident is haunting Spurs' practices teaching the youngsters how to throw long one-handed bounce passes through traffic."

I remain thankful every time they show Manu, the Admiral or the Great Duncan it attedance for games in 2026. I am sure they enjoy seeing this team carry on the Spurs tradition.

Part of that tradition is drafting well, when the Spurs have the fop pick in the draft (the Admiral and TD), late first round picks (Tony Parker), and second round picks (Manu!). But no team can match the three consecutive drafts that produced Victor Wembanyama, Stephon Castle and Dylan Harper (plus Carter Bryant as a bonus). We really need a name for those three drafts – "Big Three" is already copyrighted. Those young players were key to last season's Thanksgiving column, written in late November 2025. That was early in the season, and thus well before anyone even suspected that the young Spurs would win the Western Conference and make the NBA for the first time in over a decade. I wrote:

"The main thing Spurs fans are thankful for: we finally have a good team again."

By contrast, this season's buzz around the league, and the team, is completely different. Even without Fox for the start of the season, and Victor's recent absence, the team has remained competitive. I am very thankful to once again turn on the game and expect a Spurs win – a feeling we haven't had in years. I am also thankful to hear the pre-game commentators on the various networks describe the team in glowing terms, also something we have not been hearing for a while."

Now we are in November 2026, with Thanksgiving upon us. Time to mention some things I am thankful for, and for this year, a few things which I am not thankful for.

As mentioned above, we can push repeat on some things to be thankful for: The Spurs are good again, remain good people while winning games, and are blessed to have the ex-Spurs out supporting the team. I will add something that his year's team has that the Big Three 'teams did not — youth! When the 2014 team won it all, it came with a sense that the Big Three era was nearing its end. Even with the disappointment of the 2026 Finals, we can be thankful to have the hope that the Spurs' latest appearance in the Finals might just be the first of many.

I am also thankful that Selena Gomez is a Spurs fan! Who knew?

On the flip side, I am NOT thankful for seeing this car on my way home from the office:

I am also less than thankful about seeing this guy when I visited our local Farmers' Market:

But back to good things. I have said many times that the key thing to playing defense is caring — a team with players that really care about stopping the other team from scoring will do all the right things to make that happen. This Spurs team clearly cares about defense, and this former coach is thankful to see how hard the team defends.

I am especially thankful to being able to look at the NBA standings every morning and see San Antonio on the top line. We will all be especially thankful if the Spurs stay on that top line through the regular season and playoffs. If they do, I might just make a big Thanksgiving dinner in late June 2027 and invite all my friends. Even those who are Knick fans.

#2776. Bills’ Josh Allen makes promise to Buffalo after another brutal game in win over Chiefs

Bills' Josh Allen makes promise to Buffalo after another brutal game in win over Chiefs appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Buffalo Bills escaped Thanksgiving night with a 13-10 win over the Kansas City Chiefs, extending their winning streak to seven games and improving to 10-1. Kicker Tyler Bass rescued Buffalo in the final seconds with a 57-yard field goal, bailing out quarterback Josh Allen after one of the ugliest outings of his career.

Allen did not hide from the box score afterward. Speaking to reporters after the game, the franchise signal-caller delivered a vow to Bills Mafia regarding his recent slump. "I have to be better; I need to be better; I will be better. Thankful for my team," Allen said.

Mother Nature turned Highmark Stadium into a wet, windy slog, and Kansas City's defense made Allen pay for every mistake. Allen completed just 10 of 21 passes for a season-low 81 yards, zero touchdowns and two interceptions, posting an 18.3 passer rating. Defensive backs Alohi Gilman and Nohl Williams both picked him off inside the red zone, marking Allen's second straight game with two interceptions after doing the same against the Miami Dolphins. Chiefs cornerback Christian Roland-Wallace and linebacker Nick Bolton also combined to drop Allen for a third-down sack as Kansas City stifled Buffalo's aerial attack.

Fortunately for Allen, his supporting cast carried the load. Allen added 43 rushing yards on seven carries, while running back James Cook powered the offense with 117 yards and a 5-yard touchdown on 32 carries. Wide receiver Khalil Shakir paced Buffalo's quiet passing game with three catches for 33 yards, while Buffalo's defense held Kansas City quarterback Patrick Mahomes to 16 of 29 passing for 174 yards and zero touchdowns. That grit set up Bass for a 54-yard game-tying kick before his 57-yard walk-off winner.

At 10-1, the Bills sit atop the AFC standings. If Allen keeps his Thanksgiving promise, Buffalo will look even more dangerous down the stretch.

#2775. Fast cars, big money: Why sportswear races into Formula 1 as sponsorship spending soars 75%

The race for dominance in Formula 1 is no longer confined to the racetrack, with some of the world's biggest sportswear brands, including Nike, Adidas and Puma, vying for a piece of the action.

Sportswear sponsorship spending in F1 has surged 75% over two years and topped $200 million in 2026, according to Adam Lewis, a senior analyst at research firm Ampere Analysis.

Brands are competing for access to the sport's increasingly young and affluent fan base, while looking to capitalize on its growing influence in fashion and popular culture.

Nike gave fans a sneak preview of its F1 partnership in Las Vegas this month with a limited-edition collection sold during the Grand Prix. From 2027, Nike will supply F1 staff uniforms, a role Puma has held since 2024, and sell footwear and apparel under the championship's brand.

Other sportswear majors have taken a different approach. Adidas signed with Mercedes in 2025 and added Audi for its debut season this year, while Puma still has its longstanding deal with Ferrari, covering racewear and licensing for Ferrari-branded products.

Unlike football leagues such as the English Premier League, where sportswear brands spread their budgets across numerous clubs, F1 has just 11 teams, limiting the number of apparel partnerships up for grabs.

"F1, by contrast, is a closed market with only a handful of teams," Lewis said, adding that the scarcity of deals makes brands more willing to pay higher prices as contracts come up for renewal.

What makes F1 particularly attractive, however, is its growing influence.

"For major sportswear and lifestyle brands, F1 has grown beyond motorsport, into a powerful, year-round pop-culture and storytelling platform," Joey Khong, global consumer trends manager at market research firm Mintel, told CNBC.

F1 brings together fashion, music, luxury and celebrity culture with athletic performance and technological innovation, offering brands multiple avenues to engage with consumers, Khong said.

Team apparel has also crossed over into everyday streetwear, with social media amplifying the visibility of what drivers and team members wear off the track.

Some 34% of people who engage with F1 do so through social media, while 22% engage through online content, according to Lewis.

That constant stream of content has turned team apparel into what Lewis described as a "living billboard" for sportswear brands.

Nearly a third of F1 fans are now under the age of 35, with that share rising 18% since 2023, according to Lewis. Meanwhile, 45% of F1 fans fall into the two highest-earning socioeconomic groups, compared with 36% of the general population.

For sportswear brands, F1 offers a way to build loyalty among younger, affluent consumers.

Asia-Pacific has been a key driver of F1's fan growth, helping deliver a larger audience for its commercial partners, analysts said.

Markets across the region have gained more than 25 million F1 fans since 2023, up 36%, according to Ampere Analysis. That outpaced growth in other markets by 11 percentage points and accounted for 84% of overall fan growth.

That offers sportswear brands a growing audience to engage with in a region where F1 already races in Melbourne, Suzuka, Shanghai and Singapore.

Research also points to why that engagement could resonate. In a 2026 Mintel survey, 29% of respondents in China said sporting events would prompt them to pay attention to or try related brands, rising to 37% in households earning at least 24,000 yuan ($3,600) a month.

Fans are spending more on F1 merchandise than ever before.

F1 merchandise sales have grown by almost 200% since 2017, according to Fanatics, the sport's e-commerce partner, cited in a License Global report.

Retail sales of F1-licensed consumer products also rose 44% in 2024, one of the fastest growth rates among the sports properties tracked.

The appeal may stretch beyond race-branded gear. F1 fans were 29% more likely than the average consumer to say they planned to buy sports apparel in the next year and 27% more likely to say the same about non-sports apparel, according to Ampere's consumer research.

Mintel's Khong said F1 merchandise alone is unlikely to move the needle quickly for sports apparel giants the size of Nike or Adidas.

Instead, the bigger potential payoff could come from the partnership boosting demand across the brand's everyday product lines outside F1-specific collaborations or the sport's licensed merchandise.

"There is a route to revenue growth, but being associated with the sport is only the starting point," Khong said.

#2774. Knicks 121, Cavaliers 102: “The most Mike Brown victory imaginable”

It's easy to get lost in today's world. Everything is spinning so fast, so many things are out of control. Prices, taxes, wars, narratives, diabolical devices, our sense of togetherness. Life sometimes feels like an endless list of things that, no matter how much you scratch off, no matter what you decide not to worry about, gets longer by the minute. The NBA is no exception, a cutthroat world infected by influencing influences that expects daily reinvention, that wants to turn humans into brands and expand like a kraken gobbling up the last little dollars from the deepest pits of the coral reef.

As you watch another foul-baiting baller be rewarded for his deeds, followed by the 23rd commercial for guaranteed grifting, it can be tough to find the joy here from time to time, to remember why you ever thought this was fun. But then Jose Alvarado runs into the picture and steals a ball, only to rise up moments later for a three. He jumps for joy as he hits it, just like the little kid he once was on the playgrounds of the city that he now represents on the biggest stage there is in basketball. And you smile along with him.

There is something inherently right about a guy like Jose Alvarado. Sure, he is an annoying pest defensively, loudmouthed, confident on his good days, cocky on his better ones. A guy you would probably hate if he wasn't on your team. Not even in the fun way of hating an opponent, if there is one. No, you really can't stand that little bugger, like, at all. Yet even if you root for some other franchise whose name doesn't rhyme with clicks, deep down inside your basketball fan's heart, you have to appreciate a guy, a man, a kid like Jose Alvarado. Mike Brown surely does.

It didn't even take Alvarado's tide-turning performance in the Knicks' 121-102 destruction of the Cleveland Cavaliers in yesterday's NBA Cup game, which was, as PolyphonicSpreewell so beautifully put it in the Postgame Thread, "the most Mike Brown victory imaginable." It didn't take his 16 points in the fourth quarter or his 21 in total. But the light shining on Jose surely was a reminder that the big little Puerto Rican embodies what this New York basketball team is. Sure, they can beat you with Xs and Os, with the might of majestic superstars, with firepower that can put a New Year's fireworks display to shame. But there is more that makes the Knicks the Knicks. A group that has a heart and a soul beyond what is expected of them, some inner power they can turn to when things go sideways. Something that lets them play and, more importantly, enjoy the game innocently, freely. How it was supposed to be experienced.

Alvarado is not your type of guy? Pas de souci, says Pacôme Djenon Dadiet! It didn't matter that he didn't really hit shots consistently yesterday or, frankly, ever before. He still plunged into the action versus the James Hardens and Donovan Mitchells of the world like the Knicks' bench was a cliff and his name was Orlando Duque. Maybe not as gracefully, but definitely just as committed and energetic. And, most importantly, without any fear. There was no fear in Tyler Nickel, either, who at some point seemed as far away from real NBA minutes as his birthplace in the Shenandoah Valley is from Madison Square Garden. But don't worry, bench guy, Mike Brown's got a map, and he knows where to find you.

With injuries to OG Anunoby, Deuce McBride, and Jordan Clarkson, plus ineffective and almost shockingly bad nights from Jalen Brunson and Karl-Anthony Towns, the coach seemingly didn't have to search yesterday. He just hurled everyone he had on his pine into the action and watched them stick it to the Cavs. Mo Diawara hit a three. James Wiseman dominated the paint for stretches in the fourth quarter. He didn't look out of place; he didn't look like he didn't know what he was doing. Rather, he played like a man possessed, not by some evil power or the One Ring to rule them all, but by the unwavering confidence and belief of a bald, happy-go-lucky man who coaches champions.

These champions include a much-maligned Mikal Bridges, who carried the team throughout the entire second quarter. They also include Josh Hart and Landry Shamet, who did their thing and then some. They are all guys who had rough goes in the past but who never found themselves in Mr. Brown's doghouse. They deserve to be singled out for pushing the ball in transition, for point-of-attack defense, for just being themselves. But frankly, who doesn't? Everyone did a little and then a lot of what was needed from them. I'm sure Tyler Kolek was one of the biggest cheerleaders despite his rough night on the court. It all speaks to the culture that Mike Brown has bestowed upon this team, that he has created with all of his players. All of them. All these guys who know what hard work is. Who relish every opportunity they get.

And who have a way of playing basketball that is as much fun as you can imagine.

#2773. The Nasdaq is in freefall. Here's one stock to look at

I asked Claude after Friday's close what I should do about the tanking stock market. Its advice was surprisingly useful: avoid panic selling and look for opportunities. A 40 VIX, which it touched Friday, might provide those opportunities. Specifically, a higher VIX (higher options premiums) signals fear, but it also creates an opportunity to sell elevated premium in some premium stocks, like Amazon.

Amazon's cloud business is growing at its fastest pace in nearly five years. AWS revenue rose 39.3% YoY in the third quarter, and management reported a $578 billion backlog. Trailing 12-month operating cash flow rose 29% to $168.3 billion. However, infrastructure investment accelerated, resulting in more than $42 billion in negative cash flow. The competing forces of accelerating growth and the cost of supplying it could keep the stock in its range over the next four weeks. Its shares are only off 14% from its recent highs, comparatively better than the Nasdaq Composite's near 19% decline.

AWS demand supports the lower end of the range. The backlog gives Amazon a basis for adding capacity even as investors scrutinize the cash cost. Cash spending could temper an upside rerating.

The next leg higher may require evidence that new capacity can earn attractive returns at this scale, and that evidence may be in short supply over the next couple of months.

Sell the Dec. 18, 2026 AMZN 235/285 strangle for an indicative $5.90 credit per share.

Max reward — $590 per strangle if AMZN finishes between $235 and $285 at expiration, before fees.

Breakevens at expiration — $229.10 and $290.90.

Max risk — Unlimited on the uncovered call, but one should think of the trade as risking getting short at a net price above $290, a nearly 13% premium to today's closing price. The downside loss would reach $22,910 per strangle if AMZN fell to zero. The credit is roughly 2.3% of a $260 reference share price over four weeks, or nearly 30% on a simple annualized basis. That comparison uses the share price as its denominator. Actual return on capital depends on margin and the cost to close or settle the trade.

Buying in bear markets, as the Nasdaq just touched, is never easy. But those opportunities don't present themselves too often. What are you going to do with it?

#2772. China’s factories are growing profits again, but the AI boom is picking the winners

China's industrial profits in October grew at their fastest pace since May, supported by an AI-driven electronics boom and higher energy prices, while consumer-facing industries squeezed by weak demand continued to be a drag.

Profits at sizeable industrial firms surged 20.1% in October from a year earlier, according to data released Friday by the National Bureau of Statistics. For the first 10 months this year, the earnings grew 16% year on year, having risen 15.5% through September.

That extends a notable rebound in 2026 after three years of declines followed by a tepid 0.6% growth last year, with profits on track to log their strongest annual expansion since 2021.

Despite the sharp headline figure, "the recovery has yet to broaden into a more durable improvement in corporate profitability," said Tianchen Xu, senior economist at Economist Intelligence Unit, as the rebound was largely led by upstream sectors amid higher energy prices.

China's economy has been running at two speeds, with AI-related manufacturing and exports powering growth while household consumption has been lagging. Retail sales grew a smaller-than-expected 0.4% in October, and fixed-asset investment steepened declines to 8.1% in the first 10 months.

Profit gains were concentrated in a set of sectors tied to the global AI buildout and higher commodity prices, while consumer-facing manufacturers struggled to pass on higher input costs, squeezing margins.

Earnings in the electronics sector doubled in the first 10 months from the same period last year, as demand for AI computing power lifted memory chips and related manufacturing, said Yu Weining, chief statistician of the NBS.

Profits in high-tech manufacturing jumped 50.9%, outpacing the industry as a whole, Yu added. Makers of spacecraft and launch vehicles reported a 63.2% rise.

Commodity producers also saw sharp growth in profits as demand tied to renewable energy and AI pushed up copper and aluminum prices, Yu said. Petroleum processors swung to a combined profit from a loss a year earlier.

Showcasing an increasingly bifurcated economy, industries tied to consumers and construction recorded steepening losses. Automakers saw profits drop 26% in the first 10 months, wider than the previous month, as costs outpaced a modest revenue increase.

Producers of construction materials such as cement and glass, and furniture makers continued to suffer the fallout from a prolonged real estate slump, with profits falling 46.7% and 52.4%, respectively.

Accounts receivable, or money owed to businesses by their customers, and finished-goods inventories also grew faster than revenue, official data showed, underscoring the strain on companies across sectors.

Overall activity in October picked up "substantially" compared to the past couple of months, expanding 2.4% from a year earlier, according to Capital Economics' China Activity Proxy, a gauge that excludes official data in favor of low-profile indicators, such as freight and port traffic, that are "less politically-sensitive and so less likely to be manipulated."

The growth in October was led by strong external demand and exports, even as manufacturing activity for domestic goods languished, said Leah Fahy, senior China economist at Capital Economics.

Local governments stepped up fiscal spending for the first time since February last month, Fahy said, but that "had little impact" on arresting the property slump and reviving domestic demand.

"Local governments [are] clearly struggling to make full use of their allocated funds, [and] there is a limit to how much fiscal policy will be able to support the economy," Fahy added.

The AI-driven growth in external demand has reduced the need for Chinese policymakers to ramp up stimulus to support domestic demand and the overall economy. China's GDP growth of 4.6% in the first three quarters has put Beijing's annual growth target of 4.5-5% within reach.

As global AI-related investment starts to slow, exports would also become a "waning prop to growth," Fahy said, with overall economic expansion to decline to just 2.8% next year in her estimates, from 3.9% in 2025.

Adding to fresh headwinds on trade, the U.S. proposed new tariffs that include a 20% headline rate on Chinese goods and punitive rates on several Asian economies that analysts say have been targeted for transshipments to China. The levies would rise to 40% if the U.S.-China truce does not get extended beyond January.

"Chinese firms now export intermediate goods to the rest of Asia and reach the U.S. indirectly," said Alicia Garcia Herrero, chief economist for APAC at Natixis, adding that economies with more Chinese inputs may face higher tariff risks.

#2771. From Cooper Flagg to Ben Simmons, a look at Montverde Academy’s top NBA draft picks

Montverde Academy (FL) has established itself as one of the premier pipelines for basketball talent in the country, producing NBA stars and some of the sport's most recognizable names.

From No. 1 overall selections to first-round picks who have gone on to become franchise cornerstones, the Central Florida powerhouse has built an impressive résumé at the professional level.

With three players selected first overall in the NBA Draft, here is a look at the 10 highest NBA Draft picks to graduate from Montverde Academy.

The third Montverde Academy product to be selected No. 1 overall, Flagg was the centerpiece of the school's undefeated 2023-24 national championship team. After one season at Duke, he was selected first overall by Dallas in 2025 and won NBA Rookie of the Year in 2026. He is now emerging as the face of the Mavericks' franchise

Cunningham became Montverde's second No. 1 overall selection after leading the Eagles alongside Scottie Barnes and Moses Moody. Following one season at Oklahoma State, he was drafted first overall by Detroit. He has since become a two-time NBA All-Star and led the Pistons' resurgence, including a 60-win season in 2025-26

Simmons became Montverde's first No. 1 overall NBA Draft pick after starring for the Eagles before playing one season at LSU. He won NBA Rookie of the Year in 2018 and became a three-time All-Star with Philadelphia and Brooklyn. His NBA career has since been derailed by injuries, and he is no longer an established NBA roster player.

Russell helped lead Montverde to a national championship before playing one season at Ohio State. He was selected second overall by the Lakers and has gone on to play for several NBA franchises, including Brooklyn, Golden State, Minnesota and the Lakers.

A former five-star recruit, Barrett starred at Montverde before playing one season at Duke. He was selected third overall by the Knicks and spent four-plus seasons in New York before being traded to Toronto in 2023. He remains a key member of the Raptors.

Barnes was part of Montverde's loaded 2020-21 team alongside Cunningham and Moody. After one season at Florida State, he was selected fourth overall by Toronto. He won NBA Rookie of the Year in 2022 and has since become a two-time All-Star and franchise cornerstone.

Duren spent his final high school season at Montverde before playing one year at Memphis. Originally selected 13th overall by Charlotte, his draft rights were traded to Detroit. He has developed into one of the Pistons' key frontcourt players and earned his first NBA All-Star selection in 2026.

Queen was part of Montverde's undefeated 33-0 national championship team in 2023-24. Following one season at Maryland, he was selected 13th overall by Atlanta before being traded to New Orleans. He is now beginning his NBA career with the Pelicans.

Moody helped Montverde capture a national championship during the 2020-21 season before playing one year at Arkansas. He was selected 14th overall by Golden State and has developed into a rotation player for the Warriors. He is currently recovering from a ruptured patellar tendon suffered in March 2026.

Achiuwa graduated from Montverde Academy in 2019 before playing one season at Memphis. He was selected 20th overall by the Miami Heat in the 2020 NBA Draft. He has since played for Miami, Toronto and the New York Knicks.

#2770. Talking Points: Sunderland Labour to Drab Draw In Europe

Sunderland played out a largely forgettable 1-1 draw at the Stadium of Light at home to Polish opposition in Jagiellonia Bialystok.

In a match that had very little to write home about, this certainly wasn't the easiest article to write — hopefully we've managed to find a few things to discuss following this match…

Thursday's game really highlighted a lack of ideas in the final third.

Whether this comes from the players or the coaching staff is up for debate, but plainly and simply, something needs to change. There are no patterns of play, no clear system, and without Brian Brobbey, we look like we just don't know how to link midfield and attack.

Throughout much of this game, Enzo Le Fée, Noah Sadiki and Chris Rigg were too deep and although Le Fée and Sadiki had a couple of instances of breaking into the box and getting a ball across, it didn't happen often enough and the Lads really struggled to create anything clear cut.

The wingers were again a problem — this time Nilson Angulo and Malick Fofana.

Neither seem to want to run at their defenders and far too often turned and played backwards to a full back who was also sitting way too deep at times when we pinned the Polish side into their own box and very often ended our own attacks this way.

The most concerning thing for me was the lack of patterns of play and the fact that we look so devoid of ideas.

If you're playing with a clear style and it just isn't working in front of goal, you have something to build on. Sunderland look like they haven't practiced attacking football all season, and this needs to change if we're to achieve our goals and do something special this season.

Another game, and another mistake leading to a goal, this time from one of our most experienced players in Reinildo Mandava.

The full back has been one of our better players for the majority of the season but a weak back pass to Robin Roefs was intercepted by the Jagiellonia attacker and it was too easy for him to round the Dutchman and slot home.

There were other instances where we almost conceded from mistakes, with Reinildo being beaten too easily from a long ball at one point in the first half, Omar Alderete giving the ball away too easily and almost giving away a chance, and a mix up between Jules Ahoka and Tho,as Meunier in the second half leading to another chance for the visiting side.

Régis Le Bris needs to do something to cut these mistakes out — they weren't there last season so why are they this time round? It feels like almost every goal we concede is avoidable, which makes results and performances like this all the more frustrating.

Yes, he came off the back of an injury at the World Cup and a lack of a pre-season, but it's now November and the mistakes are still there, he looks a yard off the pace, and seems to come off injured in every game.

He clearly needs a prolonged spell out of the side, and hopefully Nordi Mukiele and O'Nien can stay fit long enough to allow that to happen — at least until Dan Ballard is back fit and playing again.

I think that'll make a huge difference to us defensively, and may actually result in being able to sacrifice a body in defence and throw an extra player forward at times, safe in the knowledge that Big Dan is there to cover off any threat from the opposition.

Our Flying Dutchman had a shaky start to the season, but now looks much more like himself when facing shots. A number of good saves kept us in the game against Jagiellonia and this has been the case for more and more games recently.

We need Roefs on top form if we are to achieve our goals this season and continue to progress in Europe as well as climb the Premier League table.

Roefs' distribution still leaves a bit to be desired at times, although it'a difficult when you're playing long balls up to Fofana, Timur Tutierov and Wilson Isidor all game, none of whom are known for their aerial prowess.

A quick kick in the first half almost saw Le Fée break away, but we didn't try this again once after this. The pace of distribution is becoming a bit of a bone of contention and there were more than a few murmurs of frustration in my area of the ground on Thursday night at the delays in getting the ball from defence to attack.

All in all a very disappointing night and a disappointing result in a season which promised so much. Something needs to change and Sunderland need to get back on track.

Hopefully we can return to our decent Premier League form from the last couple of games against Spurs on Sunday, and a positive performance and result would be most welcome after Thursday's display.
