#2769. Questions & Antlers: Early season impressions, featuring Nets Daily’s Anthony Puccio (Part 2)

Last week, we introduced you to Nets Daily's Editor-in-Chief, Anthony Puccio, with the premise that he, as a long-time Brooklyn Nets fan and writer, knows what we're going through this season following our beloved Milwaukee Bucks. In response, you came screaming off a high pick and roll, turned the corner—drawing the strongside defender—and hit him seams up in the corner with your questions. As you'll see below, he didn't let you down with the finish. Nylon.

stoneAge: Did you expect the rebuild to take so long and how far away are the Nets from being a playoff team again?

AP: I expected it to take long, but I figured they would have moved some assets to speed this thing up. I was wrong. We're looking at a Nets team currently two-three years away from playoffs—and that's being generous.

stoneAge: Which of the Nets' current players are part of their future core and what do the Nets lack the most to reach the next level?

AP: Mikel Brown Jr., Egor Dëmin, and Nolan Traore. The Nets lack a lot of things, particularly size in the front court. This isn't new. They lack athleticism. They lack three-point shooters. And they lack basic fundamentals required to build on good habits for a sustainable culture.

Mitchell Maurer: How do you navigate a landscape where so many people in the same geographic area are head-over-heels for the Knicks? A title is (as we've seen around here…) a surefire way to attract bandwagon fans, who are as vocal as they are anonymous 'round these Internet parts. The Nets are not a "nothing" franchise but they clearly don't carry the same weight (broadly speaking) as their cross-town counterparts. It's not a fair paradigm, to be considered an "afterthought" and I'm curious if there's similarities with how Bucks fans may have felt back in the heyday of the Packers and/or Brewers (who also just won a title!)

AP: Nets fans don't want to admit it, but there's absolutely an inferiority. Before the Knicks' chip, the one thing Nets fans had on Knicks fans was a better on-court product since 2000. Now we don't even have that! Put it this way: if you tell someone you're a Nets fan in NY, depending on who you tell, they might say, "Who?" Or "Why?"

Terrapin Revisited: What was it like to adjust expectations when the Nets went from title contenders to "Well, what exactly are we now?" after their big trades? Any recommendations for Bucks fans on how to adapt?

AP: There's no way of sugar-coating it—it feels like a dead end. You adapt when you simply learn to accept this is the new reality, and you never know when it might return. My recommendation: lean on the community to talk. It's difficult without picks to look forward to. Expect the worst, hope the best. Don't turn on each other though. (Editor's note: Sage advice—and one of the things I love about Brew Hoop).

Terrapin Revisited: What have you enjoyed most about the Nets during this rebuilding era? In other words, what can fans focus on as far as the bright side of such teams?

AP: Watching people in the organization make a name for themselves, hustling and shining in the shadows of the down years. Ideally it a player, but it can be any personnel. College basketball, too.

Terrapin Revisited: Do you have any insights on what to expect from veterans on the team at this time? A number of Bucks' vets are disappointing this year, I'm not sure how much that is to be expected when the stakes they are playing for are a lot lower vs. how much that provides some useful insights on the vets. What have you seen from different vets on the Nets?

AP: Right now I see two vets on the Nets who want to get their shots up and head out. It feels like they embrace the leadership role until the losses actually pile up.

MILWAUKEE, WISCONSIN – OCTOBER 14: Bogoljub Markovic #23 of the Milwaukee Bucks looks on against the Chicago Bulls during the preseason NBA game at Fiserv Forum on October 14, 2026 in Milwaukee, Wisconsin. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Patrick McDermott/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Terrapin Revisited: What recommendations can you make on how to watch the development of young players on teams like the Nets and Bucks? What might we watch for—and what might we need to set aside—in order to more effectively watch at present, and appreciate what we have?

AP: It's all a matter of expectations and perspective. Seriously. Watch for where guys say they're working on their games.

Pono: Based on the Nets' rebuilding experience, how should the Bucks navigate developing young players with potential and very young players with potential? For context, last season Ryan Rollins emerged as the Bucks' top young player but this season the full-on rebuild approach has altered his role and, early on, stunted his growth.

AP: This is where the G League needs to become a necessity. Get those very young players burn instead of rotting on bench, trying to force their games. Let them have some time dominating at the lower level, or learning, getting stronger, and ready for when the time comes.

MILWAUKEE, WI – JUNE 25: The Milwaukee Bucks head coach Taylor Jenkins and General Manager Jon Horst pose for a photo with the 2026 draft picks Brayden Burries and Nate Ament during the Bucks Draft Press Conference on June 25, 2026 at the Pieper-Hillside Boys & Girls Club in Milwaukee, Wisconsin. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this Photograph, user is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2026 NBAE (Photo by Gary Dineen/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

BarryDort: What do you think of the new NBA draft lottery? It seems like it puts more bad teams at the mercy of the ping pong balls. Unless you can somehow get a top five pick, it's really hard to find difference makers as you know from the Nets' last three drafts.

AP: Honestly, it doesn't feel like a long term solution to stop tanking.

Nate O.: With six first-round picks the last two drafts and 11 through 2033, it feels like the Nets are a great example of how to stack assets. I'm curious if you feel like this is a position of strength to be operating from, and only good things can unfold, or are there any other moves you wish the Nets had made or concerns you have moving forward?

AP: Concerns. They have eight players on rookie contracts, and getting them all necessary burn to develop has been extremely difficult. Taking five picks in one round felt a little silly at the time, and it's simply a handful for the coaching staff.

Southern Marxist: The Bucks front office has had to totally shift gears from spending any unused asset in an effort to get Giannis a second championship to accumulating and developing talent. Based on your observations of the Nets front office, what advice would you give the Bucks front office?

AP: Don't wait too long and be as transparent as possible. Fans know things aren't easy right now; I think they'd prefer to know what the direction is instead of paying for a product that's headed nowhere—or looks that way. The Nets keep their fans in the dark related to a lot of things here, and it comes across as disrespectful to paying customers.

I can't tell the experts how to do their job but at least communicate with fans. It's such a dramatic dropoff for the base of the brand—the face of it to—are gone. Reassure people that you can get back and how.

And just like that, our second instalment of Questions & Antlers for the season is in the books. A massive shoutout to Anthony for sharing his wisdom on the Nets and what their rebuilding experience might mean for our Bucks fandom.

For more from Anthony, be sure to check out his work at Nets Daily and follow him on X (@APooch). And for more Questions & Antlers, check back in around the halfway point of the season. Until then, there's plenty of ball to watch—and plenty of coverage to come.

When you play the new-look Miami Heat, it's not difficult to tell who the head of the snake is. Just like playing the Milwaukee Bucks over the years, the Philadelphia 76ers and the rest of the league have to pay extra attention to Giannis Antetokounmpo when facing the Heat.

At this stage of his career, Antetokounmpo is still very much dominant when he's able to stay healthy and on the court.

While the Heat did have an injury scare with him recently, considering giving him the night off in South Philly on Wednesday, Miami ultimately cleared the Greek Freak for action.

Without Joel Embiid, the 76ers had their hands tied. There isn't a single backup big man the 76ers can throw at Giannis to play against for 30-plus minutes. It takes a group effort, which LeBron James loved to see.

"You only can do so much versus Giannis," James told reporters.

"Obviously, he's gonna attack you throughout wherever minutes that he plays. He's gonna attack you downhill, but we just try to keep bodies in front of him, give him extra bodies, give him extra help."

The help included Jabari Walker, Dean Wade, and Dominick Barlow.

"I think [Jabari Walker] did a great job," James said. "I thought Dean did a great job. Obviously, you know [Dominick Barlow] did a great job as well. We just all try to be like help behind them, communicating, loading the ball, a couple of bodies not just the one that was guarding him."

James then went on to praise Giannis Antetokounmpo beyond his Wednesday performance.

"Giannis is great. He's going to do what he does, you can never stop a great player, but hopefully, you can keep him off balance every now and then, and make it a little tough on them. I thought we did a great job there."

Antetokounmpo checked in for 36 minutes in South Philly on Wednesday. He had a solid scoring outing, posting 32 points. While the veteran forward posted a double-double and had a strong performance, the 76ers did enough to keep a strong enough lead to take down the Heat 111-104.

#2767. Nvidia chip buyers told to rule out Chinese renters to purchase GPUs, sources say

Neoclouds are being told they won't be able to purchase Nvidia chips if any of the computing capacity will be rented by Chinese companies, sources told CNBC.

It comes after a new rule restricting remote access to artificial-intelligence semiconductors was announced by the Trump administration on Nov. 16.

Chip sellers communicated the messaging to neoclouds after the announcement, which seeks to close an export controls loophole allowing Chinese companies to rent access to Nvidia's most advanced chips when hosted in data centers overseas, two people familiar with the situation told CNBC. The people asked not to be named when discussing private information.

The interim final rule was announced by the Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, and came into effect on Nov. 19. Previously imposed export controls have effectively banned the sale of the most advanced Nvidia chips to Chinese companies and their import into China.

Prior to the new rule's announcement, Nvidia had said it would not sell its chips to customers renting more than 50% of the graphics processing units, or GPUs, to Chinese companies, CNBC reported.

The new rule impacts the full supply chain, from the companies selling Nvidia chips to the neoclouds who rent access to computing power. Neocloud stocks have come under renewed pressure lately as investors questioned the sustainability of the AI boom.

Nvidia and the U.S. Department of Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The change has prompted Nvidia chip sellers to look for new buyers of the semiconductor giant's GPUs, CNBC reported on Wednesday, citing three people familiar with the matter. Two of those people said it appeared chip sellers were seeking to offload GPUs because they were originally for use in data centers through which Chinese companies would rent access.

U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Wednesday that the new BIS rule was intended to close "loopholes."

"Some rules were issued by the Commerce Department last week to close off indirect access, either smuggling through places like Southeast Asia or remote access from data centers in third countries," he said.

U.S. lawmakers have been looking to clamp down on China's access to advanced compute.

Separately, Nvidia warned its customers that the proposed Remote Access Security Act — which has passed the House of Representatives but is yet to pass the Senate — would give the U.S. government broader authority to control access to cloud services, potentially exposing both parties to "legal action," sources previously told CNBC.

#2766. Mike Tomlin on current Steelers situation: "It's a dumpster fire"

Many folks in and around football have had opinions about the recent brouhaha between former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers and Steelers coach Mike McCarthy. Few have the insight and perspective that former Steelers coach Mike Tomlin brings to the table.

Appearing on NBC's Chiefs-Bills halftime show on Thanksgiving night, Tomlin shared his thoughts on the situation in a conversation with Mike Tirico. (A portion of it was on the live broadcast; the full interview is here.)

"I had a range of emotions watching those course of events yesterday," Tomlin told Tirico. "I think the prevailing one is just general sadness. Sad on a micro level, sad on a macro level. And by that, I mean, you know, just from an individual perspective, sad that we might be looking at the end of a legendary career of the quarterback, Aaron Rodgers, and just on the macro level, whether it's organizationally or for the game of football at large, to see laundry aired out that way in a public forum.

"Those are things that kind of run counterculture to professional football. So, just a lot of emotions, but probably more than anything, man, general sadness on a lot of levels."

Tirico asked Tomlin if he was shocked by Rodgers's decision to air dirty laundry in a public forum.

"It certainly did," Tomlin said, "but, you know, and particularly because I know both men involved, certainly had a lot of intimate work and development of relationship with Aaron over the last couple of years, and have known and competed against Mike, you know, respected and appreciated him as a coach for a long time. But I don't — what I acknowledge is I don't know them as a collective, the nature of their relationships, and so certainly I was surprised by that."

Regardless of Rodgers's conduct on the way out the door, Tomlin explained that Rodgers was viewed favorably by his teammates in 2025.

"He was beloved, and I'm not, you know, over-exaggerating that," Tomlin said. "He worked at it, and people appreciated his openness to getting to know and understand the Steeler culture, to be engaged with teammates, and particularly from my perspective, I worked with a lot of veteran players over the years and at times they lose a little patience for the process, or they lose patience for relationships with younger guys, et cetera. Man, he seemed to embrace all of that and really embrace it in a real, natural way. He was a tireless worker regarding the process.

"I called early-morning quarterback meetings on Wednesday and Thursday. He was always early and engaged. Did a heck of a job in terms of engaging and developing, assisting in the development of young players. Specifically, Will Howard was always in that meeting, and, shoot, man, [Rodgers] and I were shoulder-to-shoulder in terms of the development of Will and challenging Will to develop professional habits. And so, you know, it was early and extremely consistent during my time with him, in terms of his willingness to work in those areas, and I think that's why he's so beloved in that locker room, as been reported by multiple sources in light of this incident. That is real. The guys in that locker room love and appreciate Aaron Rodgers."

Had Tomlin gotten any sense that things were so close to breaking down?

"Absolutely none, but it's a really specific reason why, to be honest with you, man," Tomlin said. "One of my cultural mantras, when I worked there for the 19 years, is don't talk to people about your problems unless they can be a component of the solution. Because if you do, it's counterproductive, and people know where I stand there. And by that I mean if you have a problem with your playing time, don't talk to your teammate about it. Talk to somebody that can do something about it. That's problem solving.

"And so everybody understands my posture on some of that stuff, and so although I have really good, fluid communication with many of those guys, particularly, the more veteran guys, they know me and know me well enough to know that I didn't want to hear about any potential issues, because I'm not a component of the solution. And so although, particularly, like, Cam Heyward, he and I spent a little time together doing his bye week. Man, we had no mention of anything bubbling under the surface, if you will."

Can McCarthy effectively lead the team, given what Rodgers said?

"You know, it certainly created some challenges and challenges is probably an understatement if all of those things are true," Tomlin told Tirico. "And certainly the '1-2-3 Packers' is, because he acknowledged that. But his ability to overcome that or inability to overcome that in my opinion is based on relationships and the depth of those relationships and how those guys feel about him, or how those guys buy into what he's selling. And so, certainly, he's been there a short period of time. And so it makes you wonder, the level of that challenge. But it'll be relationship-based, his ability to overcome the things that you mentioned."

How does Tomlin think the Steelers will fare against the Broncos on Friday?

"Based on my knowledge of the men, particularly the leaders, the longstanding guys, they'll do a great job of getting hyper-focused on what really matters, and that's that football game," Tomlin said. "They'll use it as a mantra to bring them together. I expect those guys to fight. Now, fight and quality play are two different things. I'm certainly not gonna expect those guys not to fight. I think they're gonna fight and fight extremely hard. What I'm interested in is quality of play, quality of execution. And those are things that could be impacted by the level of distraction that they had on a short week in particular."

Tirico then asked Tomlin how he feels, overall, about what has transpired this week.

"I'm upset about it, you know, it's a dumpster fire," Tomlin said. "And I don't say that in a judgmental way. I presided over a few dumpster fires in my 19 years. But it certainly is upsetting, and I'm sure Steeler Nation is upset."

If owner Art Rooney II calls Tomlin for advice, what would Tomlin say?

"Hey, Mike, that's a hypothetical, because I know he wouldn't and, and because I know how he's trained," Tomlin said. "You know, Art is a guy that's not a hedge fund guy that bought a football team. He's not a, you know, artificial intelligence guy that bought a football team. This is a family business. It's generational. Any advice or thought process in that way, I imagine he just says, 'What would my dad do?' Or, 'What would my grandfather do?' Their mode of operation is time tested, it's generational. And so, I doubt, very, very seriously, that he called anyone for outside advice."

Finally, Tirico asked whether Tomlin believes Rodgers will get another NFL opportunity this season, if he wants one.

"There's certainly a possibility for that," Tomlin said. "And I just say that based on the 19 years that I held the position that I held. It's a 32-team league. All it takes is one. Over time, I've laughed and watched that people talk about, you know, 'No, that could never happen.' In a 32-team league, when everybody is highly competitive and there's some real challenges, particularly as you get to this portion of the journey from a player availability standpoint, there's certainly a possibility if he desires that he could play again, because all it takes is one suitor."

Having cleared waivers, Rodgers is available to that one suitor, if/when that one suitor calls. Still, that one suitor will need to be willing to accept the risk that, if Rodgers doesn't have things go the way he wants, he'll choose to do again the same thing he did this week.

#2765. U.S. tariff-hike threats pile pressure on partners to sign trade deals

The Trump administration has unveiled a fresh tariff salvo on more than a dozen trading partners, with China, Vietnam, and India facing some of the steepest potential rates and mounting pressure to conclude trade deals with Washington.

The proposed tariffs, framed as curbing excess industrial capacity, start at 15% for the European Union, South Korea, Switzerland and Taiwan and rise to as much as 35% for Vietnam.

The U.S. Trade Representative's Office said it intends to cap the new duties at lower levels for countries that have completed trade deals or are still negotiating with Washington.

The overall impact on the region is likely to be limited, said Mark Williams, chief Asia economist at Capital Economics. The proposal "puts most of the targeted countries back where they were earlier in the year," he said, after the Supreme Court struck down Trump's emergency tariffs in February.

Still, the proposal could give some countries an incentive to speed up trade talks in order to lock in lower tariff rates, analysts said.

On Wednesday, U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer said that the new policy likely will not take effect for months, with a senior U.S. official saying the duties could take effect as early as January.

China faces a capped rate of 20%, on top of an existing 25% tariff imposed during President Donald Trump's first term. The rate could rise to 40% if the trade truce reached in South Korea last October is not extended beyond January, the USTR said.

Beijing has rejected Washington's claim of excess capacity while vowing to take measures necessary to protect its rights.

On Thursday, Chinese Commerce Ministry spokesperson He Yadong called on Washington to "rectify its wrongdoing and return to the right track of resolving issues through dialogue."

He was responding to Washington's plan to raise tariffs following its Section 301 investigations, while the ministry also urged the U.S. to implement $30 billion of reciprocal tariff cuts agreed by the two sides in late September.

The renewed tensions come despite several conciliatory meetings between Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping this year, as well as what Williams described as Trump's "apparent lack of concern" about allegations that Chinese technology firms have circumvented U.S. chip export controls.

The two leaders are expected to meet for a fourth time this year at the Group of 20 leaders summit in Miami in December.

"While not happy, Beijing seems prepared to live with the new rates provided that they don't exceed 20%," said Wendy Cutler, senior vice president at the Asia Society Policy Institute.

By retaining the option to raise levies if bilateral talks deteriorate, Washington is using the tariffs as leverage to "secure expected shipments of critical minerals and rare earths, and further purchases of U.S. agricultural goods," Cutler said.

Washington has warned that tariffs on Chinese goods could double to 40% if the USTR finds that Beijing is not engaging. "If the trade war did start up again, today's measures provide the U.S. with some ammunition," Williams said.

Even the sweeping actions against the other 13 trading partners appear aimed squarely at China, said Williams, who tallied that the word "China" appears 253 times in the USTR's report on Vietnam, and 315 times in the document on the European Union.

"Foremost among the 'unreasonable acts, policies, and practices' that these countries are criticised for is being slow to restrict imports and FDI from China," Williams said.

While the proposed tariffs generally lift average rates only modestly, India and Vietnam stand out because of the wider gaps between the rates they would face with and without a deal, analysts said.

India and Vietnam face proposed tariffs of 25% and 35%, respectively, but those rates could fall sharply to 17% and 20% if ongoing talks with the U.S. produce reciprocal trade agreements.

The two countries have "a strong incentive" to conclude deals with Washington to secure the lower rates, Cutler said.

Vietnam's trade ministry said Thursday that it was working with the U.S. to complete a trade deal as soon as possible, according to Reuters. India could also gain an edge over some Asian rivals through comparatively lower levies if New Delhi concludes its long-pending trade deal with Washington.

Singapore faces a similar challenge. The proposal sets a maximum potential rate of 20% on its exports, net of existing Section 301 tariffs, which could fall to 10% if it reaches an agreement on reciprocal trade with the U.S. or concludes other negotiations addressing the USTR's findings, according to Singapore's Ministry of Energy, Trade and Industry.

The ministry said it disagreed with the findings and was "very disappointed" with the proposed action, arguing that it failed to account for the structure of Singapore's economy.

Speaking to reporters, Deputy Prime Minister Gan Kim Yong said that about 33%, or 9.4 billion Singapore dollars ($7.2 billion) of direct exports will be affected, and the effective tariff rate on affected Singapore exports will rise to 10.3% from the current 7.8%, should Washington impose the highest levy of 20%. "I'm also very surprised that this has happened because this is not something that you would expect from an important major trading partner," Gan added, saying "We have been working together for many years, and we have an FTA with the U.S.," Gan said, referring to the U.S.-Singapore Free Trade Agreement that was in force since 2004.

Under the FTA, Singapore imposes zero tariffs on U.S. goods, while the U.S. has run a trade surplus with the city-state for more than 20 years, the ministry said. That surplus grew to $33 billion in 2025 from $27 billion in 2024.

Singapore said it would submit further comments during the public consultation period and urge the USTR to reconsider.

Exemptions for semiconductors and other electronics should cushion the impact on South Korea and Taiwan, whose economies are heavily concentrated in those sectors, analysts said.

The USTR singled out South Korea for its concentration in memory-chip production and what it described as outsized government support. Yet it excluded the country's chips from the proposed excess-capacity tariffs.

Taiwan, which faces an excess-capacity tariff cap of 15%, also benefits from exemptions for other electronics, including mobile phones and networking equipment. Its effective tariff rate would rise to about 9% from 6%, Williams estimated.

Switzerland was hit with a shock 39% tariff rate back in 2025, targeting significant exports, from chemicals and pharmaceuticals to gold, watches and chocolates. In November 2025, it negotiated a deal that slashed U.S. duties to 15% in return for a commitment to invest billions and increase manufacturing Stateside.

In March 2026, the U.S. launched further Section 301 investigations into Switzerland over "unfair or discriminatory trade practices," landing on a recommendation to impose an additional 15% tariff.

Switzerland's Economy Ministry told CNBC that the country rejected the allegations raised during the investigations and said it had already presented its arguments during bilateral consultations with the U.S. Treasury.

The ministry added that it assumed the latest recommendation was consistent with the deal struck in November 2025 and said it was in close contact with U.S. authorities as it reviews next steps.

Officials for both Switzerland and the European Union said during trade discussions last year that while they had a trade surplus with Washington in goods, the relationship was balanced when accounting for both trade and services, where the U.S. dominates in areas such as technology and finance.

Brussels said it was assessing the U.S. report but rejected its finding that Europe was a source of industrial overcapacity.

"To the contrary, Europe is among those most exposed to overcapacity," European Commission Spokesperson Olof Gill said in a statement.

"The EU and the U.S. share a real concern about global overcapacity, much of it state-driven and concentrated in third countries. We regret the lack of joint action," Gill said.

The EU expects any measure taken from the investigation to respect the existing joint agreement of a 15% all-inclusive ceiling on EU goods, Gill added.

EU trade ministers are due to speak with U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer on Monday.

Citi analysts noted they expected a hit on European retailers if the measures stay as announced, with the likes of Adidas, H&M, Puma and Primark potentially seeing downgrades on full-year 2027 earnings.

Inga Fechner, senior economist for global trade at ING, said in a Wednesday note that the U.S. "tariff wall keeps changing, but it isn't coming down," adding that "U.S. protectionism is becoming increasingly difficult to unwind."

While the newly Democrat-controlled Congress could try to restrict presidential tariff authority, remove individual duties or apply greater scrutiny via hearings and investigations, actually reversing the measures will be difficult while Trump retains veto power, she said.

"We don't think the blue wave will mark a return to free trade. The Biden years already showed that tariffs in strategically important sectors enjoy support across political camps."

Protectionist moves become more targeted, she added, adding to a complex system of country- and sector-specific duties based on different legal authorities.

— CNBC's Evelyn Cheng contributed to this report.

The Golden State Warriors visit the Moda Center on Friday to take on the Portland Trail Blazers in the final group stage game of the 2026 NBA Cup.

The Warriors will need two things to advance to the knockout stage. They have to beat the Blazers and the Los Angeles Lakers to defeat the San Antonio Spurs.

On Thursday, the Warriors announced their injury report for the NBA Cup matchup against the Blazers.

Moses Moody – Out (Left Patellar Tendon Surgery)

Friday's game is scheduled for 9:30 p.m. EST. It will be streamed live on NBA League Pass.

GettyStephen Curry #30 of the Golden State Warriors reacts against the Memphis Grizzlies at Chase Center on October 23, 2026 in San Francisco, California.

Stephen Curry remains out with a right oblique injury suffered against the Houston Rockets on November 12. Curry will be re-evaluated on Friday, ahead of their game against the Portland Trail Blazers.

The two-time MVP is expected to be out for longer, but it seems like his recovery is going in the right direction. ClutchPoints' Brett Siegel predicted two more weeks will be added to Curry's return timeline.

In place of Curry, De'Anthony Melton is expected to start at point guard alongside Brandin Podziemski.

Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody continue their recovery from their respective knee surgeries from earlier this year. Butler and Moody are both expected to play later this season, with Butler likely returning first.

As for Kristaps Porzingis, he is still away from the team as he takes a break from basketball. He is not expected to be back with the Warriors or ever suit up for the franchise again.

The Warriors and Porzingis' representatives are working hard for a resolution, which is likely to be a trade before or at the deadline. Porzingis is reportedly still dealing with symptoms of postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome, or POTS.

GettyDamian Lillard #0 of the Portland Trail Blazers is guarded by De'Anthony Melton #8 of the Golden State Warriors during the first half of their preseason game at Chase Center on October 16, 2026 in San Francisco, California.

The Warriors and Blazers have a similar NBA Cup record of 2-1. They are in a four-way tie with the Los Angeles Lakers and San Antonio Spurs.

However, the Spurs are atop the West Group C standings because of their superior point differential. The Blazers are in second, followed by the Warriors and Lakers, respectively.

The Warriors can still top the group if they beat the Blazers, and the Lakers beat the Spurs. Golden State has a tie-breaker against the Lakers, so they are on top even if they both finish the group stage 3-1.

But what if the Spurs beat the Lakers instead? The Warriors will still have a chance to advance, but they have to beat the Blazers by double digits to possibly have the best record and point differential among second-place teams in the West.

#2763. Knicks Bulletin: ‘We haven’t arrived’

In the least surprising development in the NBA on Wednesday, the Knicks beat the Cavaliers.

Here are very few quotes from yesterday's affair and elsewhere. Enjoy Thanksgiving today, and make sure to save the leftovers!

On the benefits of potentially missing the NBA Cup knockout rounds:

"It is important (to win and potentially advance), but the reality of it is, when you look at the schedule, and you see that our percentage [to advance] is not that high, and so they kind of shared with me what our schedule would look like if we don't go and if we do go. And you look at it, you're like, 'Whoa, that's a lot of days off if you don't go.' And so again, you want to compete; you want to win. But if you don't, in my opinion, when you look at the schedule… you're like, there's an opportunity if you got some guys banged up to get rest and/or to work on some things in between the games that you have if you're not involved in the Cup."

On the physical and mental toll of competing in the NBA Cup:

"I didn't really break it down to an 83rd game, but when I talk about the Cup, it adds a level of stress maybe on your mind and your body because the games are intense as you go along and you're playing more games in a short amount of time.

"I remember it was like, game, game, game, and then you win the Cup, and you celebrate it that night, and you hop on a plane and go play in Indiana the next night. And so it's great. You like the competition and the setup, but it adds a lot of stress to you physically and mentally. I'm saying this because especially with the extended season we had last year and how taxing the games were for our playoff run mentally and physically, I'm trying to be smart, so if — knock on wood — we end up advancing, I'll try to be smart about it."

On refusing to chase regular-season wins at all costs and learning from Golden State's 73-win season:

"I try not to chase wins, especially early in the season. You want to win, but I'm not going to win every game at all costs.

"I wasn't part of the staff [for the first Golden State title] [Golden State] won Steve [Kerr's] first year. Then they lost in the second year, but they set a regular season record of 73 wins, and one of the things that he used to tell me, they gassed themselves trying to get this record and come playoff time they just didn't have enough in the tank to close it. And so that always sits right here for me."

On calling Pacôme Dadiet into his office and threatening to remove him from the rotation over his lack of effort:

"I didn't think he played hard in the game before. And I actually called him into my office and showed him some film. I told him, 'As a young guy, if you don't play hard, I can't play you.'

"And I said, 'Believe me, I got a lot of guys I can go to. I'm not afraid to go to somebody else. So the least you could do is play hard.' And I showed him how he wasn't. And I thought [Wednesday], he played hard."

On leaving OG Anunoby's injury management to the medical staff:

"There are a lot of medical terms that are above my head. They tell me and I don't really pick up on the injury. If he's out, I change my minutes sheet. And then I'll just wait for them to tell me when he's back. [Wednesday], OG's individual work happened before shootaround and I didn't even know he had individual work until his player development guy told me later on. You never want a guy to hurt himself more. But I don't think about it."

On the Knicks still having plenty of room for improvement despite winning 12 of their last 15 games:

"We're 12-3 since the start, but our s–t still does stink. We haven't arrived, and we still have to embrace the little things."

On Brown experimenting with an unusually deep rotation against Cleveland:

"I looked at the bench in the first quarter and realized, 'Wow, we played like 10 people in that first quarter.'"

On endorsing Tom Thibodeau as a potential head coaching hire for struggling NBA franchises:

"I made three playoffs with Thibodeau as my coach so his track record is one of success. So of course if I was a team that wanted to get back on track, I'd look at Tom Thibodeau to be my coach."

On Thibodeau's tactical knowledge, dedication and ability to prepare his teams to win:

"It's the X's and O's and the amount of care he puts into his craft, his team. He steps into that gym every day and, you know, you can say anything you want about the way he coaches, but one thing I know is his team is prepared to win a game. So that's a testament to who he is as a coach, the preparation he puts his team through and also his ability to have his team ready for the moment."

#2762. German software major SAP weighs cutting at least 10,000 jobs in AI push: Bloomberg

SAP is preparing a plan to eliminate at least 10,000 positions as the German firm reshapes its software business for the artificial intelligence era, Bloomberg reported on Thursday, citing people familiar with the matter.

The number could change after labor negotiations. SAP did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

Other software companies have also tied workforce reductions to AI initiatives, though for different reasons. WiseTech Global said in February it planned to cut about 2,000 jobs over two years as it integrated AI into its products and operations.

Atlassian said in March it would cut about 1,600 jobs partly to fund further AI investment, while Cloudflare said in May it would eliminate more than 1,100 roles as it moved toward an AI-first operating model.

In the U.S., employers cited AI in 121,401 job-cut announcements through October 2026, accounting for about 20% of the total, according to a Nov. 5 report from outplacement and executive coaching firm Challenger, Gray & Christmas.

SAP employs more than 110,000 people worldwide. Bloomberg reported that all departments had been asked to identify potential cuts, with product, engineering and sales in particular focus.

The company also underwent a reorganization in 2024, when it eliminated around 10,000 positions through a program that relied mainly on voluntary departures and retraining.

Its headcount later remained relatively flat as it hired for new roles, Bloomberg reported.

In 2023, SAP CEO Christian Klein told CNBC he did not necessarily expect a reduction in the workforce, saying he expected the company to grow and "accelerate" its total revenue.

The reported cuts come as Klein pushes SAP toward an AI-first strategy. This year, SAP completed four acquisitions — Reltio, Dremio, Prior Labs and TechWolf — and made strategic investments in AI-focused companies including Parloa, n8n and Conduct.

The acquisitions are intended to help SAP organize company data, develop AI models for business information and analyze workforce skills.

#2761. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2760. Eagles vs. Cowboys Thanksgiving: The good, the bad, and the ugly

Nick Sirianni had to be redirected away from the stands more than a few times by the Eagles' media relations team at AT&T Stadium. He tried to high five everyone wearing midnight green Thursday afternoon. The Eagles were dead two times, three times, 10 times, it seemed, and they managed to stage the largest Thanksgiving Day comeback in NFL history, overcoming a 21-point deficit (31–10) to beat the Dallas Cowboys, 39-38, in one of the most improbable victories games in Eagles' memory.

It sets the Eagles up to now become NFC East contenders, considered where the Eagles were two weeks ago.

The Eagles never gave up. They were banged up, without Cooper DeJean, Dallas Goedert, relying on Ty Robinson's blocked punt, and pivotal nine-yard sack that turned the ball over and set up Jake Elliott's game-winning 59-yard field goal with 2 seconds left.

The pickup from the Cowboys' practice squad hauled the Eagles from a 31-10 deficit to complete 21 of 31 passes for 331 yards and three touchdowns being named MVP of the game.

DeVonta Smith was amazing in a career-high game, catching a career-high 11 passes for a career-high 200 yards on 14 targets.

It is a signature victory in Sirianni's six years.

The Eagles are now 4-7 with the lowly Arizona Cardinals and Indianapolis Colts next, two teams with a combined 6-16 record, which could bring the Eagles to 6-7 record with Seattle coming to Lincoln Financial Field on Dec. 19.

The Eagles are still at the bottom of the NFC East, but in a position to push the 6-3-1 New York Giants, and the 4-5-1 Washington Commanders.

The Eagles' 20 fourth-quarter points exceeded the 17-points per game the Eagles were averaging this season.

The Eagles continued playing hard for Sirianni.

He still made dubious choices, like the decision to go for a two-point conversion after Milton's 15-yard TD run with 9:09 left to play, and the Eagles trailing 38-30.

The Eagles were down at one point 31-10 with 3:35 left in the third quarter, then proceeded to score on four of the next five drives.

There was a lot of good, some bad and some ugly in the Eagles' 39-38 win over the Dallas Cowboys.

Joe Milton exploding on national TV in completing 21 of 31 passes for 331 yards and three touchdowns. He just had a habit of coming up in clutch situations to make big plays. He probably made himself a pile of money from some NFL team, too, whether the Eagles are in his future or somewhere else. Wherever he goes, no Eagles' fan will forget what he did on Thanksgiving Day 2026 against the Dallas Cowboys, bringing a dead team back to life.

Ty Robinson's blocked punt and Kenny Moore's 3-yard scoop and score suddenly made what looked like a blowout into a game. Robinson came slicing through the line and dove at Dallas punter Bryan Anger to catch the punt with his right hand, and Moore ran after the loose ball and gathered it in for a three-yard score. Robinson later came up with a huge nine-yard sack at the Eagles' 48 with 55 seconds left, setting up the Elliott game-winner.

Jake Elliott coming through big again. He was 2-for-2 on a 48- and 59-yard game-winning field goal. This came after Elliott went 4-for-4 the previous week against Pittsburgh with field goals of 41, 29, 53 and 55 yards. Elliott is 13 of 14 on field goal attempts, and 21 of 21 on extra points—including 5-for-5 on field goals attempts of 50 yards or more. Sirianni is going to have to think more of Elliott kicking field goals instead of trying to go for it on fourth down. What sometimes gets lost is Elliott is a two-time Super Bowl champion who has made huge field goals in huge games.

DeVonta Smith seemed to be the only weapon the Eagles had, and despite added attention, he caught a game-high 11 passes for a game-high 200 yards on 14 targets, including 50- and 45-yard touchdown catches. It was Smith's 45-yard TD catch with 15 seconds left in the third quarter that continued to make the game intriguing pulling the Eagles within 31-17.

Milton's 50-yard rainbow to DeVonta Smith for the Eagles' only touchdown in the first half. Milton was about the only thing the Eagles had going in the first half. Otherwise, it was bad.

Two of Milton's first three passes converted third downs. They were the first two good signs the Eagles might be able to move the ball against the NFL's 31st defense. Milton's first completion was a nine-yard completion to Dallas Goedert on a second-and-eight to the Eagles' 17. Three plays later. Milton connected with Smith for a 16-yard reception to the Eagles' 35. Of course, the Eagles blew it by going for it on fourth-and-one at the Dallas 37.

Money on third down. Milton completed eight of his first 11 passes, three of which came on third down. On the Eagles' first drive, he hit Smith for 16 yards on a third-and-8, and on third drive, he hit Makai Lemon for 20-yard pass on a third-and-12 to the Dallas 34. It led to Elliott's 48-yard field goal.

Byron Young with nine tackles from the defensive tackle position. He had five solo tackles, assisting on four.

Jeremiah Trotter Jr. continues to lead the defense. Every time the linebacker out of St, Joe's Prep and Clemson has started this season, he has led the Eagles in tackles. Thanksgiving against Dallas was no different, with a game-high 12 tackles, seven solo, with a sack and two tackles for loss. Trotter is now second on the Eagles in tackles with 64 tackles, although has a team-high 41 solo tackles.

Cobie Durant's interception with 7:05 to play seemed to seal the victory for the Cowboys after the Eagles' fourth stop of the game. Brandon Aubrey missed a gimme 40-yard field goal attempt bailed out the Eagles.

The Eagles losing Cooper DeJean, Saquon Barkley and Dallas Goedert to injury in the first quarter. Not that it mattered.

Cornerback Quinyon Mitchell flagged with pass interference on the very first play of the game. The 17-yard call took the ball from the Dallas 23 to the Cowboys' 40 and jumpstarted one of the NFL's most potent offenses. Mitchell was called for two pass interferences penalties for a total of 46 yards. It was not a good game for Mitchell, who was later burned for a 39-yard completion on a second-and-16 right after the Eagles' blocked punt score. The quick euphoria the Eagles had after the blocked punt was quickly zapped on a Dak Prescott 14-yard touchdown scramble with 10:11 to play.

The Eagles first half. Everyone may have given up by then and began eating their Thanksgiving Day dinner. The Cowboys scored on four of their first five drives. They had outgained the Eagles, 245 to 169, had 17 first downs to the Eagles' eight, and the Eagles committed penalties for 71 yards, including Quinyon Mitchell's second interference call for 29 yards, and Riq Woolen's 10-yard interference call leading to the Cowboys' last touchdown in the first half. The Cowboys converted 6 of 7 third downs to the Eagles' 2 of 5.

With 5:09 left in the first half, the Cowboys scored on three of their four possessions, had amassed 208 total yards of offense, averaging 8.3 yards a play, 12.9 yards a pass and 5.3 yards a rush. Zak Prescott was 6 of 10 for 129 yards and a touchdown.

Dak Prescott's 31-yard touchdown pass to Kavontae Turpin for a Cowboys' 14-0 lead with 36 seconds left in the first quarter. It looked like the route was on.

The Cowboys' second drive. This time,the Cowboys needed just five plays to score. But they came up with a big third-down play again, this time, on a third-and-16, when Prescott hit George Pickens for a 36-yard completion to the Eagles' 33. Riq Woolen made the tackle, and earned a benching again, but the Eagles were so depleted, they had to keep the problem on the field.

Sirianni opting to go for it on fourth-and-one at the Cowboys' 36, passing on a 53-yard field attempt by Elliott.

Will Shipley caught jawing on the sideline with Nick Sirianni in the first quarter, no doubt about playing time.

The Cowboys' first drive of the game. It was an 11-play, 77-yard drive that chewed up 6 minutes and 11 seconds off the clock. Of the Cowboys' 11 plays, they only faced three third downs—converting all of them. The true backbreaker was Prescott's 28-yard completion to CeeDee Lamb right down the middle of the field to the Eagles' 7. Three plays later, Javonte Williams scored from a yard out for a 7-0 Dallas lead with 8:50 to play in the first quarter. Of the Cowboys' first 11 plays, they threw it only three times, while Williams gashed them for 35 yards. It was the ghost of the previous Dallas game, when Williams sliced his way through the Eagles for 155 yards rushing, averaging 6.2 yards a carry. In the first game, the Cowboys ran 65 plays against the Eagles' defense for zero tackles for a loss. Dallas pounded the Eagles for 416 total yards, averaging 6.4 yards a game, with 204 yards on the ground, averaging 5.4 yards a carry. The Cowboys' first drive seemed to give loud warnings for another long day for the Eagles' defense.

There is a reason why Jihaad Campbell has struggled, it's because he gets lost in coverage. The linebacker once again managed to lose CeeDee Lamb on a third-and-18 on the Cowboys' first drive to the Eagles' 7. Campbell thought he was reading Prescott's eyes while Lamb ran right past him. It led to the first score of the game.

#2759. The stealing, running Mavs are the perfect cast for a high-IQ duo that could change basketball

The Mavericks just beat a very good Denver Nuggets team through aggressive defense, forcing steals, and running on offense. It is indeed a new day in Dallas.

Kyrie Irving, a player that has pretty much seen it all by now, didn't even have an efficient night (5/19 on field goal attempts, 1/8 from three). He did, however, lead the team to a victory that shows the whole league that the Dallas Mavericks now are the exact opposite of who they used to be.

In a gesture as moving as it was telling, Kyrie showed the world that he and Cooper Flagg have the rarest of connections. This is a high-IQ duo who could change the trajectory of what basketball looks like. And they may be on the way to doing that as we speak.

It is not only the gesture that's important here, it's the ultimate look away pass and the back door cut by Flagg that shows a connection between the two, usually built through years of ups and downs. It was almost like they could read each other's minds. This is what real chemistry looks like.

Kirk Henderson said it best: "There are lookaway passes, then there's something like this where I feel as if Kyrie's head never even moved the direction where Coop cut to."

Jamal Crawford interpreted the gesture as "me and you", and either way this is a gesture of connection, we – as in us – we got this. And even though Kyrie meant Cooper, we all feel it a little bit.

You may be watching from across the globe, you may not even be a Mavs fan, but anybody watching, knowing what this team and city have gone through recently, felt it.

Because no one has worked harder to get the Dallas Mavericks back together than Kyrie Irving. After it was torn apart, he did everything he could to hold it together. He mentored, he was everyone's vet, he led, he coached when injured – he showed the way and the light in the dark times.

And Dallas is reaping the benefits right now. That is also why it felt so touching, seeing the vet who worked so hard to hold it all together, make this gesture to the young superstar with the highest ceiling imaginable.

They took away his running mate, his brother and a genuine friendship and on-court chemistry, resulting in the most negative period in Dallas, in which he forced himself to play too much and carry more than he should have. His resulting ACL injury took him away from his craft for more than a year, and now – finally – his hard work is really paying off. Kyrie Irving deserves this.

And despite the spiritual experience it can be to watch Irving play, especially when he has chemistry with his teammates, he didn't carry the team to this extraordinary win. Neither did his co-star Flagg really.

It was a cast of teammates who decided to outwork, outhustle and believe in their connection, along with a well-prepared coach, who had clearly done a lot of work to get this team ready. It was no easy feat. But the Mavs made it look easy.

They had 13 steals. The league leader averages 10.6 (Los Angeles Lakers). The Mavs average 9.44 this season, but led by the surprising hero of the last games, Caleb Martin, with five, they kept the pressure on the passing lanes all game, stressing the Nuggets in a way we don't see every day. Not only that, they had 16 steals last game versus Utah, and a whopping 50 steals the last four games (which all happen to be wins).

Martin started this game on fire on both ends, secured the first six points by knocking down two threes in a row and continued to force five steals in the first half, constantly the first to run in transition.

This is exactly the leadership a team needs from a role player next to superstars, and with Zaccharie Risacher next to him, the two have the potential to cause havoc defensively on every team in the league, followed by easy transition points, if they keep this up.

Caleb Martin explained the game plan in the half:

"High sense of urgency. A high level of respect for a great team," he said during the broadcast.

He went on to explain that steals were an emphasis, as well as just being active. "The more active we are," Martin said, "sense of urgency, the better a team we are, positive things happen".

And not even interview questions were about to distract Caleb Martin, who is on a mission in Dallas right now.

When asked in the half whether the loud colors of the court, which are usually a little on the bright side during Cup games, was giving him a headache, too, he answered deadpan:

"I don't know, I'm trying to win a game right now."

#2758. Trump demands probe into ‘record setting error’ after third-quarter GDP data gets massive revision higher

President Donald Trump on Thursday falsely deemed the latest gross domestic product revision a mistake, claiming that it should be "immediately investigated."

Data released Wednesday from the Commerce Department revealed that the U.S. economy exhibited much stronger growth for the third quarter than initially estimated, showing that real GDP grew at a 4.3% annualized rate. That was revised upward from the department's advance estimate of 3.4%.

That revision was a "RECORD SETTING ERROR," Trump said in a Truth Social post, suggesting that the initial release could have influenced the outcome of the 2026 U.S. midterm elections, where Democrats flipped both chambers of Congress and even multiple governorships.

"I would like to wish a Happy Thanksgiving to even those who issued fake Economic Numbers, just prior to the Election, and corrected them yesterday, three weeks late, showing that the GDP was actually 4.3%, versus 3.4%, A RECORD SETTING ERROR, and one so grave that it should be immediately investigated," Trump wrote.

However, the difference between the advance estimate and the figure in the second release is far from being an all-time record. It was the largest revision since the 1.2 percentage point revision seen in the second quarter of 2020.

On an absolute basis, the latest revision was tied for 16th among all revisions from the advance estimate to the second release since 1990. It also tied four other quarters with a similar 0.9 percentage point difference — and was approximately double the average revision of 0.43 percentage points for all 147 quarters.

The upward revision shows Trump's policies are "delivering results" that defy expectations, White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC.

Revisions to U.S. economic numbers are not out of the ordinary. Initial reports count on partial survey data and early estimates. Those data are later updated with more survey responses — which sometimes leads to changes from the initial reports.

GDP data is revised repeatedly over a five year period. By the time the final revision is done, the average absolute error from the second release to the final is 1.14 percentage points.

The advance estimate for Q3 U.S. GDP, which came just days before Election Day in early November, was still better than the figure economists had expected at the time. It also showed a significant step-up in growth from the second quarter.

Because that was a strong showing for the economy, some on Wall Street consider this week's revision to be a confirmation of the economy's recent growth trend.

Jim Baird of Plante Moran Financial Advisors said immediately following the release that it "reinforces an increasingly familiar tension" that the economy is "performing quite well, but its resilience also gives the Fed an even stronger case for further rate hikes."

"The economy isn't merely hanging in there. It continues to demonstrate considerable resilience, with consumers spending, businesses investing and few signs that restrictive monetary policy is creating significant cracks in the broader expansion," the firm's chief investment officer said.

Traders are currently pricing in a nearly 100% chance of a hike at the Federal Open Market Committee's December meeting, per the CME FedWatch tool. They're also anticipating an additional hike in January.

— CNBC's Jeff Cox and Fred Imbert contributed reporting

#2757. What Texas Tech Win Means for CFP Rankings, Big 12 Scenarios, BYU, Oregon

LUBBOCK, TEXAS - NOVEMBER 26: Julien Laventure #1 of the Texas Tech Red Raiders celebrates after a tackle during the first half of the game against the TCU Horned Frogs at Galaxy Stadium on November 26, 2026 in Lubbock, Texas. (Photo by John E. Moore III/Getty Images)

LUBBOCK, TEXAS - NOVEMBER 26: Thomas Castellanos #4 of the Texas Tech Red Raiders runs the ball during the first half of the game against the TCU Horned Frogs at Galaxy Stadium on November 26, 2026 in Lubbock, Texas. (Photo by John E. Moore III/Getty Images)

The Texas Tech Red Raiders fell behind 10-0 on Thursday night against the TCU Horned Frogs.

And then Texas Tech woke up. They rattled off score after score, sending them toward a likely win over TCU.

It's going to have fans wondering what this victory means on a number of fronts.

It will impact the College Football Playoff rankings, the Big 12 title game scenarios — as well as teams like TCU and Oregon.

This is what you need to know, assuming Texas Tech does indeed win on Thanksgiving Night.

GettyLUBBOCK, TEXAS – NOVEMBER 26: Julien Laventure #1 of the Texas Tech Red Raiders celebrates after a tackle during the first half of the game against the TCU Horned Frogs at Galaxy Stadium on November 26, 2026 in Lubbock, Texas. (Photo by John E. Moore III/Getty Images)

The first thing to think about is the CFP rankings.

Texas Tech was ranked No. 11 this week — the final at-large spot, with No. 12 going to the best Group of 6 team in the final rankings.

The Red Raiders are behind No. 8 Wake Forest, No. 9 UCLA, No. 10 BYU and ahead of No. 12 Oregon, No. 13 Iowa and No. 14 Indiana.

Texas Tech will remain, at worst, at No. 11. They could move up in the next rankings depending on what happens above them.

It's a big deal for them to remain in the tournament field this week.

GettyLUBBOCK, TEXAS – NOVEMBER 26: Thomas Castellanos #4 of the Texas Tech Red Raiders runs the ball during the first half of the game against the TCU Horned Frogs at Galaxy Stadium on November 26, 2026 in Lubbock, Texas. (Photo by John E. Moore III/Getty Images)

Texas Tech's scenario to make the Big 12 title game was simple.

If they win on Thursday night, they make the Big 12 championship game.

Utah awaits Texas Tech, which would also be in with a loss combined with a BYU loss.

BYU is impacted in two ways by a Texas Tech win on Thanksgiving.

The Cougars won't make the Big 12 title game, which means one less chance to impress the committee.

And as the team right ahead of Texas Tech in this week's rankings, the Cougars can't afford any kind of slip-up this weekend after the Red Raiders won on Thursday.

This is one less team that can slip up this weekend ahead of No. 12 Oregon — which would be the first team out if the CFP started today.

The Ducks need to beat Washington this weekend, but they'll also need someone else to lose ahead of them.

The conference championship weekend could impact things, too, but that'd depend on the CFP committee punishing teams who make their conference title games and then lose, which is tricky.

The best thing that can happen for the Ducks is Wake Forest, UCLA or BYU losing this weekend and combining with an Oregon win over Washington.

#2756. Dez Bryant goes off on Cowboys in NSFW rant after implosion vs. Eagles: ‘Get everybody the f–k out’

Dez Bryant didn't hold back after watching his former Cowboys team author the largest Thanksgiving collapse in NFL history.

The legendary wide receiver, who was in attendance in Texas, was calling for Cowboys owner Jerry Jones to clean house as the team was in the process of blowing a 21-point third-quarter lead with a calamity of a fourth quarter.

Dez Bryant Getty Images for Fanatics Sports

"Jerry, Jerry, Jerry, everybody got to get the f–k up out of here," Bryant, who played eight years on Dallas from 2010-2017, said in a video posted to social media. "F–k that. That's completely unacceptable. That is unacceptable. F–k that

"Get everybody the f–k up out of here. Fire everybody. Fire'm all. F–k it. Fire'm, get everybody the f–k out. F–k that. Fire everybody."

The Cowboys were as much to blame for losing the game as the Eagles were for winning, as they surrendered a blocked punt for a touchdown early in the fourth quarter, watched usually automatic kicker Brandon Aubrey miss from 40 yards on a kick that would have given them a five-point lead with 3:27 remaining in the game.

Dallas (5-7) chose to try to put the game away by going for it on fourth and 1 from the Eagles' 41, but Dak Prescott was sacked, setting up Philly's last-gasp effort.

Dallas head coach Brian Schottenheimer speaks at a press conference after the Cowboys' 39-38 stunning loss to the Eagles on Nov. 26, 2026, in Arlington, Texas. AP Photo/Sam Hodde

Dallas owner Jerry Jones speaks to the media after the Cowboys' stunning Thanksgiving loss to the Eagles. AP Photo/Sam Hodde

#2755. How Moe Wagner and Danny Wolf have become offensive bright spots for the Nets

It's pretty doom and gloom in NetsWorld right now, and that's justified. Sitting at 3-13 after Monday night's blowout against the Minnesota Timberwolves, the team has now suffered nine double-digit losses this season.

When a young, rebuilding team isn't winning, it's one thing, but getting completely outmatched consistently is another. That's pretty much what's been the case for Brooklyn, as nine of their 13 losses have come by 10+ points.

Interestingly, one of the biggest questions entering the season surrounded Brooklyn's frontcourt. It wasn't clear how the rotation would take shape.

Day'Ron Sharpe entered the season as the team's starting center following Nic Claxton's departure, while Moe Wagner arrived in free agency. Wagner's health was in question after he played just 66 games over the previous two seasons, including time missed due to a torn ACL. Danny Wolf was another question mark after an up-and-down rookie campaign.

So far, the frontcourt has produced more offense than expected. Brooklyn's rim protection remains a work in progress, and its defense has struggled at every level. But offensively, two of the team's biggest surprises have been Wagner and Wolf.

Offensively, two of Brooklyn's biggest surprises have been Wagner and Wolf.

Wagner has been known for his offensive game since entering the league—most notably his success from beyond the arc and his consistency.

Although injuries have hindered him significantly over the past two years, Wagner is starting to pick up where he left off before the ACL injury forced him out of action for 98 games.

Through 16 games, Wagner owns a 70.7% effective field-goal percentage, ranking in the 98th percentile among bigs, per Cleaning the Glass. A large part of that production comes from his remarkable 62.5% shooting from three-point range. That percentage almost certainly won't hold over an 82-game season, but it's an encouraging start to his Brooklyn tenure.

What's even more encouraging, though, is the success beyond just the 3-point line. For a big like Wagner, who isn't necessarily touted for rim protection or stout defense, being a multi-faceted offensive contributor is crucial.

Worth noting, though, he only played four minutes in Wednesday's win over the Milwaukee Bucks after exiting with left knee soreness.

Overall, he's returning to the offense that worked at the peak of his career in Orlando. Last season with the Magic, just 44% of his overall attempts came at the rim, while 25% came from the midrange. This year, those numbers have shifted to 60% and 19%, respectively. That's much more in line with what we saw from Wagner during his two most efficient seasons (2023-24, 2024-25), with his effective FG percentage finishing north of 60% in both of those campaigns.

Wagner has also improved drastically at creating his own shot. Last season, 88% of his made baskets at the rim were assisted. Meanwhile, that has dropped to 61%, despite a 15-percentage-point increase in his shot usage around the basket.

Obviously, there's only so much that Wagner could be expected to do. Signed to a two-year, $18.4 million contract this offseason, he was widely viewed as the Nets' backup big behind Sharpe. So far, that has held. Sharpe has started all 17 of Brooklyn's games, while averaging 24 minutes per game. Meanwhile, off the bench, Wagner has averaged 17.1 minutes a game.

Entering the season, one of the biggest concerns with the Nets' roster was where points would come from off the bench. So far, their bench scoring hasn't been too shabby, ranking 13th in bench points per game (38.7). More impressively, though, their bench ranks 11th in 3-point percentage (35.7%). Wagner is clearly a big reason behind that.

Having a big who can stretch the floor while also generating offense around the basket is a luxury.

The bigger question mark entering the year is what the Nets really had in Wolf. Just like the four Flatbush 5 members behind Egor Dëmin, whose start to the year had been disappointing, the Michigan product had a lot to prove heading into the season.

So far, though, that development has been encouraging. Just like Wagner, the 27th overall pick in last June's draft has emerged as a threat from beyond the arc. After shooting just 32% from deep as a rookie, he is converting at 46.7% this season, ranking in the 95th percentile among bigs.

There has also been an uptick in volume: 54% of his overall shot attempts have come from the 3-point range, up from 44% a season ago. His mark this year ranks in the 88th percentile among bigs. Beyond that, 35% of his overall attempts have come from non-corner 3s, which ranks in the 91st percentile.

Wolf's output has also had a large impact on Brooklyn as a whole. Last season, the Nets scored 5.1 fewer points per 100 possessions with him on the floor than with him on the bench. This season, they're scoring 5.4 more points per 100 possessions with him out there, ranking in the 83rd percentile among all players.

Additionally, the Nets' effective field-goal percentage improves by 3.2 percentage points with him on the floor, while the offensive rebounding percentage also rises by 8.3 percentage points. The latter ranks in the 96th percentile among players who have logged a minute this season. For context, he has only played 244 minutes this season, but those numbers are still fascinating.

Even more interesting is how he's been used. After opening the season outside of Jordi Fernández's rotation, Wolf has worked his way into the lineup, but in an odd spot: as the Nets' starting shooting guard. How many people had that on their bingo card entering the season?

In fact, six of his seven starts this season have come at the 2.

Against the Magic on Friday night, Fernández inserted Wolf into the starting five for Dëmin, and Wolf responded with a 20-point outing. In his second straight start, against the Minnesota Timberwolves on Monday, he finished with 11 points, four assists, and five rebounds on 4-of-7 shooting, including 3-of-5 from deep.

As our own Anthony Puccio noted earlier this month, Wolf played every second of his initial breakout performance against the Charlotte Hornets alongside either Sharpe or Wagner. That trend has held mostly true since then, letting him work away from the basket and avoid many traditional big responsibilities.

Is he truly a 5 in the NBA? There need to be more reps to make the case for or against it, but he doesn't ideally fit as a shooting guard. At 6'11", it's just not a natural fit, and it is easy for him to get exposed guarding shifty playmaking guards at the top of the key. Even though he was a ball handler at Michigan, being a guard in college versus the NBA are two completely different responsibilities. Nonetheless, the versatility is highly encouraging.

Regardless of where Wolf ultimately settles in the long term, he and Wagner have given the Nets more offensively than anyone could have asked for before the season.

#2754. Two Italians killed in Kuwait drone attack; Trump says Iran war ends ‘whenever I want it’

Two Italians have been killed after an alleged Iranian attack on Kuwait, Kuwaiti forces said Thursday.

In a post on X, Kuwait's army said the deaths followed Iranian attacks on the country, with the two Italians killed by falling missile debris. Two others were also injured.

In response, a spokesperson for Iran's military told state media that no offensive or military operations had been carried out in the area and that "these accusations should be viewed as a cover for continued malevolence and an attempt to exacerbate insecurity in the region" by U.S. and Israeli forces.

Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said on Telegram that he had spoken with his Italian counterpart, Antonio Tajani, and expressed regret over their deaths, while stressing that Tehran had no role or involvement in the incident.

"The incident is suspicious and looks like a precooked recipe for war," Araghchi wrote on X.

He also said Iran was ready to cooperate with the governments of Kuwait and Italy in investigating the incident.

Separately, Israel said Thursday that it was prepared to carry out strikes against Iran "at any moment," even without U.S. forces joining the effort, according to Israeli military spokesman Effie Defrin on Israeli broadcaster Kan.

The comments came after U.S. envoy Jared Kushner told a podcast Wednesday that Israel "needs to get out of the constant wartime mindset."

Israel has been pushing the Trump administration to back another offensive against Tehran, with Reuters reporting on Nov. 18 that Israeli spy chief Roman Gofman pushed for joint strikes when he met U.S. officials earlier in the month.

Late Thursday, President Donald Trump said on PBS that the U.S. had "victory at hand" in the Iran conflict and reiterated that victory would come "whenever I want it to be, to be honest."

Speaking during a Thanksgiving call with U.S. military personnel, Trump said Washington was still trying to give Iran "every chance to make a deal and save a lot of lives."

But he added that he was not sure whether Tehran would agree.

"Some people question why. Are they crazy? Maybe they are," Trump said.
