#2753. Cowboys vs. Eagles: Brian Schottenheimer tops our losers list

The Dallas Cowboys looked like they were going to run the Philadelphia Eagles out of AT&T Stadium on Thanksgiving.Then everything changed.

Dallas built a commanding 31-10 advantage before allowing the Eagles to storm back, turning what should have been a comfortable victory into a frantic finish. There were some outstanding individual performances, but also several familiar problems that ruined the afternoon and saw the Eagles secure a comeback victory.

It's hard not to lay a lot of the blame for this loss on the shoulders of head coach Brian Schottenheimer. His repeated inability to finish out games has crushed the Cowboys hopes this season.

Late in this game, Schottenheimer had a chance to be aggressive and go for the killing touchdown with Dallas up 38-36. Instead, once he got in field goal position he got conservative and played for the three points. Brandon Aubrey missed the kick.

Later in the fourth quarter, he had a 4th-and-1 decision to make. He chose about the worst avenue. Instead of punting, or kicking a long field goal, he went for it even though not converting would give the Eagles the ball close to field goal range. To make matters worse, while going for it, he didn't use Javonte Williams, or got Prescott out on the edge with a run/pass option. Instead, he called a drop back pass which resulted in a sack.

It was gross mismanagement of the end of the game.

Dallas stopped Philadelphia from running the football. Then Joe Milton and DeVonta Smith ripped the secondary apart. Milton threw for 331 yards and three touchdowns, averaging over 12 yards per attempt.

Smith was even more damaging, producing 11 catches for 200 yards and two touchdowns, including scores of 50 and 45 yards, those numbers are insanely brutal.

Dallas repeatedly allowed receivers behind the secondary and surrendered exactly the kind of explosive plays that have plagued this defense throughout the season. The Cowboys had Philadelphia buried at 31-10. The pass defense gave them a route straight back into the game.

This might have been the single worst play of the afternoon. Dallas led 31-17 early in the fourth quarter and simply needed to get the punt away. Instead, Philadelphia blocked Bryan Anger's punt and recovered it for a touchdown. Suddenly, a comfortable two-touchdown advantage became a seven-point game.

A blocked punt returned for a touchdown while trying to protect a fourth-quarter lead is about as bad as it gets. Then, of course, there's the missed Brandon Aubrey field goal that would have kept the game out of reach at the end of the fourth quarter. That field goal was crucial and he failed to connect the kick for three points. It's a brutal miss but it was just another addition to the miscues on special teams this week.

Dallas led 31-10, Philadelphia then scored 29 points while Dallas managed only seven. The Cowboys allowed a backup quarterback to turn what looked like a Thanksgiving demolition into a game that came down to the final minute.

The blocked punt played a major part, but this wasn't one isolated mistake. Explosive passes, penalties and defensive breakdowns all contributed. Dallas has shown repeatedly that it can build leads, but they haven't learned how to finish opponents. We've failed to see this repeatedly so don't expect it to change.

Dak Prescott gave Dallas everything it needed offensively. Prescott completed 19 of 28 passes for 291 yards and two touchdowns, while also adding a 14-yard rushing touchdown in the fourth quarter. He consistently attacked Philadelphia downfield, protected the football and kept answering whenever the Eagles threatened to take control of the game.

CeeDee Lamb continued his outstanding run of form. Lamb finished with seven receptions for 134 yards, averaging more than 19 yards per catch despite not finding the end zone.

His ability to create explosive plays consistently stretched Philadelphia's defense and opened opportunities elsewhere for George Pickens and KaVontae Turpin. Lamb has reached the stage where 100-yard performances almost feel routine.

Javonte Williams carried a massive workload and rewarded Dallas for sticking with him. Williams rushed 29 times for 101 yards and two touchdowns, while also catching a 12-yard touchdown pass from Prescott.

His one-yard touchdown opened the scoring, his 12-yard run extended Dallas' first-half advantage, and he later added the receiving score just before halftime. Three touchdowns on Thanksgiving is a pretty convincing way to land in the winner column.

#2752. Bills report card: Thanksgiving FG to beat Chiefs is one for the ages

ORCHARD PARK - Tyler Bass may have delivered the greatest field goal in Buffalo Bills history Thursday night.

No, check that. As someone who has covered this team for than 40 years and followed them for nearly 60 years, I'm making the call: It was the greatest kick in Bills history when you consider the abhorrent weather conditions, 57 yards, final play of the game, against perhaps their biggest rival, the Kansas City Chiefs, in a game that had huge implications in the race for the No. 1 seed in the AFC.

Unbelievable barely covers it, and because Bass came through in that moment, the Bills finished off an incredible day of Thanksgiving football with a 13-10 victory that improved their record to 10-1 and keep them atop the conference with six weeks left to play.

"Hard to say," Bass said when asked if it was his best kick ever. "I've played in a lot of bad conditions here. Definitely was up there as far as conditions wise, but I just treat it as the next one and just try to put my best foot forward and trust it."

Bass is as modest as they come and he always downplays his accomplishments. Let me be clear: This kick was spectacular in every way.

Bass said the most difficult aspect wasn't the wind, which had died down from earlier in the evening, or even the rain which had also curtailed a bit. The issue was the soggy, chewed up field, and trying to find a good spot for holder Tommy Doman to put the ball down.

"It was all pretty torn up, it's been raining a lot, but I just look down, Tommy usually calls it out, and just kind of saw a spot that somewhat looked pretty good, and just kind of made sure the footing was okay, and after that, just let it ride."

Outside of Bass, no one was happier when that ball sailed through the uprights than Josh Allen who played one of the worst games of his career. He admitted that, too, and he thanked Bass for coming through.

"Obviously, the weather was what it was tonight, but I have to better, I need to better, I will better," Allen said. "Thankful for my team, thankful for my defense, thankful for my kicker, thankful for James Cook and our O-linemen. Man, this took a lot of people. I made it a little harder on ourselves, I'm going to put my hand up there. But we found a way to get to 10-1."

Allen is not infallible, and we certainly found that out. He was terrible in this game and yes, the weather had a lot to do with it, but he was terrible, period. How terrible? If you care about passer rating, his mark of 18.3 was the second-worst of his NFL career. That's what happens when you complete 10 of 21 passes for just 81 yards, throw two red zone interceptions that took points off the board, and suffered two sacks including a huge one on Buffalo's penultimate possession.

Again, the conditions were ridiculous and Allen said it was "one of the grossest weather games I think I've ever played." Still, he had receivers open and he missed them, he made some poor decisions, and this has continued a trend that is starting to become worrisome.

Khalil Shakir led the way with just three catches for 33 yards plus a couple drops including one that led to Allen's second pick, though it was a poor pass. DJ Moore did almost nothing, same for Dalton Kincaid. Buffalo's passing game was completely devoid of rhythm all night and much credit goes to a Kansas City defense that drove Allen nuts.

With the conditions as they were, this was always going to be Cook as the focal point of the offense and he certainly was as he tied his career-high with 32 carries, which resulted in 117 yards and the Bills' only touchdown early in the second quarter. The Bills knew they had to pound Cook and he was the only aspect of the offense that produced anything.

Brady tried to give him a few breaks but Ray Davis was basically useless with six yards on four carries. Allen had 43 yards, much of that on a pair of scrambles, but he had some poorly timed designed runs that did nothing. Credit to the offensive line for creating some cracks for Cook to run through, but every yard was difficult as his 3.7 average per carry would indicate. His longest run was just 10 yards.

Like Allen, Patrick Mahomes struggled mightily in the conditions, though he certainly had more success completing 16 of 29 for 174 yards without turning the ball over. However, he could only produce two scoring drives, one on each side of the second-half kickoff, and he was part of the reason why the Chiefs had only 13 first downs and were 2-of-11 on third down.

The Bills played without CB Christian Benford but Max Hairston and Davison Igbinosun both held up fine as the Chiefs' wide receivers caught just five passes for 56 yards, none of those by speedy Xavier Worthy. Hairston had Worthy on 13 coverage snaps and shut him out, while Igbinosun had Rashee Rice on 11 snaps and held him to one catch for nine yards.

One of the biggest plays of the game came when Amani Hooker crushed Rice on the Chiefs' second-to-last possession that wiped out a first down that would have allowed them to probably at least get the game to overtime.

As for the pass rush, it managed just one sack, that by Greg Rousseau, but Sumer Sports had the Bills at a 35.8% pressure rate so they were affecting him.

The Chiefs did not have Kenneth Walker so they leaned on rookie Emmett Johnson who ran hard and piled up 72 yards, plus he caught four passes for 24 yards. But that was it. The only other two yards rushing came from Mahomes so the Chiefs finished with 74 yards and a 3.7 average per carry on a night when running the ball was vital to success.

Their inability to establish it is a big reason why they were in some tough third-down situations as they couldn't stay ahead of the sticks, and it's also why they had the ball for barely 23 minutes. Ed Oliver and DeWayne Carter both had strong games in the middle against the run, as did Hooker when he had to come up and get involved.

Bass made the kick of his life to win it, and he also drilled a 54-yarder earlier in the fourth quarter that tied the game at 10-10. The last kick was also set up by Greg Dortch's excellent 17-yard punt return that gave Bass a chance.

Tommy Doman had a crazy up and down night. His first punt went into the end zone for a net of just 23 yards, and his second went 61 yards and was downed at the 4. His third was an awful 23-yarder that set up the Chiefs for the end of first half field goal. But his fourth was a 57-yarder that pinned the Chiefs at their own 9 with two minutes remaining, and his last was a 46-yarder that pinned them at the 11. Those last two played a huge role in the field position advantage the Bills needed at the end to win the game.

The second half did not start well as the Bills allowed Jalen Royals to return the kickoff to the 44 and they took advantage of that drive start to drive for a go-ahead touchdown but for the night the Chiefs averaged just 24.3 yards on kickoff returns.

Joe Brady once again took the ball to start the game after winning the coin toss and the Bills ended up punting on their opening series. They've scored just two opening-drive TDs this season. Also, they gave the Chiefs the double-dip opportunity and Kansas City cashed it in with 10 points to take a 10-7 lead early in the third. That was simply an arrogant call by Brady and it finally burned him.

Brady's call for a pass on first down from the Chiefs 47 with 1:40 to go was one of his worst of the season, and it set the tone for that drive to fail, but he was bailed out by his defense. He failed all night to get the passing game going, though his QB played a role with his inaccuracy.

As for the defense, Jim Leonhard has his guys on a roll, and there were no asterisks for this one. No backup QBs here. This was Mahomes and the Chiefs, and the defense was outstanding all night in limiting one of the best offenses in the league to 13 first downs, 241 yards and 10 points.

Sal Maiorana has covered the Buffalo Bills for more than four decades including 37 years as the full-time beat writer/columnist for the D&C. He has written numerous books about the history of the team, and he is also co-host of the BLEAV in Bills podcast/YouTube show. #2751. SpaceX shares jump on alternative trading platform after Musk says he will expand share donations

Shares of SpaceX jumped 7.47% in overnight trading on Blue Ocean ATS after CEO Elon Musk expanded his SpaceX share-donation plan for U.S. children.

"I will be donating another share of SpaceX to all kids in America every year," Musk said in a post on X.

Blue Ocean ATS is an alternative trading system for U.S. equities that operates from 8 p.m. to 4 a.m. ET, outside regular U.S. stock market hours. According to the platform's holiday schedule, overnight trading resumed at 8 p.m. ET on Thanksgiving Day. U.S. stock exchanges were closed Thursday and are scheduled to close early Friday.

SpaceX shares closed 1.30% higher on Wednesday ahead of Thanksgiving.

The latest pledge follows SpaceX's announcement Wednesday that it would donate one share of its Class A stock to every active Trump Account, tax-advantaged investment accounts for American children, with Musk personally matching the gift.

In a separate post on X, Musk said SpaceX's contribution would be transferred next month, while his personal donation would follow when his lockup period expires in June next year.

Nearly 70 million Trump Accounts had been created as of October, according to the White House.

Since the program's launch in July, families and friends have contributed more than $1 billion to the accounts, bringing total deposits from all sources to more than $9 billion, CNBC reported earlier this week.

#2750. Highlights: Victor Wembanyama drops 44 points and 16 rebounds in Portland

Coming off a shorthanded win in Sacramento, the Spurs traveled to Portland to take on the Trail Blazers in NBA Cup play. Victor Wembanyama returned after sitting out the Sacramento game, but De'Aaron Fox was ruled out again with a shin injury. The first half was defined by two different-paced quarters: the first quarter was a high-scoring track meet that ended with Portland leading 38-35, but in the second quarter, both teams struggled to shoot. After scoring 22 points each, the Blazers took a 60-57 lead into halftime. The lead changed back and forth throughout the third, with Devin Vassell beating the buzzer at the end of the quarter. With the Spurs up by three heading into the fourth, a wild quarter was about to take place. For every punch Portland threw at the Spurs, the Spurs would counter with Wemby being Wemby. Late in the game, the Spurs had multiple chances to tie it up. However, unforced turnovers and ill-advised shots gave Portland a chance to seal the game with under a minute left. After Deni Advija banked in a lucky three to beat the shot clock buzzer, the Spurs needed a miracle. Unfortunately, the miracle did not happen, and the Spurs lost 116-111.

Wemby continues his MVP campaign by dropping a whopping 44 points (17-30 FG, 7-10 FT), 16 rebounds, three assists, and two blocks. When someone says Wemby is being Wemby, they usually mean he does an unimaginable and unguardable act on the court that results in a made basket. The Alien's highlight tape contains multiple examples of no regard for human life. The following is a clinic on how to embarrass Donovan Clingan. First, Wemby gets Clingan to bite up on his pump fake, and then he drives to the basket with long strides for the easy one-handed jam.

HIGHLIGHT OF THE GAME: Wemby pulls out the street moves by going behind-the-back on the fast break and finishing with a scoop layup off the glass with his left hand.

Stephon Castle was the lead guard with Fox and Dylan Harper out, and he turned in a near triple-double performance: 20 points, nine assists, and six rebounds. Here, Castle and Wemby run a beautiful two-man game that results in an AREA 51 alley-oop connection.

And now, a series of and-ones converted on Clingan. First, a jump hook with the left in the paint? Through contact? Okay, Wemby.

Second, Wemby grabs the board, leads the fast break by himself, drives in on Clingan, goes with the hook shot with his right hand, and puts it in off the glass. Oh, plus the foul, too.

We interrupt this Wemby 1 on 1 masterclass with a bailout pass out of shot to a cutting Carter Bryant slam. Thank you for your patience.

Back to regularly scheduled programming, Wemby goes to work on Clingan and steps through side contact to finish strong at the rim for the and-one. If you're keeping score at home, that's three and-ones scored on Clingan from Wemby. If you were wondering, Clingan eventually fouled out and picked up a technical foul on his way out. He said something about how Wemby is soft and flopping? If that was the case, Micah Nori should've challenged one of his fouls.

Bonus highlight: Here is how Vassell beat the third-quarter buzzer: A leaning three from the wing in transition. Nothing but net.

#2747. Sovereign wealth funds are acting more like private equity giants — and inheriting their risks

For decades, sovereign wealth funds were among private equity's most deep-pocketed backers, entrusting billions of dollars to buyout firms to invest on their behalf. Increasingly, they are doing the dealmaking themselves.

From originating transactions and taking controlling stakes in companies to building their own investment platforms, some of the world's largest state investors are adopting the playbook of traditional private equity giants, according to industry veterans.

"The imminent change is that the lines between LPs and GPs are becoming increasingly blurry. This could bring more fundamental changes in the long term," Diego Lopez of Global SWF told CNBC.

Limited partners, or LPs, typically commit capital to private equity funds, while general partners, or GPs, are responsible for sourcing deals and managing investments.

Sovereign funds and state investment companies are increasingly taking on the GP role, Lopez said, pointing to the establishment of their own private equity management platforms, such as Mubadala Capital and Singapore's Seviora.

The shift is also reflected in the way some of the world's largest sovereign investors are approaching dealmaking.

"They originate transactions rather than waiting for them. They build platforms rather than buying single assets. They take control positions and operate what they own," PwC said in their latest report on sovereign wealth funds.

"Whether you're a company, a general partner, or a government, you'll find that these institutions are no longer the limited partner you're raising money from; they're the principal sitting across the table."

Last year, Abu Dhabi's MGX, an investment vehicle established by state fund Mubadala in 2024, anchored the roughly $40 billion acquisition of Aligned Data Centers alongside BlackRock, Nvidia and Microsoft. A year earlier, a consortium led by Mubadala Capital acquired a 68% equity stake in alternative asset manager Fortress, which at the time managed more than $48 billion.

Some sovereign funds are striking deals at a speed typically associated with large private equity firms, according to PwC.

Singapore's GIC recorded 553 transactions between July 2023 and July 2026, Abu Dhabi's Mubadala 397 and the Abu Dhabi Investment Authority 367, according to PwC's analysis of PitchBook data, which includes transactions by subsidiaries.

The shift comes as sovereign wealth funds command unprecedented financial firepower. Their assets have surged about 59% over the past five years to $16.5 trillion, up from $10.4 trillion in 2022 and more than nine times the $1.8 trillion they managed in 2005, according to data provided to CNBC by research firm Global SWF which focuses on state-owned investors.

PwC projects that sovereign wealth fund assets to reach $22.4 trillion by 2030.

While sovereign funds' push into direct investing has been underway for years, a more challenging fundraising environment for PE firms and a resurgence in megadeals have recently accelerated the trend.

The fundraising slowdown has tilted bargaining power toward large institutional investors, allowing them to negotiate more opportunities to invest directly alongside private equity managers, said Kyle Walters of PitchBook.

A key attraction is the potential to save on fees. Co-investments typically carry little or no management fees and no carried interest, which is the share of investment profits that managers receive. Additionally, direct deals can eliminate external manager fees altogether.

"For a large SWF writing big checks, the fee savings in dollar terms can be substantial," Walters said.

Sovereign funds also enjoy structural advantages over traditional buyout firms. Unlike conventional private equity funds, which operate within predetermined lifespans, sovereign investors can hold assets for decades, the experts highlighted.

They can also deploy substantial sums from their own balance sheets without having to raise fresh capital, an advantage particularly valuable in capital-intensive sectors such as AI infrastructure and energy.

But deep pockets and patient capital alone do not guarantee success.

As sovereign wealth funds take controlling stakes in companies, they assume operational responsibilities traditionally borne by private equity managers, while potentially stretching their own capacity to source deals, conduct due diligence and manage complex assets, PwC cautioned.

"Building the required capability, the talent, the systems, and the governance is a slower process than committing the capital," PwC said. The firm also flagged concentration risks as sovereign investors increasingly pile into similar investment themes, particularly AI, data centers and power infrastructure, leaving even geographically diversified portfolios vulnerable to the same underlying risks.

Despite their growing ambitions, industry experts are skeptical that sovereign wealth funds will supplant established private equity managers.

"This new path these large LPs are paving is more of a partnership than a competition between GPs and LPs," Walters said, noting that much of the increased activity is taking the form of co-investments.

He pointed to the $55 billion take-private deal for video-game maker Electronic Arts announced last year, in which Saudi Arabia's PIF contributed significant capital, as an example of how both sides ae joining hands.

Traditional buyout firms also retain an edge in sourcing deals and managing portfolio companies.

"However, capital alone does not replace the specialist sourcing, operational expertise, and active ownership capabilities required to consistently create value, which experienced managers have built over time," said Vincent Ng, managing director and co-head of Asia at Partners Group.

Direct investing also raises concentration risks, while illiquidity could become a problem during crises when governments need access to capital, experts said.

Rather than displacing traditional buyout firms, sovereign investors' growing ambitions could change the economics of private equity, forcing managers to become more flexible in how they work with their biggest clients.

"This is likely to reshape the relationship between asset owners and managers, with more customized mandates, co-investments, and long-term partnerships," Ng said.

#2745. What we learned from the Spurs’ loss to the Trail Blazers

One of the few things I hope for as a spectator is that teams playing the late-night game bring it. It's too hard to stay awake as it is after a long day, so fans need excitement to keep them from falling asleep. Fortunately, it was not a problem for these teams, yet the only issue the San Antonio Spurs had late was their basketball IQ.

I had this game circled on the calendar because the physicality was off the charts the last time they played on Nov. 7. It appears it was not a distant memory for the Portland Trail Blazers, as the ESPN broadcast highlighted how they felt embarrassed by that game and wanted their lick back. Nonetheless, it was grown-up hoops and it wouldn't be a surprise if these squads didn't like each other.

Wednesday night being an Emirates Cup game added to the fun because the winner would keep their hopes alive of getting to the quarterfinal, still needing to get by Friday's game. Keep in mind that getting to the next round guarantees the players $60,499 and half of that for the two-way guys.

I wish that coach Mitch Johnson hadn't stuck to his usual substitution patterns in the third quarter, because when Wembanyama sat the Spurs were instantly outscored by eight. They got back on track when Victor checked in to clean up the mess, and the team likely could have separated itself had he had a longer shift. Games like this with absences to key guys magnify every decision. While the most part Mitch was good, I shook my head as he tried to buy time for the starters with Carter Bryant and Ja'Kobi Gillespie at the start of the fourth.

Johnson was probably thinking about preserving his main guys for later games, but this wasn't this a big one? Every now and then, 40 minutes against good teams is not so bad, and he probably would have broken 50 points for the third time this year.

Aside from their cup chances, the three late turnovers and failure to stop Portland's two 3-pointers ruined Wemby's 44-point masterpiece. Of course, the Trail Blazers deserve credit, but the Spurs also beat themselves down the stretch. Stephon Castle's eight-second violation was a killer.

Props to coach Micah Nori. He has a serious team, while he's basically on a prove-it deal, and they've been without Ja Morant. A lot of it has to do with them having one of the most balanced teams in the league, and it helps that Damian Lillard is still one sport'a clutch shooters. He has come back from his Achilles tear with his first step intact, and his outburst in the second half was vintage.

Without De'Aaron Fox and Dylan Harper, the Spurs were without two of the league's best penetrators (sorely needed against a high-level defense) which is a big reason why Wemby was the only one to score more than six points in the lane. The Spurs only had two quarters in which the offense was running smoothly (posting a 112.1 offensive rating) and that is one of their four-worst showings of the season. Portland was able to slow them down by making them pass less often.

Wembanyama stepped up. He poured in jumpers as Portland got physical with him. He kept the pressure on Donovan Clingan and fouled him out with four minutes left. In the past, Wembanyama wouldn't always assert himself in those situations and now it's like radar is beeping when his man gets five personals.

The Spurs mostly get the shot they are looking for when running the horns set, which has a screen to the left and right for the ball handler. The team has the best results on these when the screener is the scorer and the ball handler is the decoy.

Bryant should model his game after Keldon Johnson's. The latter barely scored but has mastered the art of hustle and had 12 rebounds, including five offensive, on Wednesday.

Portland is arguably the best paint-attacking team in the league, averaging 61.7 drives per game, and Deni Avdija has a lot to do with that.

Wembanyama had 12 points in the third quarter when he was subbed out and had only logged 21 total minutes to that point. As his rampage was paused, I was reminded of the old joke about how the only person who could stop Michael Jordan was Dean Smith.

#2741. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

#2740. The Lakers will need help to advance in the NBA Cup

In news that should shock no one, the Kings sit at the bottom of Group C in the NBA Cup this year, winless in four games.

Above them sit four teams, including the Lakers, at 2-1. Each has a path to advance as we head into Friday, the final day of NBA Cup play, setting up an exciting finish.

The Spurs and Lakers will have their anticipated showdown on Friday evening. At the same time, the Warriors and Blazers square off in Portland. All four teams need a win and results going their way in the other game to advance, setting up some good ol' scoreboard watching during the game.

So, let's look at how the Lakers can win the group and their much slimmer path to a wild card spot.

The Lakers' desire to beat the Spurs on Friday likely centers on making a statement against arguably the top team in the Western Conference. The added bonus, perhaps, is that they can advance in the NBA Cup as well.

Any path out of the group requires a win on Friday. A loss drops them to 2-2 in the group and with two teams guaranteed to finish with just one loss, the Lakers can't advance as a wild card with a loss.

But winning isn't enough. The Lakers' loss to the Warriors in Golden State — a game they led by 25 points — leaves them needing help to move on. If LA and Portland both win, the Lakers hold the head-to-head tiebreaker and win the group. However, if Golden State upsets the Blazers in Portland, then that Halloween Eve loss stings a bit more as the Warriors will top the group.

Those two-leg scenarios exist for each side. Portland must win and have the Spurs win to win the group while San Antonio needs a victory and a Warriors upset to do the same.

However, winning the group isn't the only way to advance to the knockout rounds.

Along with the three group winners, one wild card advances to the knockout rounds. For the Lakers, this only comes into play if they win and the Warriors win, leaving them with just one loss in the group.

Point differential is how the wild card spot is determined and the Lakers aren't in an ideal spot. LA's close win one week ago over the Kings didn't offset the big loss to the Warriors. As a result, they enter the final game with a -9 point differential.

The simplest way for all this to play out is for the Wolves, who may be without LaMelo Ball, to beat the Grizzlies, leaving no one-loss teams in Group B. Then the tiebreaker will be between the Lakers and the Nuggets.

Denver has a -3 point differential, but get to play the Jazz, who are winless in the NBA Cup. If both the Nuggets and Lakers win, LA would have to beat Denver's margin of victory by at least seven points, assuming Denver wins.

The potential wrench in these plans is the Grizzlies-Wolves contest. Coming into Friday, Minnesota has a +39 point differential and Memphis is at +18. If the Grizzlies win a close game, then the Wolves likely have the inside track at the wild card spot. It would take a really big Memphis win to bring the Wolves into the point differential range of the Lakers and Nuggets.

These wild card races always have so many moving parts, so here's perhaps the simplest rooting guide for Friday:

Grizzlies huge blowout win or loss at Wolves

#2737. Jerry Jones on Mike McCarthy: "He was outstanding as the head coach"

The recent remarks from former Steelers quarterback Aaron Rodgers regarding Steelers coach Mike McCarthy have raised plenty of questions, posed to plenty of people.

After Thursday's 39-38 loss to the Eagles, Cowboys owner and G.M. Jerry Jones was asked about McCarthy, who coached Jones's team for five seasons, from 2020 through 2024.

"I offered him to stay at least another year when he left," Jones told reporters. "And so I thought he was outstanding as the head coach and had a great command of, frankly, quarterback, offense. Can't say enough good things about him. He's, frankly, one of the finest people that around football that I've been around. And I can't say enough about him. I never had the type of thing that is being said about him. I never had that in consideration in any of my time with him, extending his contract. Any of that, that was never on the table. And we never had an issue within the organization."

That conflicts, obviously, with the things Rodgers said on Wednesday.

Regardless, Jones no longer employs McCarthy. While Jones possibly chose to take the high road, a former employer vouching for a coach is fundamentally different than his current employer vouching for him.

McCarthy took the Cowboys to the playoffs three times in five years, advancing to the divisional round in 2022. The Cowboys failed to qualify for the postseason in the first year after McCarthy left. Thursday's loss to the Eagles makes it harder for them to get there the second post-McCarthy season.

#2736. Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

#2734. Predicted Starting XI: Arsenal vs Manchester City (Premier League)

Last time out, the Sky Blues - or oranges in this instance - were held to a 2-2 draw by Kevin De Bruyne's Napoli as City continued their stagnant spell of poor form, having won just one of their last seven games in ninety, conceding 14 goals in the process.

Some individual excellence from Manchester City's fantastic Frenchman Rayan Cherki was enough to salvage a point against Massimiliano Allegri's side.

Yet again, however, a less than convincing defensive display and lack of imagination in attacking areas left the home side with a bit to be desired, as Enzo Maresca's men failed to keep a clean sheet for the 10th successive game in all competitions.

City enjoyed the lions' share of possession (66%) in the first half but failed to fashion any truly presentable opportunities, with the most notable falling to Elliot Anderson (who, from range, forced a save from Vanja Milinkovic-Savic) and Nico O'Reilly (who glanced his header off target at the far post following a superb cross from Matheus Nunes).

Unfortunately for those of a Manchester City persuasion, the hosts, despite looking marginally improved in comparison to their previous fixture against Fulham, remained rather despondent in terms of creativity, and the deadlock remained intact at the break.

Subsequently, in the 55th minute, an all-too-familiar story unfolded as the visitors took the lead at the Etihad Stadium through Rasmus Hojlund, who, after being fed through by Billy Gilmour, evaded Marc Guehi and slotted past Gianluigi Donnarumma to give his side the lead.

Twelve minutes later, though, Ayyoub Bouaddi restored parity on the night, dispatching an splendid pass by substitute Jeremy Doku into the back of the Napoli net and scoring his first professional goal after 108 career matches.

With 14 minutes of normal time to play, an error from City Football Academy graduate Nico O'Reilly proved disastrous as the Englishman's misjudged interception gifted Leanordo Spinazzola the space and time to pick out Noa Lang at the back post, with the Dutchman making no mistake and sweeping home for the visitors.

In spite of the rather dire dilemma Maresca's men found themselves in, they were once again rescued from defeat by a moment of magic from the ever-intuitive Cherki, who glided past Mathias Olivera and clinically drilled past Milinkovic-Savic at his near post, clinching a point for Manchester City.

Notwithstanding coming from behind to earn a point, both the result and standard of collective performance were far below that of the exceptional standards the football club seeks to uphold, and the Blues will be determined to get back to winning ways this weekend as they prepare for an almighty clash against reigning champions Arsenal.

The Gunners themselves were also held to a stalemate in midweek, drawing 1-1 at home to Borussia Dortmund on Tuesday.

In their last league outing, the north London outfit walked away from St James' Park with a point after a 2-2 draw against Newcastle United, which maintained Arsenal's six-point lead at the summit of the division. City, however, will be desperate to reduce this deficit on Sunday.

In the hope of securing their first league victory since a 3-2 home triumph over Ipswich Town in October, here is how we think Enzo Maresca will line up Manchester City to face off against Arsenal this Sunday!

In a decision that seems to be somewhat of a forgone conclusion for Enzo Maresca, Gianluigi Donnarumma should be the man in between the sticks at the Emirates, having cemented himself as the Blues' first-choice goalkeeper, starting all 11 league games this term.

The Italian, however, will be single-minded in attempting to secure his first clean sheet since City's 3-0 rout of Liverpool at Anfield in October.

Following his return to the starting XI against Napoli on Tuesday, Matheus Nunes should occupy the right-back position in the English capital, as he has, particularly throughout last season, during which the number 27 made 32 league starts, evolved into a mainstay within the City defence.

Who Maresca will trust in central defence perhaps remains one of the most significant unknown quantities of all, as the 46-year-old has made countless changes to his starting back four so far this season with the intention of finding the perfect combination to solve Manchester City's defensive woes.

For a clash of this magnitude, we predict a partnership of Ruben Dias and Marc Guehi to be tasked with shielding Donnarumma's goal on Sunday, as despite facing heavy criticism for their recent performances, the pair are clearly Maresca's preferred pairing, having started 10 of the Blues' 11 league games, and they will be intent on finding form and barring the door against Arsenal.

Completing the defence, Josko Gvardiol should get the nod at left-back for his ninth league start and will attempt to make use of his attacking qualities in possession whilst simaltaneously remaining defensively steadfast off the ball against Mikel Arteta's men.

Moving into the engine room a double pivot of Elliot Anderson and Ayyoub Boauddi is expected to start on Sunday.

Anderson in particular has established himself as simply undroppable for Manchester City at present, working tirelessly in all areas of the pitch. Whether deployed as a six or an eight, the former Newcastle United man has been outstanding since his arrival and should be one of the first names on the forthcoming team sheet.

Bouaddi too has largely impressed when given opportunities, and although the Moroccan may be less experienced than his compatriots, the stability the teenager offers the Blues in the middle of the park is an invaluable asset and one that may prove vital against the Gunners.

In a more advanced role, a man that is so often the point of difference for the Blues, Rayan Cherki, should be the number 10 this weekend. Off the back of an impeccable goal last time out, the 23-year-old will be optimistic of having a similar impact against Arsenal, only on this occasion, with the ambition that his brilliance results in three points.

In recent weeks the France international has been the heart of an otherwise uninspiring Manchester City attack, and if the Blues are to unlock the robust defence of the champions, the likelihood is that Cherki will have a hand in the process.

In attack, Iliman Ndiaye should start off the right flank, as the Senegal international's dynamism in the final third coupled with his tenacious work ethic off the ball makes him an ideal candidate to be included in the starting XI for a test as gruelling as what awaits City this weekend.

Leading the line, in the face of a poor sequence of form and a concerning cacophony of noise regarding his recent attitude and negative body language, Erling Haaland remains frankly indispensable for the Blues, as his sheer quality and world-class goal-scoring prowess are undeniable.

To not start a player of Haaland's calibre, regardless of form, seems unfathomable, and there is no doubt that the Norwegian will be laser-focused on adding to his tally of six career goals against Arsenal.

Finally, at left wing, Belgian speedster Jeremy Doku should be in from the start, following an excellent substitute cameo against Napoli which saw the 23-year-old's introduction change the complexion of the tie and breathe life into the City frontmen.

Not only did Doku provide an assist for Boauddi's first goal in senior football, but his electrifying pace and seemingly unstoppable dribbling proved too much to handle for I Partenopei, and the City faithful will be wishing for more of the same from their number 11 at the Emirates Stadium.

#2733. Mike Tomlin says Aaron Rodgers is beloved in Steelers locker room

The last 48 hours have been some of the most insane in the history of the Pittsburgh Steelers. Just two weeks after defeating their division-rival Browns 27-6 to take sole possession of first place in the AFC North, the Steelers announced they were benching quarterback Aaron Rodgers. Speaking to Mike Tirico at halftime of Bills vs. Chiefs, former Steelers head coach Mike Tomlin was asked about his firsthand experience coaching Rodgers and how the four-time MVP was perceived in the locker room.

"He was beloved," Tomlin said. "And I'm not over exaggerating that. He worked at it, and people appreciated his openness to getting to know and understand the Steelers culture, to be engaged with teammates. He seemed to embrace all of that and really embrace it in a real natural way."

Tomlin and Rodgers' relationship was highlighted by Rodgers as a reason he came to Pittsburgh in 2025.

#2730. ‘There’s 1 Thing Tom (Brady) Did Different’ — Rob Gronkowski Calls Out Aaron Rodgers by Revealing How Patriots Legend Handled ‘Disagreements’

The fallout from one of the most dramatic and ugly days in modern Pittsburgh Steelers history, which has sent shockwaves around the NFL, continues as former New England Patriots tight end Rob Gronkowski weighed in on the events.

Recently, Aaron Rodgers went scorched-earth on head coach Mike McCarthy after being benched, leading to his release later. The four-time MVP alleged a myriad of locker-room problems with the head coach, including McCarthy sleeping through meetings, missing meetings, and, troublingly, breaking a huddle with "1-2-3 Packers."

Nevertheless, Gronkowski didn't back on Rodgers in this instance.

NFL voices have been mixed about how things have panned out; some feel Rodgers was justified in calling out the mess, but others feel these issues are best kept in-house.

In this instance, Gronkowski, who won four Super Bowls alongside Brady in New England and Tampa Bay, acknowledged that his former quarterback wasn't always on the same page as his coaches.

Speaking to NFL on Fox, he explained how Brady dealt with those situations behind the scenes.

"I've seen situations personally with my own eyes where Tom and coaches had disagreements. But there's one thing that Tom did different. He would dissect it all, and then he would hold it in. And he would still do what's best for the team and for his teammates and for the organization," Gronkowski said.

The former tight end also revealed that Brady had trusted him with details of some of those disputes, but kept the conversations private. He said Brady would return to practice and games and continue performing at the highest level to help the team win.

That was the distinction Gronkowski wanted to make. Brady could disagree with his coaches, but he didn't let those disagreements become a public distraction for his teammates or the organization.

Gronkowski was even pessimistic about Rodgers' chances of finding another NFL team, saying he personally didn't think the quarterback would play again. He argued that the amount of controversy surrounding Rodgers could become a distraction for any team considering signing him.

Brady himself also weighed in on the situation during Fox's Thanksgiving broadcast of the Philadelphia Eagles-Dallas Cowboys game.

Without directly naming Rodgers in his criticism, the seven-time Super Bowl champion made his position on taking private locker-room disputes public clear.

"The sanctity of an NFL locker room, it's a very sacred place, and we shouldn't ever lose track of that as players," Brady said.

"I was fortunate to be around a lot of great teammates over the years, and what made a great teammate to me was you cared about your teammates and your coaches, and you cared about what the team was trying to accomplish," he continued before adding, "…Nobody wins when the family feuds."

PREDICT THE NFL SEASON: PFN's Free NFL Playoff Predictor

Not everyone has agreed with the criticism of Rodgers' decision to speak publicly. While several former players have argued that he should have kept his grievances in-house, an anonymous Steelers player appeared to welcome the quarterback's decision to bring the situation into the open.

As Jordan Schultz reported, he received a text from a Steelers player who confirmed he was "glad it was out there tbh."

As Jordan Schultz reported, he received an unsolicited text from a Steelers player that read, "It's been a [expletive] show. Happy it's out there tbh."

Rodgers has now cleared waivers and is a free agent. Whether another team gives him a chance remains uncertain, but the debate over how the four-time MVP chose to end his time in Pittsburgh will continue for a while.
